🔬 Ontario Grade 1 Science & Technology

Full-Year Homeschool Curriculum · 2022 DCP · 60+ Lessons

Strand A · STEM Skills
Week 1–2 · Opening Unit

🔬 What Is Science? The Art of Wondering

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — A1.1 – Develop and use the skills, habits of mind, and strategies of scientific investigation
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Imagine you walk outside right now and see a puddle of water on the sidewalk. Now look again five minutes later — the puddle is smaller. An hour later — it's completely gone! Where did all that water go? Nobody poured it away. The sun didn't drink it. The ground didn't swallow it. Where. Did. It. Go?

This is what scientists call a mystery — something that happens right in front of us but we don't immediately know why. And here is the most exciting secret in the world:

Science is nothing more than the art of asking "Why?" and then going on a magnificent adventure to find out.

Every single discovery in all of human history started exactly the way you just did — with a person who noticed something surprising and couldn't stop thinking about it. In this very first lesson, you are going to discover what it truly means to be a scientist.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand that science is a way of asking questions and investigating the world around us
  • Identify the difference between an observation (something you notice) and an inference (a guess about what it means)
  • Practice the habit of scientific wondering — noticing surprising things and asking questions about them
  • Learn that scientists make mistakes, and that mistakes are one of the most important parts of science
  • Understand that science is for everyone — not just people in lab coats
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Before You Begin: Setting the Stage (5–10 min)

This first lesson is about sparking a flame that should never go out — the flame of curiosity. Your goal today is not to teach facts. Your goal is to show your child that the world around them is endlessly mysterious and magnificent.

What you need: A glass of water, a piece of paper, a rock, a leaf (or any three objects from outside), a magnifying glass if you have one, a pencil

Opening Activity: The Mystery Box Game (10 min)

Put three objects in a bag without your child seeing them. Let them feel one object at a time without looking. Ask: 'What do you think this is? How do you know? What clues did your fingers give you?'

After they guess, reveal the objects. Explain: 'The clues your fingers felt — that's called an OBSERVATION. And the guess you made from those clues — that's called an INFERENCE. Scientists do this exact thing every single day, just with fancier tools!'

Key Concept to Teach (10 min)

Read the Big Lesson aloud together. Stop at every question mark and actually pause — let your child think and answer. When they give an answer, respond with 'That's interesting! What makes you think that?' rather than immediately telling them if they're right.

The Puddle Experiment (20 min)

Pour a small amount of water on a sidewalk or driveway. Draw a chalk outline around it. Check it every 15 minutes. Record what you see. This simple experiment introduces evaporation AND the scientific method at the same time.

Teaching tip: If your child is frustrated when they don't know an answer, say: "Scientists don't always know either — that's exactly why they do experiments!"
📖

The Big Lesson

The Most Important Question in the World

What is science? You might think science means memorizing the names of planets, or knowing what dinosaurs looked like, or understanding why the sky is blue. And those things are part of science. But they're not what science is.

Science is a way of thinking. It's a way of looking at the world and saying: "Hm. That's strange. I wonder why that happens." And then — and this is the most important part — actually going to find out.

You've Been a Scientist Your Whole Life

Think about the very first time you saw a caterpillar turn into a butterfly. Or the first time you dropped something and it fell to the floor — every single time, without exception. Or the first time you noticed that ice melts. You probably didn't say, "I am now conducting a scientific experiment." But you were.

Every time you notice something surprising and wonder about it, your brain is doing science. The difference between you now and a professional scientist is mostly this: scientists have learned very careful ways to find answers that are reliable — meaning they work for everyone, not just sometimes.

The Great Puddle Mystery — Solved!

Remember that disappearing puddle? Here's what actually happens. Water isn't just a liquid — it can turn into an invisible gas called water vapour. When the sun warms a puddle, the tiny water molecules at the surface get excited and jump up into the air. This process is called evaporation. The water doesn't disappear — it transforms. It becomes part of the invisible air all around you right now.

But here's the truly mind-blowing part: that same water will come back. The water vapour rises, cools down, and eventually becomes clouds. And then it falls as rain. That puddle on your sidewalk might become rain in another city, then a river, then a cloud, then rain again — and it has been doing this cycle for billions of years.

Amazing fact: The water in your glass right now might have been inside a dinosaur 70 million years ago. Water on Earth is constantly recycled — it is never truly created or destroyed.

What Scientists Actually Do

A scientist doesn't just read about the world in books. Scientists go out and test ideas. They ask a question, make a prediction (called a hypothesis), do an experiment, observe what happens, and then think about what their results mean.

The most important thing about being a scientist is this: you have to be willing to be wrong. Some of the greatest discoveries in history happened because a scientist was wrong about something and figured out why. Being wrong isn't failing — being wrong and learning from it is how science works.

Science Is For You

Here is something important to understand: science is not just for people who are very smart, or who go to special schools, or who work in laboratories. Science is for everyone who has ever been curious about something. That means it is absolutely for you.

This year, you are going to think like a scientist every single week. You are going to observe, question, predict, experiment, and discover. And some of the things you discover might surprise even the grown-ups around you.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Scientists call the first step of science observation — looking very carefully at something. Right now, go outside (or look out a window) and observe for exactly 2 minutes without saying a word. Then come back and answer these questions:

  • What is one thing you noticed that surprised you?
  • What is one question you now have about what you saw?
  • If you had a superpower and could shrink down to the size of an ant, what do you think you would discover that normal-sized people can't see?

Auto-saved

Deep think: Scientists argue about ideas. Is arguing good or bad in science? Why might two scientists disagree about the same experiment?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: My Science Journal

Create your very own Science Journal! Decorate the cover with your name and drawings of things you're curious about. On the first page, write: "Things I Wonder About:" and list or draw at least 5 things you genuinely wonder about in nature.

Auto-saved

Day 2: The Observation Station

Choose one natural object (a rock, leaf, stick, flower, or shell). Study it for 5 full minutes using all your senses (except taste!). Record everything you observe:

  • How does it look? Colour, shape, texture, patterns?
  • How does it feel? Smooth, rough, heavy, light?
  • Does it have a smell? Describe it.
  • Can you hear anything if you tap it gently?
  • What questions does it give you?

Auto-saved

Day 3: Draw Your Own Scientist

Draw a picture of yourself as a scientist. What tools do you have? Where are you working? What are you investigating? Write one sentence explaining what discovery you are making.

Auto-saved

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

Week-Long Project: My Wonder Wall

Over the next 5 days, you will build a Wonder Wall — a collection of questions you genuinely want answered about the natural world.

  1. Each day, go outside (or look out the window) for 10 minutes and observe.
  2. Write down or draw at least 2 things you notice that make you wonder.
  3. Turn each observation into a question (What? Why? How? When? Where?)
  4. On Day 5, arrange your best questions on a big piece of paper and decorate it.
  5. Choose your FAVOURITE wonder question and explain: Why does this question matter to you?
Parent note: Help your child phrase observations as questions. "The bird sits on that branch every morning" → "Why does the bird always choose that same branch?"

Auto-saved

🏠

Homework Activities

Homework Option 1: Wonder Walk

Go for a 20-minute walk with a family member. Each of you choose one thing that makes you wonder. When you get home, tell each other your wonder. Then try to find the answer together!

Homework Option 2: Scientist Interview

Ask a parent, grandparent, or older family member about something they've always wondered about. Write down their wonder and your own wonder. Do they match?

Auto-saved

Homework Option 3: Puddle Investigation

If it rains or you can pour water on the ground, draw around a puddle with chalk, mark the time, and check it every 30 minutes. Draw what you see each time. Where does the water go? Write your conclusion.

🌟

Keep Wondering…

Here are some questions to keep wondering about all year:

  • If you could invent any scientific instrument to study something, what would you want to measure and why?
  • Why do you think some people are afraid of science? How could you help them see how exciting it is?
  • Can animals be scientists? Do you think a crow using a stick to get food is doing science? Why or why not?
  • What is something that scientists still do NOT know the answer to?
Research challenge: Ask a grown-up to help you look up "unanswered questions in science." Pick one and tell someone about it.
Week 2–3

❓ Asking Big Questions — The Power of "Why"

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — A1.1 – Formulating questions; A1.2 – Making predictions
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Here is a challenge: for exactly one day, count how many times you ask the word 'Why?'. You might be surprised — young children ask about 300 questions every single day! Scientists think this is one of the most important things a human brain can do.

But not all questions are equal. Some questions are easy to answer: "What colour is the sky?" (Blue.) But some questions are so deep and so important that humans have been trying to answer them for thousands of years.

The deepest question: Why is there something rather than nothing? Why does the universe exist at all? Scientists and philosophers have worked on this question for millennia and still don't have a complete answer.

In science, asking the right kind of question is actually a skill — and today you're going to practice it.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Distinguish between questions that can be investigated scientifically and those that cannot
  • Practice turning observations into testable questions
  • Understand what a hypothesis is and how to write one
  • Learn the structure of a good scientific question
  • Recognize that good questions lead to better investigations
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

The Two Types of Questions (15 min)

Start with a sorting game. Write these questions on paper strips and ask your child to sort them into two piles:

  • Do plants grow faster in sunlight or shade?
  • Is the sky beautiful?
  • How long does ice take to melt?
  • Is broccoli yucky?
  • Do worms prefer wet or dry soil?
  • What is the best colour?

Help them see: some questions have definite, measurable answers (scientific questions). Others depend on personal opinion (not testable by science).

Introducing Hypotheses (10 min)

Explain: A hypothesis is a careful guess that predicts what will happen and includes your reason. Model the format: 'I think [X] will happen because [reason].'

Practice together: If you put a plant in a dark closet for a week, what do you think will happen? Have your child write a hypothesis before you do the experiment.

Materials for classwork: 2 identical plants (or 2 bean seeds in cups), one sunny spot, one dark spot, water
📖

The Big Lesson

Not All Questions Are Created Equal

In science, a question needs to have certain special qualities to be a scientific question. Let's find out what those qualities are.

First, a scientific question must be one that you can actually test. You can't do an experiment to find out if pizza is delicious — because different people have different opinions. But you CAN do an experiment to find out how much sugar is in a glass of orange juice versus apple juice. One is a matter of opinion; the other has a real, measurable answer.

The Magic Word: Testable

The most important word for a scientific question is testable. A testable question has a clear, definite answer that anyone can find by observing or measuring — not just by using their feelings or opinions.

Here's the pattern: Good scientific questions often start with: "Does _____ affect _____?" or "What happens when ___?" or "How much/many/long ___?"

What Is a Hypothesis?

Once you have a great testable question, scientists make a hypothesis (say it: HY-poth-eh-sis). A hypothesis is a careful prediction — an educated guess about what the answer might be.

A hypothesis isn't just any random guess. A good hypothesis includes your reasoning. It says why you think something will happen. Here's the difference:

  • Bad hypothesis: 'I think the plant in sunlight will grow taller.'
  • Good hypothesis: 'I think the plant in sunlight will grow taller because plants use sunlight to make their food, so more light means more food and more growth.'

See the difference? The good hypothesis shows that you already know something about how plants work, and you're using that knowledge to make a prediction. That's scientific thinking!

The Plant in the Dark Experiment

Here is a beautiful experiment you can do at home. Take two identical plants — or two cups with the same amount of soil and the same kind of seed. Put one near a sunny window and one in a dark closet (or cover it with a cardboard box). Give them the same amount of water. Observe what happens over the next two weeks.

Before you start, write your hypothesis: What do you think will happen to each plant? Why?

Then observe every single day. Draw pictures. Measure the height. Notice the colour of the leaves. Are you surprised by anything? When the results come in, do they match your hypothesis? If not — fantastic! Something unexpected is always the most exciting result in science.

Why Being Wrong Is Scientific

In 1928, a scientist named Alexander Fleming was growing bacteria in his laboratory and got very frustrated — his bacteria kept dying. His experiment was ruined. He threw it away.

But a colleague asked him to look more carefully first. Fleming noticed that a mould was growing in the dish, and the bacteria around the mould were all dead. He wondered: Is this mould killing the bacteria?

He had found penicillin — the first antibiotic. A medicine that has saved hundreds of millions of human lives. It all started because he asked a question about something going wrong.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Here are some interesting observations. Your job is to turn each one into a testable scientific question and then write a hypothesis for it:

  • You notice birds come to your bird feeder more in the morning than in the afternoon.
  • Ice in a glass melts faster on a hot day.
  • Flowers seem to open more on sunny days.
  • Your dog gets excited when you put on your shoes.

Auto-saved

Challenge question: Can you think of a scientific question about something in YOUR house or yard that nobody has ever investigated before?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Question Maker

Go through your house and look out the window. Find 5 things that make you wonder. For each one, write a testable scientific question. Then rank them from most to least interesting.

Auto-saved

Day 2: Hypothesis Writing Station

Take your TOP question from yesterday and write a proper hypothesis using the format: 'I predict that [X] will happen because [reason].' Then draw a picture showing what you imagine the experiment will look like.

Auto-saved

Day 3: The Plant Experiment Begins

Set up your plant-in-the-dark experiment. Write today's date. Measure both plants (or plant both seeds). Draw and describe them. Check in daily for the next week.

Auto-saved

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

Week Project: Question Journal

For 5 days, go outside or look out a window and observe for 10 minutes. Each day, write 3 testable questions you come up with. At the end of the week:

  1. Count how many questions you collected total.
  2. Circle your very best question.
  3. Write a full hypothesis for your best question.
  4. Design a simple experiment to test it (you don't have to actually do it, but describe what you would do).

Auto-saved

🏠

Homework Activities

Homework: The Family Question Dinner

At dinner tonight, everyone at the table must ask one 'Why?' question about nature. Write all the questions down. Then, as a family, try to figure out which ones are testable and which are opinion questions. Try to find the answer to one question together.

Auto-saved

No internet needed — try to figure it out by thinking, observing, or doing a simple test together!
🌟

Keep Wondering…

Keep wondering about the art of questioning:

  • What question would you ask if you could talk to any animal?
  • Scientists say that children ask better questions than most adults. Why do you think that might be true?
  • If you could travel forward in time 100 years, what scientific question do you think will finally be answered by then?
Week 3–4

👁️ Observing Like a Professional Scientist

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — A1.1 – Using senses and tools to observe; A1.3 – Making observations and recording data
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Close your eyes. Now listen for 30 seconds without moving. What do you hear?

Open your eyes. You probably heard at least 5 sounds you hadn't noticed before. This is one of the most important things to understand about science: you can look right at something and still not see it. Our brains are so busy processing information that they filter out most of what our senses pick up.

A shocking experiment: In a famous science video, people were asked to count how many times basketball players passed a ball. Most people were so focused on counting that they completely missed a person in a gorilla suit walking right through the middle of the players and waving. 50% of watchers never saw it at all!

Scientists call this inattentional blindness — the scientifically proven fact that we miss things that are right in front of us when we're not paying attention the right way. Today, you'll learn to pay attention like a scientist.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Use all five senses safely to make detailed observations
  • Distinguish between observation (what you notice directly) and inference (what you interpret)
  • Practice describing observations precisely, using numbers and measurements when possible
  • Learn to use simple scientific tools (magnifying glass, ruler, measuring cups) to extend observations
  • Understand why good observations are the foundation of all science
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

The 5 Senses Warm-Up (10 min)

Materials: Magnifying glass, ruler, measuring cup, a selection of objects from nature (rocks, leaves, bark, seeds, flowers), blindfold, containers with different textures

Before the lesson, prepare a 'mystery bag' with 5 objects of different textures. Blindfold your child and have them feel each one carefully. Ask: What shape is it? Smooth or rough? Heavy or light? Hard or soft? Wet or dry? After each one, reveal it and compare their description to the actual object.

Teaching Observation vs. Inference (15 min)

This distinction is crucial and will be used all year. Hold up a lemon. Say:

  • 'The lemon is yellow' — is this an observation or inference? (Observation — we can see it directly)
  • 'This lemon tastes sour' — observation or inference? (Observation — we can taste it directly. Though it requires tasting!)
  • 'This lemon has been in the fridge' — observation or inference? (Inference — it's cold, but we're guessing why)

Help your child see: observations are direct sensory experiences. Inferences are conclusions we draw from observations.

The Magnifying Glass Activity (20 min)

Hand your child a magnifying glass and take them outside. Challenge them to find the most interesting tiny thing they can — a crack in concrete, a section of bark, a piece of soil, a flower petal. Have them describe what they see at 10x magnification that they couldn't see before. Record in their journal.

📖

The Big Lesson

Your Senses Are Amazing Scientific Instruments

You were born with five extraordinary scientific instruments built right into your body: your eyes, ears, nose, tongue, and skin. These instruments have been collecting data your entire life. Every single sensation you've ever had — every colour you've seen, every sound you've heard, every texture you've felt — was your body doing science.

But here's the thing about these built-in instruments: they can make mistakes. They can be fooled. And they can miss things. That's why scientists have developed both special skills for using their senses better, AND special tools to extend what their senses can detect.

The Art of Precise Description

Here's a test. Which description is more useful to a scientist?

  • 'The rock is medium-sized, kind of heavy, grayish-brown, and a bit rough.'
  • 'The rock is 4 cm long, 3 cm wide, weighs about 85 grams, is dark gray with brown speckles, has a rough surface on one side and a smooth cleavage surface on the other, and rings like a bell when struck against another rock.'

The second one, of course! Great observations are precise. They use numbers, measurements, and specific words. They describe exactly what you observe — not vague words like 'big', 'some', or 'a bit'.

Observation vs. Inference: The Critical Difference

One of the most important thinking skills in science is knowing the difference between an observation and an inference.

An observation is something you directly detect with your senses right now. An inference is a conclusion you draw from an observation — a story you tell about what the observation means.

Example: You walk into the kitchen and see wet puddles on the counter and a glass tipped on its side. Observation: wet counter, tipped glass. Inference: someone knocked over a glass of water. These are NOT the same thing — the water could have come from somewhere else entirely!

Scientific Tools: Extending Your Senses

Throughout history, scientists have invented tools that let us observe things too small, too far, too hot, or too fast for our unaided senses:

  • Magnifying glass: makes tiny things look bigger (your leaf veins become highways!)
  • Ruler: gives precise measurements so descriptions can be compared
  • Thermometer: measures temperature more precisely than 'warm' or 'cold'
  • Balance/scale: tells you exactly how much something weighs
  • Microscope: makes things invisible to the naked eye suddenly visible (a single drop of pond water contains thousands of living creatures)
  • Telescope: brings distant objects close (we can see craters on the moon!)

Each of these tools is really just an extension of your senses. They let you observe things your body couldn't observe alone.

Why Careful Observation Matters

In 1962, a biologist named Rachel Carson noticed that there were fewer birds singing in the spring than there used to be. She observed this carefully, recorded it, and investigated it. She discovered that a chemical called DDT, used to kill insects, was building up in the food chain and killing birds.

Her careful observations led to a book called Silent Spring, which changed how the whole world thinks about chemicals and nature. One woman's careful observations helped protect birds, bees, and countless other animals. That's the power of scientific observation.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Look at the same leaf (or any natural object) for 5 full minutes. At first you might think you've seen everything there is to see. Keep going. Write down your observations in order — the ones from minute 1, then minute 3, then minute 5. What did you notice only after a long time looking?

Auto-saved

Deep think: Scientists from 200 years ago had no microscopes or telescopes. What do you think they got wrong about science because of this? What might scientists today be getting wrong because they don't have the right tools yet?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Super Observation Notebook

Choose a 'special spot' in your yard, on your balcony, or near your window. Spend 15 minutes observing it VERY carefully. Record: 10 things you can see, 5 things you can hear, 3 things you can smell, 2 things you can feel (if safe). Be as precise as possible — use numbers, sizes, colours.

Auto-saved

Day 2: Magnifying Glass Safari

Take your magnifying glass outside. Your mission: find something that looks COMPLETELY DIFFERENT under magnification than it does with your naked eye. Describe and draw what you see normally AND what you see magnified.

Auto-saved

Day 3: The Observation vs Inference Sort

Go outside and make 10 observations. Then, for each observation, write one inference (what you think the observation means). Circle the observations in blue and the inferences in red. Are they always easy to separate?

Auto-saved

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

Week Project: Nature Detective Case File

You are a nature detective! Your assignment is to observe ONE specific place (a corner of the yard, a patch of garden, a window ledge with a bird feeder) every day for 5 days, at the same time each day.

  1. Each day: write the date, time, weather, and then make at least 10 precise observations.
  2. Use at least one measurement tool each day (ruler, thermometer, or just count things).
  3. Draw what you see each day.
  4. On Day 5, compare all your observations. What changed? What stayed the same?
  5. Write your conclusion: What did 5 days of careful observation teach you about this place?

Auto-saved

🏠

Homework Activities

Homework: Food Observation

At your next meal, slow down and observe your food like a scientist BEFORE you eat it. Notice colours, shapes, textures, smells. Write your observations. Make one inference about each food (where did it come from? How was it made?). Then — eat and find out if your inferences were right!

Auto-saved

🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • The naked human eye can see objects as small as 0.1mm. A microscope can show things 0.001mm — 100 times smaller. What do you think we might discover if we could see things 1000 times smaller?
  • Why do you think blind scientists can still do excellent science?
  • Dogs can smell 10,000 to 100,000 times better than humans. What do you think the world 'smells like' to a dog?
Week 4–5

🎯 Making Predictions — Thinking Before Doing

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — A1.2 – Making predictions; A1.3 – Designing fair tests
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Imagine you are about to drop a feather and a rock from the same height at the same time. Which will hit the ground first?

Most people say the rock. And in regular life, they're right — the rock hits first. But here's the mind-blowing science: in a vacuum (a place with no air), both objects fall at EXACTLY the same speed. Galileo proved this in the 1600s by dropping objects from the Leaning Tower of Pisa. NASA proved it in 1971 by dropping a feather and a hammer on the Moon — they hit at the same time, live on television.

The lesson: What seems obvious is not always true. This is why scientists make predictions AND test them — because the universe is full of surprises.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand what a prediction is and how it differs from a wild guess
  • Learn to write predictions using 'If... then... because...' format
  • Understand what makes a test 'fair' (controlling variables)
  • Practice making predictions and then testing them
  • Learn that surprising results are the best results in science
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Variables and Fair Tests (Core Teaching — 20 min)

Materials: Paper, scissors, paper clips, ruler — for paper airplane testing

The concept of a 'fair test' is fundamental to science and can be taught very concretely. Make two paper airplanes — one normal, one very crumpled. Drop them from the same height. Ask: Is this a fair test? Why not? (They're different!)

Help your child understand: to test whether ONE thing affects the result, everything ELSE must stay the same. This is called 'controlling variables'. Introduce the vocabulary: independent variable (what you change), dependent variable (what you measure), controlled variables (what you keep the same).

The Dropping Race (15 min)

Set up a series of dropping experiments: same objects from different heights, different objects from the same height, same object with different amounts of weight added. Have your child predict before each one and record results.

📖

The Big Lesson

The Power of the Prediction

In science, a prediction is not a wild guess. It's a thoughtful estimate based on what you already know — about the world, about patterns you've observed, and about how things usually work.

The best format for a scientific prediction is: 'If [something happens], then [result will occur], because [reason].'

Example: "If I add more weight to my paper airplane, then it will fly a shorter distance, because heavier objects need more force to travel through the air and gravity will pull them down faster."

What Is a Fair Test?

Here's a challenge: You want to know whether watering plants more often makes them grow taller. You set up two plants. Plant A gets water every day. Plant B gets water once a week. Plant A is near a window. Plant B is in a dark corner. Plant A is a tomato plant. Plant B is a cactus.

You check them after a month and Plant A is much taller. Can you conclude that more watering caused this? No! Because everything was different between the two plants, not just the watering. This is an unfair test.

A fair test (also called a controlled experiment) changes only ONE thing and measures how THAT one thing affects the result. Everything else must be kept exactly the same.

Variables — The Scientific Word for 'The Things That Can Change'

Scientists use a special word: variable. A variable is anything in an experiment that can change. There are three kinds:

  • Independent variable: The ONE thing you deliberately change (e.g., amount of water)
  • Dependent variable: What you measure to see if it was affected (e.g., plant height)
  • Controlled variables: Everything ELSE that you keep the same (same plant type, same soil, same light, same temperature)

Why Galileo Was Brilliant

When Galileo dropped objects from the Leaning Tower of Pisa, everyone around him 'knew' that heavier objects fell faster — Aristotle, the greatest philosopher in history, had said so 2,000 years earlier. Galileo dared to question this. He made a prediction: if weight doesn't matter, both objects will fall at the same speed. He tested it. He was right.

This moment is considered one of the births of modern science. Galileo showed that observing and testing beats having an authority figure tell you something is true. This principle — evidence over authority — is one of the foundations of scientific thinking.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Design your own fair test! Choose a question from the list below or make up your own. Write: (1) Your question, (2) Your prediction with reason, (3) Your independent variable, (4) Your dependent variable, (5) Three controlled variables.

  • Does the colour of a cup affect how fast ice melts inside it?
  • Does the length of a paper airplane's wings affect how far it flies?
  • Does the temperature of soil affect how fast seeds sprout?

Auto-saved

When scientists get results that are different from their prediction, should they be disappointed or excited? Explain why.
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1 & 2: Paper Airplane Fair Test

Build 3 paper airplanes — all identical except ONE variable you choose to change (wing length, weight of paper clip added, nose shape). Fly each one 5 times and measure how far it goes. Average your results. Did your results match your prediction?

Auto-saved

Day 3: My Prediction Scorecard

Make 10 predictions today about small, everyday things (will this leaf sink or float? will this ice cube melt in 10 minutes? will the cat come when called?). Test each one. What percentage of your predictions were correct? What does that tell you?

Auto-saved

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

Week Project: The Big Fair Test

Design AND carry out a complete, fair scientific test on a question of your choosing. Your report must include:

  1. Your testable question
  2. Your hypothesis
  3. Your independent, dependent, and controlled variables
  4. Your materials list
  5. Your step-by-step procedure
  6. Your results (measurements or observations)
  7. Your conclusion (did results match your prediction? why or why not?)

Auto-saved

🏠

Homework Activities

Walk through your home and find 5 things that are the result of someone testing which design works better (the shape of a door handle, the design of a fridge, the material used for a jacket). For each, predict: what problem were the designers trying to solve? What did they probably test?

Auto-saved

🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Has science ever proven something 'wrong' that everyone thought was 'obviously right'? Name one example.
  • If you had to design the most perfect experiment ever, what question would it answer?
  • Why do scientists repeat experiments many times before they trust the results?
Week 5–6

📊 Collecting and Recording Data

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — A1.3 – Collecting and recording data; A1.4 – Analysing data
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

On November 19, 1969, astronaut Pete Conrad stepped onto the moon and said 'Whoopee! Man, that may have been a small one for Neil, but that's a long one for me!'

But the real triumph of that mission — and of the entire Apollo space program — wasn't the landing itself. It was the data. NASA engineers had collected, recorded, and analyzed more than 38 years of data about rocket trajectories, fuel consumption, gravitational forces, and atmospheric conditions. Without that data, there would be no moon landing.

Data changed history. Every scientific discovery you've ever heard of — vaccines, clean water, safe buildings, GPS on your phone — exists because people carefully collected and recorded data. Today, you become a data scientist.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand what data is and why scientists collect it
  • Learn different ways to record data: tally marks, tables, drawings, measurements
  • Practice making clear, organized data tables
  • Begin to identify patterns in data
  • Understand the difference between qualitative data (descriptions) and quantitative data (numbers)
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials: Ruler, thermometer (optional), dice, coins, graph paper or lined paper, coloured pencils

Introducing Data Types (10 min)

Play a simple game: roll a die 20 times and record each result using tally marks. Count up the tallies. Which number came up most? Least? This is simple quantitative data collection. Now go outside and count how many birds, clouds, or types of leaves you see. Same idea, different context.

Making a Data Table (15 min)

Show your child how to set up a clean data table: a title, column headers, and rows. Emphasize: data tables need labels! '5' is not useful data. '5 centimetres' is. '5 minutes' is. Always include units.

Finding Patterns (10 min)

After collecting some data, ask: What do you notice? What is the most common result? What is surprising? Can you see a pattern? Graph one simple set of data together (bar graph with coloured blocks).

📖

The Big Lesson

What Is Data?

Data is information. Specifically, it's the information scientists collect during observations and experiments. Without data, a scientist has only opinions and guesses. With data, they have evidence.

Data comes in two main types:

  • Qualitative data: Descriptions using words. Colour, smell, texture, appearance. ('The flower is yellow with six petals.')
  • Quantitative data: Measurements using numbers. Height, weight, time, temperature. ('The flower is 14cm tall and has 6 petals, each 3cm long.')

Both types are valuable! Qualitative data gives context and richness. Quantitative data gives precision and allows comparison.

How Scientists Record Data

There are many ways to record data. Scientists choose the best one for their type of information:

  • Data tables: Grid of rows and columns — best for organized measurements
  • Tally charts: Quick counting — great for events that happen over time
  • Drawings and diagrams: Pictures with labels — essential for describing shapes, structures, organisms
  • Bar graphs: Show and compare amounts visually
  • Line graphs: Show changes over time
  • Photographs: Capture a moment precisely

The Most Important Rule of Data

The most important rule is: record exactly what you observe, not what you expect to observe. This sounds easy, but it's actually one of the hardest things to do in science.

Our brains are wired to see patterns and confirm what we expect to see. If you expect a plant to grow taller in sunlight, you might measure it slightly more generously than the plant in the shade. Scientists have special methods (like having someone else check their measurements, or recording data without knowing which condition the subject is in) to fight against this tendency.

Confirmation bias: The scientific term for our brain's tendency to notice evidence that confirms what we already believe and ignore evidence that doesn't. ALL humans have it. Good scientists actively fight against it.

Patterns in Data — The Purpose of It All

The reason scientists collect so much data is to look for patterns. A pattern is when data points behave in a consistent, predictable way. Patterns let scientists make general statements about how the world works.

For example: if you measure the temperature every day for a year, you'll find a very clear pattern — warmer in summer, cooler in winter, with gradual transitions in between. That pattern is what we call the seasons!

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Collect data on the weather for the next 7 days. Every day at the same time, record: temperature (if you have a thermometer, otherwise just use words like 'cold', 'cool', 'warm', 'hot'), cloud cover (sunny, partly cloudy, mostly cloudy, overcast), precipitation (none, light rain, heavy rain, snow). After 7 days, look at your data and answer:

  • What pattern do you see?
  • What was the most common weather this week?
  • If someone asked you 'What was the weather like this week?', what would you say?

Auto-saved

✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Data Collection Station

Using dice, a coin, and a spinner (or just a pencil on paper), run three 'probability experiments'. Roll the die 30 times, flip the coin 20 times, spin the spinner 25 times. Record all results in neat data tables with labels. What patterns do you see?

Auto-saved

Day 2: Bar Graph Artist

Using your coin flip data from Day 1, create a bar graph. Make it neat, colourful, and fully labelled (title, axis labels, units). Now look at your graph: what story does it tell?

Auto-saved

Day 3: Real-World Data Hunt

Find 3 examples of data being used in the real world in your home or neighbourhood. (Hint: nutrition labels, temperature on the oven, speed limit signs, weight of produce, time on a clock are all forms of data.) For each example, explain why this data matters.

Auto-saved

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

Week Project: My Nature Data Report

Spend 10 minutes outside every day for 5 days making careful observations. Choose ONE specific thing to measure and track (height of a plant, number of birds visiting, temperature, types of bugs seen, clouds in the sky). Build a proper data table. At the end, make a graph. Write a paragraph explaining what pattern you found and what you think it means.

Auto-saved

🏠

Homework Activities

Tonight at dinner, look at the food on your plate and collect data: count how many different colours of food there are, estimate the portions (in handfuls or spoonfuls), notice which foods are crunchy vs soft. Write a one-paragraph 'dinner data report'. What do you notice about your diet from the data?

Auto-saved

🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Scientists who study the environment sometimes collect data for decades before they have enough to identify a pattern. Why is patience so important in science?
  • Can data ever lie? How might the same data be used to tell two completely opposite stories?
  • What if two scientists collect data on the same question and get different results? What should they do?
Week 6–7

💡 Drawing Conclusions — What Does It Mean?

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — A1.4 – Analysing data; A1.5 – Communicating findings
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In 1850, a doctor named John Snow was trying to stop a deadly cholera epidemic in London. No one knew what caused cholera — some thought it was bad air. Dr. Snow had a different idea. He made a map of London and put a dot at the location of every cholera death he knew of.

When he stood back and looked at the map, the answer leapt out at him: almost all the deaths clustered around one street — and that street had one water pump.

He had found the pattern in the data. Snow removed the handle from that water pump, people stopped using it, and the epidemic ended. John Snow concluded from his data — a dot map — that cholera was spread through contaminated water, not air. This conclusion saved thousands of lives. Today he is considered the father of modern epidemiology (the science of how diseases spread).

A conclusion is where the real science happens — taking your data and figuring out what it means for the world.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand what a scientific conclusion is
  • Learn to draw conclusions that are supported by evidence
  • Practice the difference between a conclusion and an opinion
  • Learn to communicate findings clearly in words and pictures
  • Understand why conclusions must be honest — even when results are surprising
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials: The data from previous experiments, paper, pencils

The EXEC Method for Conclusions (20 min)

Teach your child this simple framework for writing a conclusion:

  • Evidence — What did your data show? Give specific numbers or observations.
  • Xplains — Use the evidence to explain what happened.
  • Exception — Was there anything surprising or that didn't fit the pattern?
  • Connect — How does your conclusion connect to the real world?

Practice this framework with a simple example first, then have your child apply it to their own experiments.

📖

The Big Lesson

The Most Exciting Moment in Science

You've asked a question. You've designed a fair test. You've collected data. Now comes the most exciting moment of all: figuring out what it means.

A conclusion is a statement that explains what you found and what it means. A good conclusion is always connected directly to evidence — it doesn't just state an opinion.

Opinion: "I think water evaporates because it wants to escape." Conclusion based on evidence: "Based on my measurements, the puddle lost 3 cm of depth in 2 hours on a sunny day but only 0.5 cm in 2 hours on a cloudy day. I conclude that sunlight increases the rate of evaporation."

What If Your Results Don't Match Your Hypothesis?

This is a crucial scientific moment. You predicted one thing and got another. What do you do?

First: check your experiment. Did you make an error? Were all your variables actually controlled? Is your data accurate?

If you're satisfied the experiment was done correctly: change your hypothesis. Science is about following the evidence, not defending your original idea. The greatest scientists in history are the ones who were most willing to change their minds when the evidence demanded it.

Communicating Your Findings

A discovery that nobody knows about is no discovery at all. Scientists share their findings — through papers, presentations, graphs, and diagrams — so that other scientists can check their work, build on it, and ask new questions.

When you communicate your findings, be clear and honest: share what you found, how you found it, and be upfront about anything that might have gone wrong or uncertain. Science is built on trust.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Take your plant experiment results (from Lesson A2 or A4) or make up a simple set of data. Write a complete conclusion using the EXEC method:

  • Evidence — What did the data show?
  • Explains — What does this mean?
  • Exception — Was anything surprising?
  • Connect — Why does this matter in real life?

Auto-saved

✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Conclusion Detective

Read these 'scientist reports' and decide: does each one give a proper conclusion backed by evidence, or just an opinion? Fix the opinion ones to make them proper science conclusions.

Report A: "I think plants are happier in sunlight because they look prettier."
Report B: "In my experiment, the plant in sunlight grew 8cm over 2 weeks while the plant in the dark grew only 1cm. I conclude that sunlight is necessary for plant growth."
Report C: "My dog is the fastest dog in the world."

Auto-saved

Day 2 & 3: Present Your Science!

Choose your favourite investigation from this strand and prepare a short 5-minute 'science presentation' for your family. Include: your question, your hypothesis, your data (draw a graph or table), your conclusion, and one new question your investigation raised.

Auto-saved

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

Week: Science Communication Project

Create a full, beautiful science poster about any investigation you've done this term. Your poster must include: question, hypothesis, materials, method (step-by-step), data table or graph, conclusion, what you would do differently next time. Make it colourful and clear enough for a 5-year-old to understand.

Auto-saved

🏠

Homework Activities

Find a news story or YouTube video about a recent scientific discovery. Ask a parent to help if needed. Then answer: What was the question? What evidence did scientists find? What conclusion did they draw? Do YOU agree with their conclusion? Why or why not?

Auto-saved

🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Why do you think scientists share their work with other scientists even before it's 'finished'?
  • Has someone ever told you something they thought was true but turned out not to be? How did you figure out they were wrong?
Week 7–8

⚙️ Engineering Design Thinking

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — A1.6 – Engineering design process; A2.1 – Connections to technology
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

The Eiffel Tower in Paris weighs about 7,300 tonnes and is 330 metres tall. It was built in 1889 — more than 130 years ago — without computers, without electric cranes, without modern machinery.

The engineer who designed it, Gustave Eiffel, used pure mathematics and an understanding of forces to create something that has stood for over a century. But his success wasn't magical — it came from a process:

Identify the problem → Research → Brainstorm solutions → Choose the best one → Build a prototype → Test → Improve → Share. This is the Engineering Design Process, and it is used for everything from the Eiffel Tower to your smartphone to the shoes on your feet.

Today you become an engineer.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand the Engineering Design Process (EDP) and its 8 steps
  • Distinguish between science (investigating what exists) and engineering (creating what doesn't exist yet)
  • Practice identifying problems and brainstorming creative solutions
  • Build and test a prototype, then improve it based on results
  • Understand that failure in engineering is a step toward success, not the end
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials: Marshmallows and spaghetti (or toothpicks and clay), paper, tape, scissors, a small weight (like a coin), ruler

The Marshmallow Tower Challenge (30+ min)

The classic marshmallow challenge: using 20 pieces of spaghetti, 1m of tape, 1m of string, and 1 marshmallow, build the tallest freestanding structure possible that holds the marshmallow on top. You have 18 minutes. This is a brilliantly simple demonstration of the engineering design process.

After the challenge, debrief: What went wrong? What did you try first? What did you change? How did you improve? This debrief is where the real learning happens.

Science vs Engineering (10 min)

Help your child see the difference: science asks 'How does the world work?' Engineering asks 'How can I make the world work better for people?' Both are essential. Engineers need science. Scientists need engineers to build their tools.

📖

The Big Lesson

Creating the Future

Science tries to understand the world as it is. Engineering tries to create the world as it could be.

Engineers are problem-solvers. They see something that doesn't work as well as it could and they say: 'I can fix that. I can make it better.' Then they follow a careful process to create something new.

The 8 Steps of Engineering Design

  1. Define the problem (what exactly needs to be solved?)
  2. Research (what do we already know?)
  3. Brainstorm solutions (think of many ideas — the crazier the better!)
  4. Select the best solution (evaluate your brainstorm ideas)
  5. Build a prototype (a first version — rough is fine!)
  6. Test your prototype (does it actually work?)
  7. Evaluate and improve (what went wrong? how can you fix it?)
  8. Communicate your solution (share what you made and learned)

Notice steps 6, 7, and 8 can loop back to step 5 many times. The best engineers test and improve their designs again and again. This is why engineering often takes months or years — great designs go through many, many versions.

Famous Engineering Failures That Led to Success

James Dyson built 5,126 failed prototypes of his revolutionary vacuum cleaner before creating one that worked perfectly. He said: 'I made 5,127 prototypes of my vacuum before I got it right. There were 5,126 failures. But I learned from each one.'

SpaceX rocket boosters crashed and exploded dozens of times before they could land successfully. Each failure gave engineers crucial data that made the next attempt better. Today SpaceX lands rockets routinely — something that seemed impossible just 20 years ago.

The key mindset of engineering: There is no such thing as a failed prototype. There are only prototypes that haven't been improved yet.

Engineering Is Everywhere

Look around the room you're in right now. Everything you see was engineered: the shape of the chair to support your spine, the angle of the window to let in maximum light, the material of the floor to be durable and safe, the design of your pencil so it doesn't roll off desks. Engineering isn't just bridges and rockets — it's everything humans have ever made.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Choose ONE problem in your home or life that bothers you. (Examples: socks always fall down, backpack straps hurt your shoulders, it's hard to open food packages, lights are left on in empty rooms.) Go through the engineering design process steps 1–4: define the problem, research it, brainstorm 5 solutions, choose the best one. Draw your solution!

Auto-saved

✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Days 1–3: The Big Build Challenge

Your engineering challenge: build a bridge out of index cards and tape that can hold the weight of a full water bottle between two stacks of books. You may only use 10 index cards and 30cm of tape. Test it. Improve it. Record each version and what you changed.

Auto-saved

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

Week Project: Engineer Your World

Complete a full engineering design project:

  1. Choose a problem (something in your life that could be better)
  2. Research: how do people currently solve this problem?
  3. Brainstorm 5 solutions with drawings
  4. Choose the best and explain why
  5. Build a prototype with household materials
  6. Test it with at least 3 different tests
  7. Improve your design based on test results
  8. Present your final design to your family with a poster explaining all 8 steps

Auto-saved

🏠

Homework Activities

Look at one piece of technology you use every day (a fridge, a door, a light switch, a pencil). Imagine you are the engineer who invented it 100 years ago. What problem were you trying to solve? What was the simplest version you might have built first? Draw it. How many improvements do you think it took to get to the version we use today?

Auto-saved

🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • If you could design any invention in the world, what problem would you solve?
  • Why do engineers study science? Can you be a good engineer without knowing science?
  • What do you think is the most impressive engineering achievement in human history? Why?
Week 8

🌍 Science Changes the World

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — A2.1 – Connections to society; A2.2 – Indigenous knowledge systems
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In 1796, a doctor named Edward Jenner noticed something fascinating: milkmaids — women who worked with cows — almost never got smallpox, one of the deadliest diseases in history. Instead, they often got a mild cow disease called cowpox.

Jenner wondered: Does getting cowpox protect you from smallpox? He tested this idea — ethically, on a willing patient — and found that yes, it did. He called his discovery 'vaccination' (from 'vacca', Latin for cow).

The result of one scientist's curious observation: Smallpox killed over 300 million people in the 20th century before it was finally eradicated in 1980 — the first human disease ever wiped off the planet — entirely due to vaccines based on Jenner's original discovery 200 years earlier. One question changed history forever.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand how science has changed human life for the better throughout history
  • Recognize that science is done by people from all backgrounds and cultures
  • Learn about Indigenous knowledge systems as a form of scientific understanding
  • Understand that science has responsibilities — it can be used for good or harm
  • Feel a sense of personal connection to the scientific tradition
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

The Timeline of Discoveries (15 min)

Work with your child to create a simple timeline of the most important scientific discoveries you can think of together. Include: fire control (400,000 years ago), farming (10,000 years ago), writing (5,000 years ago), printing press (500 years ago), electricity (150 years ago), vaccines, internet. Ask: which one do you think is most important? Why?

Indigenous Knowledge (10 min)

Introduce the concept that many Indigenous peoples around the world have developed sophisticated understanding of plants, animals, weather, and ecology over thousands of years of careful observation — long before modern science. This is a valid and important form of knowledge. Examples: Indigenous weather prediction from plant and animal behaviour, traditional medical plants (many modern medicines come from plants Indigenous peoples identified), navigation by stars without instruments.

This is a wonderful opportunity to explore if your own cultural background has traditional knowledge about nature. Ask grandparents or elders if you have access to them.
📖

The Big Lesson

Standing on the Shoulders of Giants

The scientist Isaac Newton, who discovered the laws of gravity and motion, once wrote: 'If I have seen farther than others, it is by standing on the shoulders of giants.' He meant that all scientists build on the discoveries of the scientists who came before them.

Every single thing in your life that makes it safer, longer, more comfortable, and more connected to other people is the result of a long chain of scientific discoveries. The phone (or tablet or computer) you might use was made possible by discoveries in physics, chemistry, materials science, and computer science spanning over 300 years of work by thousands of scientists from dozens of countries.

Science Across Cultures

Science is not owned by any one country or culture. The number system you use every day was developed in India and passed to Europe through Arabic mathematicians. The concept of the hospital was developed in the Islamic world. The understanding of how vaccines work owes much to experiments done in China and the Middle East centuries before Edward Jenner.

Indigenous peoples around the world — including the First Nations, Métis, and Inuit peoples of Canada — developed profound knowledge of the natural world through thousands of years of careful observation, experimentation, and knowledge-sharing.

Example of Indigenous science: Many First Nations in Canada learned to identify which plants could be used as medicine, which mushrooms were edible vs poisonous, how to predict weather from animal behavior and plant conditions, and how to farm in harmony with the land. This knowledge took generations of observation and careful testing to develop — it is science.

Science Has Responsibilities

Science is powerful. And with great power comes great responsibility. Throughout history, science has been used both to help people and to cause harm.

Today, scientists think carefully about the ethics of their work — about what should and shouldn't be done, about who will be affected by their discoveries, and about how to make sure science benefits everyone and not just some people.

As you grow up as a scientist and citizen, you will be part of deciding which scientific projects get funded, which technologies get developed, and how discoveries are used. That's a tremendous responsibility — and a tremendous opportunity.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Think about a scientific discovery or invention that you think has made the world better. Now think about whether it has also made some part of the world worse. (Examples: cars provide transportation but also pollution; phones connect people but also distract us; pesticides protect crops but can harm insects.) Write about both sides.

Auto-saved

If you were a scientist and discovered something amazing but you knew it could be used to hurt people, what would you do?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: My Scientific Heritage

With a family member, explore: Where are your ancestors from? Look up one scientific or traditional knowledge tradition from that culture. What did they contribute to human understanding of the natural world? Write about it and draw a picture.

Auto-saved

Day 2 & 3: Dream Discovery

What scientific discovery do you wish someone would make? What problem in the world would it solve? Draw a picture of your dream discovery and write about it: What is it? How would it work (your best guess)? Who would it help? Are there any potential downsides?

Auto-saved

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

Week Project: The Story of a Discovery

Research (with a parent's help) the story of ONE major scientific discovery. Write and illustrate a short children's book (at least 5 illustrated pages) that tells the story of this discovery for a younger child. Include: Who made it? When? Where? What question were they trying to answer? What did they discover? How did it change the world?

Auto-saved

🏠

Homework Activities

Ask three adults in your life this question: 'What is one scientific discovery or invention you couldn't imagine living without? Why?' Record their answers. Then answer it yourself. Do your answers surprise you?

Auto-saved

🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • What problem in the world do you think is most urgently in need of a scientific solution?
  • Some people say that the best scientists are also poets and artists — people with big imaginations. Do you agree? Why?
  • If you could go back in time to any moment in the history of science and watch a discovery happen, which would you choose?
Reference · All Year

📚 Vocabulary Dictionary

All key science words for Grade 1
📖

Science Word Bank

Learn these important science words. Read each word and its meaning. Can you use the word in your own sentence?

Science
A way of asking questions about the world and testing ideas to find answers
Observation
Something you notice directly using your senses
Inference
A conclusion you draw from what you have observed
Hypothesis
A careful prediction that includes a reason; an educated guess
Variable
Anything in an experiment that can change
Fair test
An experiment where only ONE thing is changed at a time
Data
Information collected during an observation or experiment
Qualitative
Data described in words (not numbers)
Quantitative
Data measured in numbers
Conclusion
A statement explaining what your evidence means
Evidence
Data or observations that support a conclusion
Engineering
Using science and creativity to solve problems and build things
Prototype
The first test version of something you design
Pattern
A regular, repeating result seen in data
Evaporation
The process of liquid water turning into invisible water vapour

Strand B · Life Systems
Week 9–10 · Strand B Opens

🌱 What Is Alive? The Greatest Mystery in Science

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B3.1 – Identify and describe basic characteristics of living things
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

A flame can eat wood. It breathes in oxygen. It moves, it grows bigger, it can make more flames, and eventually it dies. A river flows and moves and carves canyons. A car engine roars, moves, and needs fuel.

Are any of these things alive? And if not — why not? Because they seem to do some of the things that living things do.

Here is the real puzzle: Biologists (scientists who study life) have debated for centuries about exactly what makes something alive. Even today, they sometimes disagree. Viruses — the tiny things that cause colds and flu — are so strange that scientists still argue about whether they count as alive! The question "What is life?" is genuinely one of the most fascinating unsolved questions in all of science.

By the end of today, you will understand the scientific answer to this ancient mystery — and why it's more amazing than you might expect.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Identify the 7 characteristics shared by all living things
  • Explain why being able to do 'some life-like things' doesn't make something alive
  • Sort objects into living, once-living, and never-living categories with scientific reasoning
  • Understand that life comes in an astonishing variety of forms
  • Practice the scientific habit of careful definition
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials: A candle (or video of a flame), a collection of objects (rocks, sticks, seeds, leaves, plastic toys, pictures of animals), sorting labels

The Sorting Game (15 min)

Lay out 10-15 objects or pictures and ask: 'Which of these is alive?' Let your child sort them freely first, without guidance. Listen carefully to their reasoning — it reveals their existing understanding and misconceptions.

Common misconceptions to address:

  • Seeds look dead but are alive (they're dormant — like sleeping)
  • A dead leaf is no longer alive, but it was once living
  • Fire does some 'life-like' things but is not alive
  • Rocks have never been alive

The 7 Signs of Life (Main Teaching — 20 min)

Introduce the 7 characteristics one at a time. For each one, find an example in the room or outside. Then ask: Does a flame do this? Does a river do this? Do all living things do this?

The 7 characteristics: Movement, Respiration, Sensitivity, Growth, Reproduction, Excretion, Nutrition — sometimes remembered as MRSGREN.

📖

The Big Lesson

The Question That Has Puzzled Humans for Thousands of Years

You have probably never thought twice about whether a dog is alive or whether a rock is alive. But what about a seed sitting in a drawer for 10 years? Or a virus that can only do anything when it's inside another creature? Or a coral reef that looks like a rock but is actually millions of tiny animals?

Life is one of the most extraordinary and mysterious things in the universe. As far as we know, Earth is the only planet anywhere with life on it — though scientists are searching. And life here comes in astonishing variety: from bacteria so tiny you'd need 50,000 of them side by side to reach 1 millimetre, to blue whales 30 metres long.

The 7 Characteristics of All Living Things

After centuries of observation and debate, biologists have identified 7 characteristics that ALL living things share:

  1. Movement: All living things can move — either their whole body or parts of it. Even plants, which seem still, move: their roots grow down, their stems grow up, and some flowers track the sun across the sky. Movement in animals is obvious — but it's there in plants too, just much slower.
  2. Respiration: All living things release energy from food through a chemical process called respiration. This is different from breathing! Even plants and bacteria respire — they use oxygen or other chemicals to get energy from their food.
  3. Sensitivity: All living things can sense and respond to changes in their environment. A plant turns toward light. A dog hears you come home. A bacterium swims toward sugar. Even the simplest living thing responds to its world.
  4. Growth: All living things grow and develop according to a plan encoded in their cells. Growth in living things is different from growth in non-living things — a crystal 'grows' by having more material added to it from outside, but a plant grows by making new cells from within.
  5. Reproduction: All living things make copies of themselves — have offspring. A cat has kittens. A flower makes seeds. A bacterium splits in two. Without reproduction, life would vanish from Earth within a single generation.
  6. Excretion: All living things remove the waste products of their chemical processes. Humans excrete in urine, sweat, and breath (carbon dioxide). Plants excrete oxygen (though they need it too) and water vapour through their leaves.
  7. Nutrition: All living things need to take in materials and energy from their environment. Animals eat food. Plants absorb water, minerals, and use sunlight to make sugar. Fungi absorb nutrients from dead material.

Why Isn't Fire Alive?

Fire can grow, move, and spread. It needs fuel (nutrition) and oxygen (respiration). But it cannot reproduce — fire cannot create another fire by itself without burning new material. It cannot sense and respond to its environment in the way living things do. And crucially, it has no cells, no internal organization, no genetic material.

Life requires ALL 7 characteristics, not just some. And most importantly, life is built from cells — the basic units of life. Every living thing is made of at least one cell. Fire, rivers, and rocks have no cells at all.

The Amazing Variety of Life

Life on Earth comes in forms so bizarre and wonderful that scientists regularly discover species they couldn't have imagined. There is a jellyfish (Turritopsis dohrnii) that appears to be biologically immortal — when stressed, it can revert back to its juvenile form and start its life cycle over again. There are tardigrades (water bears) — tiny animals that can survive the vacuum of space, extreme radiation, and temperatures from -272°C to 150°C.

There are trees that are 5,000 years old. There are birds that can navigate by the stars. There are plants that eat insects. Life on Earth is astonishing — and you are part of it.

Mind-expanding fact: Scientists estimate there are 8.7 million species of living things on Earth. We have formally discovered and named only about 1.5 million of them. Millions of species may still be unknown to science — hiding in rainforests, in the deep ocean, and in the soil beneath your feet.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Use the 7 characteristics (MRSGREN) to investigate these items. For each one, go through all 7 characteristics and mark whether the item has that characteristic. Then decide: is it living, once-living, or never-living?

  • A mushroom growing on a log
  • A piece of bread
  • The flame of a candle
  • A river flowing over rocks
  • A dried bean (seed)
  • A piece of wool (from a sheep)

Auto-saved

If scientists found something on another planet that could grow and move but could NOT reproduce, would you call it alive? Why or why not?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Living Things Safari

Go outside (or look out a window very carefully) for 20 minutes. Try to find and record as many different living things as you possibly can. For each one, write or draw: What is it? Which of the 7 characteristics can you observe directly?

Auto-saved

Day 2: The Great Sort

Create 3 columns: LIVING / ONCE-LIVING / NEVER-LIVING. Sort these 15 items into the columns and explain your reasoning for each: a rock, a wooden table, a worm, milk, a plastic toy, a dead leaf, a seed, an apple, sand, a dog, a feather, bread, a coal, an insect wing, an egg. Some are tricky! Discuss with your parent.

Auto-saved

Day 3: Draw the Web of Life

On a big piece of paper, draw as many different kinds of living things as you can — try to include something from each major group: animals, plants, fungi, bacteria (you can look these up with a parent). Try to include at least 20 different types. Draw lines between any that are connected.

Auto-saved

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

Week Project: My Living Neighbourhood

Over 5 days, you are going to do a survey of every living thing you can find in your home, yard, or neighbourhood. Each day, visit the same area and record what you find. Use the 7 characteristics to confirm that each thing is truly living.

  1. Day 1: Indoor living things (plants, pets, any insects or mould you find)
  2. Day 2: Yard/balcony living things
  3. Day 3: Living things on a walk (trees, birds, insects, fungi)
  4. Day 4: Microscopic potential (examine soil or pond water if available with magnifying glass)
  5. Day 5: Complete your species list. Count how many you found. Make a poster showing your top 10 most interesting finds.

Auto-saved

🏠

Homework Activities

Family Research Night

With a family member, look up one of the 'extremophile' organisms — living things that survive in extreme conditions. Examples: tardigrades, Pompeii worms (live near volcanic vents), Deinococcus radiodurans (survives radiation). Share what you find: Where does it live? How does it survive? Which of the 7 characteristics does it use in unusual ways?

Auto-saved

🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • If life can survive in volcanoes and in space, where else in the universe might life exist?
  • Could humans ever create life from scratch in a laboratory? Would that be a good idea?
  • There are animals that have not changed in 300 million years (like horseshoe crabs). Why do you think they didn't need to change?
Week 10–11

🔍 The Signs of Life — Looking More Closely

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B2.1 – Investigate characteristics of living things; B3.1 – Describe characteristics
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Have you ever looked at a drop of pond water under a microscope? Inside that one tiny drop — smaller than your pinky fingernail — there might be 500 different living creatures. They swim, eat, reproduce, and die, all in a world invisible to our eyes.

Anton van Leeuwenhoek, a Dutch lens-maker in the 1670s, was the first human to see this world. He ground glass lenses more finely than anyone before him and looked at water from a nearby lake. What he saw shocked him so completely that he wrote letters to the Royal Society of London describing 'little animals' that 'moved very prettily' in the water.

He had discovered the microbial world — an entire universe of living things that had been there all along, completely invisible to all of human history. Right now, there are more bacteria living on and in your body than there are cells IN your body. You are not just you — you are an ecosystem.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Closely observe and compare living things using senses and tools
  • Identify external features (structures) that living things have
  • Understand that living things have structures that help them survive
  • Compare the observable features of animals and plants
  • Begin to understand that even very different living things have things in common
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials: Magnifying glass, collection of leaves, insects (dead is fine), seeds, bark pieces, ruler, graph paper for drawing

Structured Observation Activity (20 min)

Collect 3 different leaves from different plants (if possible). Have your child compare them very carefully using their senses and a magnifying glass. Guide them to notice:

  • Shape of the leaf (oval, pointy, round, lobed?)
  • Edge of the leaf (smooth, jagged, wavy?)
  • Colour (dark green, light green, has spots?)
  • Texture (smooth, fuzzy, waxy?)
  • Veins — the pattern they make
  • Size (measure with ruler)

Ask: Why do you think different plants have different shaped leaves? (Introduce the idea that each feature might help the plant in some way — survival value.)

Animal Body Parts Walk (15 min)

Look at pictures of 5 very different animals (spider, fish, bird, snail, elephant). For each, ask: What body parts does it have? What do you think each part is for?

📖

The Big Lesson

Every Feature Has a Purpose

Living things are not random collections of parts. Every feature of an organism — its shape, colour, size, texture, internal organs — has been shaped over millions of years of evolution to help that organism survive.

When you look at a bird's beak, you're seeing millions of years of problem-solving. A hummingbird's long, thin beak lets it reach deep into flowers for nectar. A hawk's sharp, hooked beak lets it tear meat. A pelican's enormous pouched beak lets it scoop fish from water. Same basic structure — a beak — but shaped differently to solve different problems.

Comparing Animals and Plants

One of the most interesting activities in biology is comparing very different organisms and looking for what they have in common.

Animals have: mouths (to take in food), lungs or gills (to respire), eyes and ears (to sense the environment), legs or fins or wings (to move), skins or shells (to protect themselves).

Plants have: roots (to absorb water and anchor in soil), stems (to support and transport), leaves (to capture sunlight and make food), flowers and seeds (to reproduce).

Both animals and plants have: cells, a way to get energy (nutrition), a way to reproduce, a way to sense and respond to their environment, and a lifespan with a beginning and an end.

The Microscopic World

But visible features are just the beginning. Even more fascinating is what you cannot see. Every cell in your body right now — and you have about 37 trillion of them — is a tiny living machine. Each cell has its own 'brain' (the nucleus), its own energy factory (mitochondria), and its own manufacturing system.

Cells are so small that 10,000 of them could fit on the period at the end of this sentence.

Amazing comparison: You have 37 trillion cells. The bacterium E. coli has just one. Yet that single cell can find food, move, reproduce, and respond to its environment. What your single trillion cells do together — think, feel, run, dream — is built from billions of years of evolution starting from that one-cell ancestor.

Reading Nature's Labels

Every visible feature of a living thing is like a label telling you about that organism's lifestyle. A plant with thick, waxy leaves lives somewhere dry — the wax prevents water loss. An animal with big eyes lives where light is dim. An animal with lots of sharp teeth is probably a hunter. An animal with flat grinding teeth probably eats plants.

Scientists call this 'reading the adaptations' — figuring out how an organism lives from the clues of its body. It's like being a detective, and you can practice it anywhere you find living things.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Look very carefully at one living thing outside — a plant, a bug, a bird, anything. Spend at least 5 minutes observing it. Then answer:

  • List 10 things you can observe about this organism.
  • For 3 of those features, make a guess about WHY that feature helps the organism survive.
  • What question do you most want answered about this organism?

Auto-saved

If you could design a living thing perfectly adapted to live in the Arctic (very cold, icy, few plants), what features would it have?
✏️

Look at pictures of these animals: fish, bird, frog, spider, snail, deer. For each feature listed, write which animals have it: legs, wings, fins, shell, fur, scales, feathers. Then answer: Which two animals are most similar? Most different? What surprised you?

Invent a completely imaginary living thing. Design its body for a specific environment you choose (deep ocean, dark cave, hot desert, Arctic tundra, treetop canopy). Draw it and label all its features. Write one sentence explaining what each feature is for.

  • A detailed scientific drawing (in pencil first, then colour)
  • The organism's name (common name is fine)
