🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Scientists have discovered species living 10 km underwater in total darkness with no sunlight. How do you think they get energy?
  • Why do some animals have camouflage (blending in with their environment)? Can you think of a plant that does this?
  • If a new continent appeared tomorrow with no living things on it, how do you think life would eventually get there?
Week 11–12

🐾 What Animals Need to Survive

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B3.2 – Describe basic needs of living things; B2.2 – Investigate needs
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

The Arctic ground squirrel is one of the most extreme mammals on Earth. Each autumn, it digs a burrow 2 metres underground. Its body temperature drops to -3°C — below freezing. Its heart beats just once every minute. Its breathing becomes almost imperceptible.

For 8 months, this animal is technically in a state that scientists call hibernation — but which is so extreme that it borders on being clinically dead. And yet, every spring, it wakes up. Its heart speeds up. Its temperature rises. It digs its way to the surface.

How does it survive winter without any food, water, or warmth for 8 months? This is the mystery of basic needs. All animals need the same basic things to survive — but the strategies they use to get those needs met are endlessly astonishing.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Identify the 4 basic needs of animals: food, water, air/oxygen, and shelter
  • Understand that different animals meet these same needs in very different ways
  • Compare how various animals are adapted to get their basic needs in different environments
  • Investigate how one animal meets all 4 basic needs in its specific habitat
  • Begin to understand the relationship between an animal's body and its environment
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials: Paper, pencils, books or internet access (with parent supervision) for animal research

Opening Discussion: What Do YOU Need? (10 min)

Start with your child: What did you do when you woke up this morning? (Drank water, ate breakfast, breathed air, slept in a safe bed.) Explain: these are your basic needs. Now ask: Does every animal have the same basic needs? Let them predict before you teach them.

The 4 Basic Needs (15 min)

Teach the 4 basic needs clearly:

  • Food: Energy source. Different animals eat very different things (plants, animals, fungi, bacteria), but all need to take in energy.
  • Water: All living things need water for their chemical processes. Even desert animals get water somehow.
  • Air (oxygen): Most animals need oxygen to respire. Even fish get dissolved oxygen from water through their gills.
  • Shelter: Animals need protection from weather, predators, and extreme temperatures.

Animal Research Station (20 min)

Help your child research one animal in depth: How does it get food? Where does it get water? How does it breathe? Where does it shelter? What happens if one need isn't met?

📖

The Big Lesson

The Four Things Every Animal Needs

All animals — from the tiniest ant to the largest elephant, from a goldfish in a bowl to a blue whale in the Pacific Ocean — need four things to stay alive:

  • Food — to provide energy and building materials
  • Water — to keep chemical processes running
  • Air (oxygen) — to release energy from food through respiration
  • Shelter — to be safe from weather, predators, and extreme temperatures

But what makes the natural world so endlessly fascinating is the variety of ways animals meet these same four needs. The strategies are so creative, so extreme, so clever, that biologists are still discovering new ones every year.

Food: The Diversity of Eating

Animals that eat only plants are called herbivores. Cows, deer, rabbits, elephants, caterpillars, and most insects are herbivores. They have teeth and digestive systems specially designed for processing plant material, which is tough and hard to digest.

Animals that eat only other animals are called carnivores. Lions, eagles, sharks, wolves, and spiders are carnivores. They typically have sharp teeth or claws or beaks designed for catching and killing prey.

Animals that eat both plants and animals are called omnivores. Bears, humans, raccoons, and chickens are omnivores. We are among the most flexible eaters on Earth.

Record breaker: The blue whale eats up to 40 million krill (tiny shrimp-like animals) per day — about 3,600 kg of food. Meanwhile, some spiders can go for months without eating at all.

Water: Getting It in Unusual Ways

You probably get your water by drinking it. But many animals get water in surprising ways:

  • Koalas get most of their water from the leaves they eat
  • Desert beetles collect water by standing in fog and letting it condense on their back
  • Frogs absorb water directly through their skin
  • Seagulls can drink salt water (they have special glands to remove the salt)
  • Thorny devil lizards collect water through tiny channels in their skin that direct water to their mouth

Air: Breathing in Many Ways

Humans breathe air through our lungs. But there are other ways to get oxygen:

  • Fish absorb dissolved oxygen from water through their gills
  • Insects breathe through tiny holes all along their body called spiracles
  • Many worms and frogs breathe partly through their moist skin
  • Whales and dolphins are mammals — they breathe air but must hold their breath underwater

Shelter: Creative Homes

Animals need shelter to be safe. The variety of animal homes is one of the wonders of the natural world:

  • Beavers build dams and lodges from sticks and mud
  • Bowerbirds build elaborate decorated stages to attract mates
  • Termites build mounds taller than a person, with their own air conditioning system
  • Emperor penguins huddle together in groups of thousands to share body heat
  • Arctic foxes grow extra-thick fur and curl into a ball, using their own body as shelter
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Choose one of these animals and investigate how it meets all 4 basic needs: an Arctic polar bear, a desert camel, a deep-sea anglerfish, or a city pigeon. For each need, describe: does this animal get this need the same way you do? Or differently? What's the most surprising thing about how it survives?

Auto-saved

If you had to survive in a forest with no tools, no food, no water, and no shelter, what would you do first? What would you look for? What do you think would be the hardest need to meet?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Animal Needs Research

Choose your favourite animal. Research it deeply (use books or supervised internet). Create a 'Survival Fact Sheet':

  • What does it eat? How does it hunt or find food?
  • How does it get water?
  • How does it breathe?
  • What kind of shelter does it build or find?
  • What happens if any of its needs aren't met?

Auto-saved

Auto-saved

Choose an animal. Draw and describe the perfect habitat for it — a place where all 4 of its needs would be met. Label your design to show where the food, water, air, and shelter come from. If the habitat is disrupted (for example, a river dries up), what happens to your animal?

📋

  1. Chapter 1: Your chosen animal and its basic needs
  2. Chapter 2: How it gets food (include drawings)
  3. Chapter 3: How it gets water and air

Auto-saved

🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Some scientists think there might be life on other planets that doesn't need water or oxygen. What else could they use instead?
  • Why do you think some animals can survive much longer without food than humans can?
  • What would happen to all other animals if all the plants on Earth disappeared?
Week 12–13

🌿 What Plants Need to Survive

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B3.2 – Describe basic needs of plants; B2.2 – Investigate plant needs
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Right now, every green leaf you can see — on trees, on grass, on house plants, on weeds growing through the sidewalk — is performing one of the most extraordinary chemical reactions in the history of Earth.

These leaves are doing something that no human factory can replicate, no machine can copy, and no scientist has been able to fully reproduce in a laboratory: they are turning light into food.

Think about that for a moment. A plant takes water from the soil, invisible carbon dioxide gas from the air, and sunlight — actual light from a star 150 million km away — and uses them to make sugar. Not just any sugar: it's the foundation of almost all energy in almost all ecosystems on Earth. Every piece of food you have ever eaten — the meat, the vegetables, the bread, the fruit — can be traced back to a plant capturing sunlight.

Today you will discover what plants need — and understand why they are among the most important organisms on Earth.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Identify the 4 basic needs of plants: sunlight, water, air (carbon dioxide), and nutrients from soil
  • Understand the process of photosynthesis at a basic level
  • Investigate how plants respond when a need is not met
  • Compare plant needs to animal needs
  • Grow and observe a plant over time
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials: Bean seeds (fastest-growing), soil, small cups or pots, water, a sunny windowsill, one dark location, food colouring (optional for celery-in-coloured-water experiment)

Set Up the Main Experiment (10 min)

Plant bean seeds in 4 identical cups of soil. Label them: (1) Sun + Water (normal), (2) Dark + Water (no sun), (3) Sun + No Water, (4) Normal but add too much water. This experiment will run for 2-3 weeks and provide data for ongoing lesson discussions.

The Photosynthesis Story (15 min)

Tell it as a story: Imagine you are a plant. Your roots are drinking water from the soil. Your leaves are breathing in carbon dioxide from the air. The sun is shining on your leaves. Inside each leaf cell there are tiny green factories called chloroplasts...

Draw the simple equation on paper together: Water + CO₂ + Sunlight → Sugar + Oxygen. Plants are making sugar AND releasing the oxygen you're breathing right now. Help your child feel genuine wonder at this.

The Celery Experiment (20 min, ongoing)

Put a stalk of celery (or white carnation) in a glass of water with 10-20 drops of red or blue food colouring. Leave it for several hours. The coloured water will travel up through the celery, showing how plants transport water.

📖

The Big Lesson

Plants Are the Foundation of Life

Before there were animals on Earth, there were plants (and plant-like organisms). And that's not a coincidence — most animals depend, directly or indirectly, on plants for the energy they need to live.

Plants are the only organisms that can capture energy from sunlight and convert it into food that animals can eat. This makes them absolutely foundational to almost every ecosystem on Earth.

What Plants Need

Like animals, plants have basic needs. But they meet those needs very differently:

  • Sunlight: Energy for photosynthesis — to make their own food
  • Water: Needed for photosynthesis and all cellular processes; absorbed through roots
  • Air (Carbon Dioxide): Used as a raw material in photosynthesis; absorbed through tiny holes in leaves called stomata
  • Nutrients from Soil: Minerals like nitrogen, phosphorus, and potassium are absorbed through roots and used for growth and cell-building

The Marvel of Photosynthesis

Photosynthesis is one of the most important chemical reactions in the history of our planet. Here is how it works:

  1. Plant roots absorb water from the soil
  2. Tiny holes in the leaves (called stomata) open to let carbon dioxide gas from the air enter the leaf
  3. Inside each leaf cell are tiny green structures called chloroplasts
  4. Chloroplasts contain a green chemical called chlorophyll, which absorbs sunlight
  5. The energy from sunlight powers a chemical reaction that combines water and carbon dioxide
  6. This produces glucose (sugar) — the plant's food — and oxygen as a by-product
  7. The oxygen is released through the stomata into the air — which is the oxygen you breathe!
Mind-blowing: The oxygen in every single breath you take was produced by plants (and algae) through photosynthesis. You literally cannot breathe without plants. And every calorie of energy in every food you eat traces back to a plant that captured sunlight.

What Happens When Needs Aren't Met?

Plants show very clearly when their needs are not being met:

  • Too little water: Leaves wilt (go floppy), turn yellow, and eventually die
  • Too much water: Roots rot (can't get oxygen), leaves turn yellow, plant dies
  • Too little light: Plant grows towards any light source, grows very thin and pale (etiolation), leaves become yellow
  • Too little nutrients: Leaves turn yellow (often starting at edges), growth is stunted
  • Too much or wrong nutrients: Leaf burn, unusual colouring, death

The Root System — Underground Mystery

A plant's roots are an incredible, mostly invisible system. An average tree has thousands of kilometres of roots. They reach deep into soil looking for water and minerals. They anchor the plant firmly against wind.

But roots also do something even more extraordinary: they communicate. Through a network of fungi that connects the roots of different trees (called the 'Wood Wide Web' by scientists), trees can actually send sugar and chemical signals to each other. Old trees have been observed to feed young trees through this network. Wounded trees send chemical warnings to neighbors through it. The forest floor beneath your feet is a massive, living communication network.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Check on your 4 plant cups from the experiment. Even after just a few days, you should see differences. Observe and record for each plant:

  • Describe what it looks like (colour, height, leaf condition, any new growth)
  • Which need is not being met?
  • What do you think will happen in the next week?

Auto-saved

If photosynthesis is so amazing, why don't humans just get their energy from sunlight instead of eating food? Why can't we do photosynthesis?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Chlorophyll Chromatography

Crush some fresh green leaves and rub the green colour onto a strip of paper or a coffee filter. Then hold the strip with the coloured end dipped in rubbing alcohol (with adult supervision). Watch as the colours separate — you'll see the different pigments in chlorophyll. Observe and draw the colours you see. What surprised you?

Auto-saved

Day 2: Photosynthesis Equation Art

Create a beautiful illustrated poster showing the photosynthesis equation. Draw the sun, a leaf, water drops, carbon dioxide (tiny circles), sugar molecules (hexagons), and oxygen bubbles. Label everything. Write the equation: Water + CO₂ + Sunlight → Sugar + Oxygen. Make it colourful and clear enough to teach a younger child.

Auto-saved

Day 3: Celery Lab Report

Write up your celery experiment as a formal lab report: Question, Hypothesis, Materials, Procedure, Results (drawing of what happened), and Conclusion. Why did the colour travel up the celery? What part of a real plant does this demonstrate?

Auto-saved

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

Week Project: Plant Growth Diary

Continue observing your 4 experimental plant cups for the next 5 days. Each day, for each plant:

  1. Measure the height (or note 'no germination yet')
  2. Observe the colour and condition of any leaves or sprouts
  3. Draw it
  4. Write your prediction for tomorrow

After 5 days, write a full conclusion: What did each plant teach you about plant needs? What would you do differently in a new experiment? What new questions do you have?

Auto-saved

🏠

Homework Activities

Take a walk and try to find a plant growing somewhere surprising — in a crack in concrete, in a very dark corner, in very dry soil. Observe it carefully: How is it surviving? What need might be hard for it to meet in that spot? Take notes or draw it. Why do you think plants grow in hard places like this?

Auto-saved

🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Plants evolved on Earth 500 million years ago. Animals evolved shortly after. Could animals have evolved first, or do plants have to come first? Why?
  • Scientists are trying to teach bacteria to do photosynthesis to fight climate change. What problems do you think they might run into?
  • Some plants eat insects (like Venus flytraps). Why do they do this if they can photosynthesize?
Week 13–14

🏠 Animal Homes — The Science of Habitats

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B3.3 – Identify and describe basic needs in relation to habitat
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

The Great Barrier Reef off the coast of Australia is the largest living structure on Earth — so large it can be seen from space. It stretches 2,300 km and is home to more than 1,500 species of fish, 4,000 species of molluscs, 240 species of birds, and countless other animals.

But the reef itself is built by one of the simplest animals on Earth: coral polyps. Each coral polyp is a tiny, soft-bodied animal — related to jellyfish. By secreting calcium carbonate, billions of these tiny animals over thousands of years have built the largest biological structure in Earth's history.

A habitat is not just a place where animals happen to live — it is a complex, living system where every species plays a role. Change one species, and the whole system shifts. This is what ecologists mean when they say everything in nature is connected.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Define 'habitat' and explain what a habitat provides for the animals that live there
  • Identify 5 major habitat types (forest, wetland, ocean, grassland, tundra) and describe what makes each unique
  • Understand how animals are adapted to specific habitats
  • Explain what happens when a habitat is disturbed or destroyed
  • Understand the concept of a 'niche' — an animal's specific role in its habitat
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials: Paper, coloured pencils, nature books or supervised internet for research

Habitat Sorting Activity (15 min)

Create 5 labelled cards: forest, wetland, ocean, grassland, Arctic/tundra. Show pictures of 15 different animals and ask your child to sort them into the habitat where they think each lives. Discuss why.

The Key Teaching: Habitat vs Just 'Home'

Help your child understand: a habitat isn't just a 'home' in the sense of a house. A habitat is an entire system — the food, the water, the shelter, the other organisms, the temperature, the rainfall — everything that makes an animal's survival possible. An animal isn't adapted to a single spot; it's adapted to a whole web of conditions.

📖

The Big Lesson

What Is a Habitat?

A habitat is the natural environment in which an organism lives — the place where it finds its food, water, shelter, and the right conditions (like temperature and moisture) to survive and reproduce.

A habitat provides everything an animal needs. If even one element of a habitat changes dramatically — the temperature rises, a river disappears, a forest is cut down, a key food source vanishes — the animals that depend on that habitat face a crisis.

The Major Habitats of Canada and the World

Boreal Forest (Taiga): Canada has the world's largest boreal forest. It stretches across the northern half of the country, full of spruce, pine, and fir trees. Home to moose, bears, wolves, lynx, beavers, and millions of birds that come to breed in summer. The cold winters make survival challenging, so many animals have evolved thick fur, hibernate, or migrate south.

Wetlands: Swamps, marshes, bogs, and fens — wetlands are among the most productive habitats on Earth. Though they cover only 6% of Earth's land, they provide habitat for 40% of all species. They filter water, store carbon, and absorb floods. Ontario alone has more than 100,000 wetland areas.

Grasslands (Prairies): The prairies of western Canada were once home to millions of bison — the largest land animals in North America. Today, much of the prairie has been converted to farmland. Prairie species (burrowing owls, prairie dogs, pronghorn antelope) are adapted to open land, wind, and temperature extremes.

Arctic Tundra: The northernmost parts of Canada are tundra — frozen ground, barely any trees, bitterly cold winters and short summers. Yet it teems with life: Arctic foxes, polar bears, lemmings, musk oxen, billions of migratory birds that nest in summer. The animals here are masters of cold survival.

Freshwater Habitats: Canada has 20% of the world's fresh water — rivers, lakes, and streams teeming with trout, salmon, loons, otters, herons, and frogs. These habitats are lifelines that connect land and water ecosystems.

Niche — Your Specific Role in the Community

Within a habitat, each species has a niche — its specific role. It's not just where it lives, but what it eats, what eats it, when it's active, and what effect it has on other species.

No two species can occupy exactly the same niche for very long — they would compete directly for the same resources. Nature resolves this through specialization: one bird eats insects from tree bark, another eats insects from leaves, another catches them in the air. Same food source (insects), different niches, different adaptations.

Canada's amazing wildlife: Canada is home to more than 200 species of mammals, 600 species of birds, 43 species of reptiles, and 43 species of amphibians — not counting the tens of thousands of insect and invertebrate species. Canada's wilderness is one of the most ecologically significant places on Earth.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Think about your own 'habitat' — your home and neighbourhood. Answer these questions as if you were a wildlife scientist studying humans:

  • What do humans in this habitat eat, and where do they get it?
  • Where do humans get their water?
  • What kind of shelter do humans in this habitat have?
  • What is the human 'niche' in this community (what role does the human play)?
  • What would threaten the humans' ability to survive in this habitat?

If you moved to the Arctic tomorrow with no technology, what new adaptations would you need? Since we can't change our biology, what technology have humans developed to survive there?

🌱 Where Plants Live — Plant Habitats

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B3.3 – Basic needs of plants in relation to their habitat
🔭

In the Atacama Desert in Chile — one of the driest places on Earth — there are plants. They survive years without rain by absorbing tiny amounts of moisture from the morning fog, through specialized leaves. In Antarctica, moss and algae grow on rocks. Plants have found a way to live on almost every surface on Earth. How?

The Big Lesson

Plants Are Everywhere — But Not Just Anywhere

Like animals, plants need specific conditions to thrive. Plants have adapted to nearly every habitat on Earth, from the scorching desert to the frozen tundra, from the deep shadow of a rainforest floor to the rocky cliff edge swept by sea winds.

Sun-loving plants grow in open fields, meadows, and the tops of forests — they have broad, flat leaves to capture as much sunlight as possible. Shade plants grow under forest canopies — they have very large, dark green leaves containing extra chlorophyll to capture the limited light that filters through the trees.

Desert plants like cacti store water in their thick stems and have deep roots to reach underground water, and spines (modified leaves) instead of flat leaves to reduce water loss. Water plants like water lilies have leaves with waxy, water-repelling surfaces (so water runs off and doesn't weigh them down) and long, flexible stems that can bend in water currents without breaking.

Plants Change Their World

Plants don't just live in habitats — they create them. A forest is made by trees. Without the trees, there would be no forest habitat for any of the birds, insects, fungi, or understory plants that live there. When a single tree falls in a forest, dozens of species are affected — some lose shelter, others gain sunlight they couldn't access before. Plants are ecosystem engineers.

Amazing: A single large oak tree can support over 500 different species of insects, birds, mammals, and fungi. Remove the tree and all those species must find somewhere else to live — or perish.

The grasses of the prairies hold the soil in place — without them, wind and rain erode the top layer of soil that all plants need. Mangrove trees along coastlines protect shorelines from storm waves and provide nurseries where young fish grow up safe from larger predators. Every plant species plays a role in its ecosystem.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Go outside and observe 3 different plants growing in very different spots (sunny, shady, dry, wet). For each plant, answer: What conditions does this plant seem to prefer? What features does it have that help it survive here? Could this plant survive in the other plants' spots?

Week 15–16

🦁 How Animals Get Their Food — Predators, Prey & Food Chains

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B3.1 – Describe characteristics including feeding relationships
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In 1995, wolves were reintroduced to Yellowstone National Park in the USA. They hadn't been there for 70 years. What happened next astonished scientists. The elk (which wolves hunted) started avoiding river valleys where they could be caught. The vegetation in those valleys recovered. Trees grew back. The riverbanks stabilized. Rivers changed their course. The entire geography of the park changed — just because wolves returned. Scientists called this a 'trophic cascade' — when one predator changes an entire ecosystem. Wolves changed rivers. This is how connected everything in nature truly is.

🎯

The Big Lesson

The Hunters and the Hunted

Every animal needs energy to stay alive. The way animals get that energy creates one of the most fascinating and complex systems in nature: the food chain, and the broader food web.

A food chain shows who eats whom in a simple line: grass → grasshopper → frog → snake → hawk. Each organism feeds the one above it and is food for the one above that. But real ecosystems are much more complex — most animals eat several different things, and are eaten by several different predators. When you connect all the food chains in an ecosystem, you get a food web.

Animals that hunt and eat other animals are predators. Animals that are eaten are prey. Many animals are both — a frog is a predator when it eats insects, but prey when a heron eats it.

Producers, Consumers, and Decomposers

Producers are plants (and algae). They produce food from sunlight through photosynthesis. All other organisms get their energy ultimately from producers.

Consumers are animals. Primary consumers (herbivores) eat producers. Secondary consumers eat primary consumers. Tertiary consumers eat secondary consumers.

Decomposers — bacteria and fungi — break down dead organisms and return their nutrients to the soil, where producers can use them again. Decomposers are one of the most important groups in any ecosystem, though they're often overlooked.

The energy rule: Each time energy moves up a food chain, about 90% is lost as heat. So 1000 kg of grass supports about 100 kg of deer, which supports about 10 kg of wolves. This is why top predators like lions and wolves are always much rarer than the animals they eat.

Hunting Strategies: The Art of Getting Food

Animals have evolved extraordinary strategies for getting food. Spiders build webs so perfect that they're nearly invisible — and so strong that engineers are studying spider silk for making bulletproof vests. Archerfish spit precise jets of water to knock insects off branches above the water surface. Electric eels generate electric fields of up to 600 volts to stun prey. Humpback whales work together to create 'bubble nets' — blowing curtains of bubbles around a school of fish to concentrate them before lunging upward with open mouths.

The diversity of hunting strategies in nature is a testament to the power of evolution: every possible solution has been tried, refined, and perfected over millions of years.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Draw a food web for your local area. Start with plants and grasses at the bottom. Add the animals that eat those plants. Add the animals that eat those animals. Try to include at least 8 organisms. Then: what would happen to your food web if one species was removed?

Week 16–17

☀️ How Plants Make Food — The Miracle of Photosynthesis

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B2.2 – Investigate how plants make food
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Here is a thought experiment: Take every living thing that has ever existed on Earth — every dinosaur, every whale, every insect, every human, every blade of grass — and trace the energy in their bodies back to its original source. 99.9% of it will lead back to the same place: sunlight captured by a plant or algae through photosynthesis. The sun is the engine of almost all life on Earth. And the green leaf is the mechanism that transforms that engine's output into something every other organism can use.

The Big Lesson

Going Deeper Into Photosynthesis

You learned in Lesson B4 that photosynthesis turns water, carbon dioxide, and sunlight into sugar and oxygen. Now let's go deeper.

Inside each plant cell are tiny green structures called chloroplasts. They're green because of a chemical called chlorophyll — and chlorophyll is green because it absorbs red and blue light from the sun very well, but reflects green light back to your eyes.

Here's a beautiful detail: chlorophyll doesn't just absorb any light — it specifically absorbs the wavelengths of light that are most abundant in sunlight. Over billions of years, evolution has tuned plants to take maximum advantage of the available light. This is why most plants are green and not red or black (though some plants in deep shade have evolved red or purple pigments to capture whatever light they can get).

Day vs Night

Plants photosynthesize during the day when sunlight is available. At night, they can't make food. But they still need energy to stay alive, so they do what animals do: they respire, breaking down the sugar they made during the day to release energy.

Here's the beautiful part: during the day, plants make much more oxygen from photosynthesis than they use in respiration. So during the day, plants release net oxygen. At night, they release small amounts of carbon dioxide. This is why forests are particularly refreshing — the trees are pumping out oxygen all day.

The oxygen equation: One large tree produces enough oxygen for 4 people to breathe for a year. A 1-hectare forest (about the size of a soccer field) absorbs about 6 tonnes of carbon dioxide and produces 4 tonnes of oxygen each year.

Plants Respond to Light

Plants actively seek sunlight. Place a pot plant near a window and in just a few days, all its leaves will have turned to face the sun. This movement is called phototropism. Plants produce a chemical that causes cells on the shaded side of the stem to grow longer than cells on the sunny side — bending the plant toward the light.

Some plants are so sensitive to light that scientists can observe them turning to track the sun across the sky from morning to evening — this is called heliotropism, and sunflowers are the famous example. Young sunflowers track the sun across the sky, turning to face it all day. This warms the flower, which attracts pollinators and speeds seed development.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Investigate phototropism! Put a small plant in a cardboard box with a small hole cut in one side (facing the window). Close the box. Check the plant after 3 days. Which way have the stems and leaves grown? Draw your results. Why did this happen?

Week 17–18

🐛 Animal Life Cycles — From Beginning to End

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B3.1 – Describe characteristics of living things including life cycles
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

A caterpillar builds a chrysalis around itself and liquefies — its entire body dissolves into a kind of biological soup of cells. Then, from that soup, a completely different creature assembles itself: a butterfly. This is not fiction. This is one of the most mind-bending processes in the biological world, and scientists are still working out exactly how it happens. The butterfly has a completely different body, different diet, different behaviour — and yet it's the same individual organism that was once a caterpillar.

🎯

The Big Lesson

What Is a Life Cycle?

Every living thing goes through stages from birth (or germination) to death. This sequence of stages is called a life cycle. Different organisms have very different life cycles in terms of length, number of stages, and what happens at each stage.

The key stages of most animal life cycles: birth (or hatching from an egg), growth and development, reproduction, old age and death.

Two Types of Development

Animals develop in two main ways:

Incomplete metamorphosis (three stages: egg → nymph → adult): The young animal (nymph) looks like a small version of the adult. Examples: grasshoppers, dragonflies, crickets. The nymph moults (sheds its skin) several times as it grows.

Complete metamorphosis (four stages: egg → larva → pupa → adult): The larva looks completely different from the adult. Examples: butterflies, moths, beetles, flies, bees. The transformation in the pupa stage (like a chrysalis) is called metamorphosis.

Record life cycles: A mayfly adult lives for only 24 hours — but spent 1-3 years as an aquatic nymph. The Greenland shark doesn't reach sexual maturity until it's about 150 years old — and may live for over 500 years, making it the longest-lived vertebrate animal on Earth.

Mammal Life Cycles

Mammals (including humans) have a different kind of life cycle. Most mammals are born live (not from eggs), are fed milk by their mothers, and go through childhood, youth, adulthood, and old age.

Human babies are remarkable — we are born in an extremely helpless state compared to most mammals. A wildebeest calf can run within minutes of birth. A human infant can't walk for about a year. This is because our brains are so large that we must be born 'early' (before our heads get too big to pass through the birth canal). We finish most of our brain development in the first few years of life.

Bird Life Cycles

Birds lay eggs (usually kept warm by a parent). When the egg hatches, the chick is either helpless (like robins, born naked and blind) or relatively developed (like ducks, which can swim almost immediately). Many birds undergo incredible journeys in their lives — migratory birds like the Arctic Tern travel from the Arctic to the Antarctic and back — a journey of 70,000 km — every year of their lives.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Observe an insect or bird in your neighbourhood for at least 3 different days. Can you identify what stage of its life cycle it is in? Can you find evidence of other stages (egg cases, cocoons, baby birds in a nest)? Record all evidence of life cycles you can find.

Week 18–19

🌸 Plant Life Cycles — Seeds to Seeds

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B3.1 – Life cycles of plants; B2.2 – Investigate plant growth
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

A seed found in the Egyptian tomb of Tutankhamun — over 3,000 years old — was planted in 2012. It germinated. It grew into a plant. A seed that had been 'sleeping' inside a sealed tomb for three millennia woke up when given water and light. Seeds are one of the most remarkable survival mechanisms in the natural world. They hold complete genetic blueprints, enough stored energy to begin life, and can wait for the right conditions — sometimes for centuries.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand key concepts for this lesson
  • Practice scientific investigation and observation
  • Connect learning to the real world and daily life
  • Record findings and reflect on new questions
  • Build on previous lessons in the strand

Parent Teaching Guide

How to Teach This Lesson

Materials needed: Pencil, journal, magnifying glass, outdoor space if possible

Read the Big Lesson aloud together, stopping to discuss each bold concept. Use the real-world examples to ground abstract ideas. Do the classwork activities over 2-3 days. Check your plant experiment progress throughout this lesson.

Parent tip: The most important thing is curiosity — if your child asks a question you can't answer, celebrate it! Say "That's a great question — let's find out together."
📖

The Big Lesson

The Plant Life Cycle

Most plants go through these stages: seed → germination → seedling → mature plant → flower → pollination → fruit/seed formation → seed dispersal. Then the cycle begins again.

Germination: When a seed gets enough water, warmth, and oxygen, it breaks out of its seed coat and begins to grow. The root goes down first (always!), then the shoot grows up. The seed contains enough stored energy (as starch) to get the plant started before it can photosynthesize.

Flowering and Pollination: Most flowering plants need to transfer pollen from one flower to another (or from one flower's stamen to its pistil) to create seeds. This is done by insects (bees, butterflies, beetles), birds (hummingbirds, sunbirds), wind, or water.

The Bee-Flower partnership: Bees need nectar and pollen for food. Flowers need bees to carry their pollen. So flowers evolved to attract bees: bright colours, sweet scents, landing platforms. And bees evolved to collect pollen efficiently: fuzzy bodies that pollen sticks to, specialized 'pollen baskets' on their legs, a dance language to communicate where flowers are. This mutual benefit is called mutualism — one of the most beautiful relationships in nature.

Seed Dispersal — Getting Away From Mom

A plant cannot move, so its seeds must travel. Plants have evolved extraordinary seed dispersal strategies:

  • Wind: Dandelion seeds have parachutes, maple seeds have helicopter wings (samaras), cottonwood seeds are wrapped in cottony fluff
  • Animals (external): Burdock and cleavers seeds have hooks that cling to fur or clothing (how Velcro was invented!)
  • Animals (internal): Fruits are delicious so animals eat them and deposit seeds (in their droppings) far away
  • Ballistic dispersal: Witch-hazel pods explode, shooting seeds up to 15 metres away
  • Water: Coconuts can float for months and germinate on distant beaches
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Plant 5 different kinds of seeds (bean, sunflower, radish, grass, any herbs). Measure them every day. Which germinates first? Which grows fastest? Draw their growth over 2 weeks. Why do different seeds grow at different speeds?

Day 2: Try to figure out how each seed travels (wind, animal, explosion?). Day 3: Write a story from a seed's perspective — from being on the parent plant to germinating somewhere new.

Week 19–20

🕸️ Living Things Depend on Each Other

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B3.3 – Interdependence of living things
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In 1967, biologist Robert Paine removed a single species from a tidal pool on the Washington coast — the purple sea star (a predator that eats mussels). Within 18 months, the number of species in the tidal pool dropped from 15 to 8. Mussels took over every surface, crowding out all other species. One predator was keeping the entire community in balance. Paine coined the term 'keystone species' for an organism whose removal causes an ecosystem to collapse. Like the keystone in an arch, the whole structure depends on it.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand key concepts for this lesson
  • Practice scientific investigation and observation
  • Connect learning to the real world and daily life
  • Record findings and reflect on new questions
  • Build on previous lessons in the strand
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

How to Teach This Lesson

Materials needed: Pencil, journal, magnifying glass, outdoor space if possible

Read the Big Lesson aloud together, stopping to discuss each bold concept. Use the real-world examples to ground abstract ideas. Do the classwork activities over 2-3 days. Check your plant experiment progress throughout this lesson.

Parent tip: The most important thing is curiosity — if your child asks a question you can't answer, celebrate it! Say "That's a great question — let's find out together."
📖

The Big Lesson

The Web of Life

No living thing exists in isolation. Every organism — plant, animal, fungus, or bacterium — affects and is affected by the other organisms around it. These interactions are so numerous and complex that scientists spend entire careers studying just a few of them.

Some key relationships:

Predation: One organism eats another. This controls population sizes and drives evolution (prey evolves to escape; predators evolve to catch).

Competition: Two organisms compete for the same resource (food, space, water, light). Competition drives specialization — organisms evolve to use different resources so they don't compete as directly.

Mutualism: Both organisms benefit. Examples: bees and flowers (both need each other), clownfish and sea anemones (fish is protected by anemone stings, anemone is cleaned and fed by fish), human gut bacteria (bacteria get food, we get vitamins and immune support).

Commensalism: One benefits, the other is unaffected. Example: barnacles growing on whales — the barnacles get transportation to new food sources; the whale is neither helped nor harmed.

Parasitism: One benefits at the expense of the other. Ticks, fleas, and tapeworms are parasites. Some parasites are spectacularly sophisticated — there is a fungus that infects ants, takes control of their brains, makes them climb to a specific height on a plant, and then makes the ant clamp onto a leaf with its mandibles before killing it — using the ant's body as a place to release its spores.

You have about 39 trillion bacteria living in and on your body. Most of them are essential to your health. The bacteria in your gut help you digest food, produce vitamins, train your immune system, and may even affect your mood. You are not just one organism — you are an ecosystem.

Ecological Cascade Effects

When humans change ecosystems — by removing predators, introducing invasive species, or changing the climate — the effects cascade through the entire system in ways that are often hard to predict. The reintroduction of wolves to Yellowstone (mentioned in the Wonder Hook) changed rivers. The extinction of the dodo in Mauritius caused specific trees to stop reproducing, because the dodo was the only animal that ate and spread their seeds. These cascade effects show how deeply interconnected living things truly are.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Map the interdependencies in your backyard or neighbourhood. Choose one organism (a tree, a flower, a patch of grass) and think about: Who eats it? Who lives on it? Who pollinates it? Who disperses its seeds? Who competes with it? Draw a web connecting all these relationships.

Week Project

How are people trying to control it?

Week 20–21

🍂 Seasons and Living Things

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B3.2 – How living things respond to seasonal changes
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Every autumn in the Canadian city of Ottawa, something magical happens. Hundreds of thousands of trees simultaneously change colour — turning red, orange, gold, and purple — and then, over a few weeks, drop every single one of their leaves. A tree that might have 200,000 leaves in summer stands completely bare by November. This isn't just beautiful — it's one of the most brilliant survival strategies in the biological world. The autumn leaf drop is a tree preparing for winter as carefully as any animal migrating or hibernating.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand key concepts for this lesson
  • Practice scientific investigation and observation
  • Connect learning to the real world and daily life
  • Record findings and reflect on new questions
  • Build on previous lessons in the strand
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

How to Teach This Lesson

Materials needed: Pencil, journal, magnifying glass, outdoor space if possible

Read the Big Lesson aloud together, stopping to discuss each bold concept. Use the real-world examples to ground abstract ideas. Do the classwork activities over 2-3 days. Check your plant experiment progress throughout this lesson.

Parent tip: The most important thing is curiosity — if your child asks a question you can't answer, celebrate it! Say "That's a great question — let's find out together."
📖

The Big Lesson

Life Adapts to Seasons

The four seasons of the year are not just changes in weather. They are changes in the availability of light, food, water (frozen vs liquid), and temperature — all of which profoundly affect every living thing.

Living things have evolved four main strategies for surviving winter in Ontario:

Migration: Flying or travelling to warmer places where food is available. Monarch butterflies fly up to 4,000 km to Mexico. Canada Geese fly to the southern US or northern Mexico. Ruby-throated hummingbirds fly all the way to Central America. Many birds return to Canada in spring to breed, taking advantage of the explosion of insects and plants in the northern summer.

Hibernation: Entering a dormant state, dramatically slowing metabolism to survive without food. True hibernators include groundhogs, bats, and some bears (though bear hibernation is less extreme than true hibernation). Body temperature drops, heart rate slows dramatically, and stored fat is burned slowly over months.

Adaptation: Staying active all winter but changing behaviour or appearance. Snowshoe hares turn from brown to white in winter (camouflage against snow). Canadian winters are full of chickadees, nuthatches, and woodpeckers — birds that stay active, caching (hiding) food in autumn and finding it in winter.

Dormancy (for plants): Deciduous trees drop their leaves to prevent water loss (they can't get water from frozen soil) and protect their vascular system from frost damage. The brilliant autumn colours are actually revealed as the green chlorophyll breaks down, showing the yellow and orange pigments that were always present but hidden. The red and purple pigments are newly made in autumn.) Perennial plants die back above ground, but their roots survive underground.

The incredible monarch migration: Monarch butterflies travel 4,500 km from Canada to specific mountains in Mexico — a journey no individual butterfly has ever made before. The butterflies that return in spring are the great-great-grandchildren of those that left. How they navigate this long journey to a place they've never been is still not fully understood by scientists.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Keep a seasonal change journal for 3 weeks. Each week, record: What is the temperature? What are the trees doing? What animals have you seen? What sounds do you hear? What plants are in bloom or dormant? At the end of 3 weeks, describe every change you noticed.

Day 3: Design your own 'seasonal survival strategy' — if you had to survive a Canadian winter in the wild with no technology, what would you do?

Week 21–22

🌍 Humans and the Natural World

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B3.3 – Human impacts; B2.1 – Investigating environmental connections
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In just the last 50 years, human activities have caused wildlife populations to decline by an average of 69%. That means that on average, there are about one-third as many wild animals on Earth today as there were when your grandparents were children. This is the largest biological shift in millions of years. But here's the equally important news: there are success stories. The bald eagle was nearly extinct in 1963 — today there are hundreds of thousands. The humpback whale has recovered significantly. When humans decide to protect nature, it can recover. The question is whether we will decide to.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand key concepts for this lesson
  • Practice scientific investigation and observation
  • Connect learning to the real world and daily life
  • Record findings and reflect on new questions
  • Build on previous lessons in the strand
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

How to Teach This Lesson

Materials needed: Pencil, journal, magnifying glass, outdoor space if possible

Read the Big Lesson aloud together, stopping to discuss each bold concept. Use the real-world examples to ground abstract ideas. Do the classwork activities over 2-3 days. Check your plant experiment progress throughout this lesson.

Parent tip: The most important thing is curiosity — if your child asks a question you can't answer, celebrate it! Say "That's a great question — let's find out together."
📖

The Big Lesson

Human Impact — The Double Edge

Humans are the most powerful species on Earth. We can redirect rivers, move mountains, and change the atmosphere. This power makes us capable of causing tremendous damage to natural systems — but also capable of restoring and protecting them.

Negative impacts include: Habitat destruction (forests cut for farms and cities), pollution (plastic in oceans, chemicals in water and air), overhunting and overfishing, introducing invasive species, and climate change (adding greenhouse gases that warm the planet).

Positive impacts include: Protected areas (national parks, wildlife reserves), species recovery programs, habitat restoration (planting forests, restoring wetlands), reducing pollution, and sustainable farming and fishing practices.

Indigenous Stewardship — A Long Tradition

For thousands of years before European arrival, Indigenous peoples managed the landscapes of Canada in ways that maintained their richness. Controlled burning of prairies encouraged new grass growth and attracted bison. Selective fishing of salmon maintained healthy river ecosystems. Forest gardens maintained diverse food sources without destroying habitat. This traditional ecological knowledge is now recognized as scientifically sophisticated and is being integrated with modern conservation science.

The Great Pacific Garbage Patch is a collection of plastic debris in the North Pacific Ocean covering an area twice the size of Texas. Most of it is microplastic — tiny pieces that enter the food chain when fish and sea birds eat them. A single water bottle you use today could contribute to this patch for 450 years.

What Can One Person Do?

Individual actions matter. Reducing waste, choosing sustainable products, planting native plants, reducing energy use, supporting conservation organizations, and voting for environmental protection when you're old enough are all real impacts.

But collective action matters more. The laws that protect endangered species, that regulate pollution, and that establish national parks were won by groups of people who cared enough to organize and advocate. The most powerful thing any individual can do is help others understand why nature matters.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Survey your house for the environmental impact of your daily life. Make a list: How much water does your family use per day? How much electricity? How much plastic waste? What food comes from far away? Then choose one thing you could change to reduce your impact. Make a plan and try it for one week.

Week 22–23

🔬 Life Systems Investigation — Your Own Scientific Inquiry

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B2.1 – Plan and conduct investigations; B2.2 – Collect and record data
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Now you know about living things, their needs, their habitats, their life cycles, and how they depend on each other. You are no longer just a learner — you are equipped to ask your own scientific questions about life and investigate them.

This is the most important lesson of Strand B — not because of what you'll learn from a text, but because of what you'll discover through your own investigation.

The greatest biologists in history didn't just read about nature. They went outside and looked. Darwin spent years observing birds and turtles and noting tiny differences. Jane Goodall spent decades living among chimpanzees. Beatrix Potter (before writing her famous books) was a serious naturalist who made detailed scientific drawings of fungi. Your own backyard, park, or windowsill is your laboratory.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Design and carry out a complete biological investigation
  • Apply the scientific method to a life science question
  • Collect, record, and analyse biological data
  • Draw conclusions supported by evidence
  • Communicate findings in a clear, organized report
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Supporting the Investigation (Multiple Days)

This lesson is your child's opportunity for genuine scientific autonomy. Your role is to support and facilitate, not to direct. Help them choose a question that is:

  • Genuinely interesting to them
  • Realistic to investigate with available materials
  • Clearly testable with a measurable result

Good investigation ideas: Do worms prefer light or dark soil? Do different plants in our yard attract different insects? Does music affect plant growth? Do birds prefer certain colours of seeds?

Parent note: Let your child make mistakes and learn from them. If the first experiment doesn't work, discuss why and try a different approach. The process matters more than the result.
📖

The Big Lesson

Your Own Investigation

Here is your challenge for this lesson: design and carry out a real biological investigation. You will go through all the steps of the scientific method:

  1. Choose a question about a living thing that you genuinely want to answer
  2. Research: What do you already know about this topic?
  3. Write a hypothesis: What do you predict will happen and why?
  4. Design a fair test: What will you change? What will you measure? What will you keep the same?
  5. Gather materials and carry out the experiment over 3-5 days minimum
  6. Record data carefully every day (measurements, drawings, observations)
  7. Analyse your data: What patterns do you see?
  8. Draw a conclusion: What did you find out? Did it match your hypothesis?
  9. Communicate: Prepare a report or presentation to share your findings

Choosing Your Question

Your question should be about a living thing and should be testable. Here are some ideas to get you thinking:

  • Does the colour of light affect how plants grow?
  • Do caterpillars prefer certain leaves to eat?
  • Do earthworms move toward moisture or away from it?
  • Do birds visit a bird feeder more in the morning or afternoon?
  • Does soil type affect how fast seeds germinate?
  • Do bees spend more time on certain coloured flowers?

Or think of your own! The best investigation question comes from something you've genuinely wondered about.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Plan your investigation here. Answer all of these questions before you begin:

  • What is your question?
  • What do you already know about this topic?
  • What is your hypothesis?
  • What will you change (independent variable)?
  • What will you measure (dependent variable)?
  • What will you keep the same (controlled variables)?
  • What materials do you need?
  • How many days will your investigation run?
  • How will you record your data?

  7. Results (data table or graph)

Week 23–24

🎓 Life Systems Showcase — Everything I Know

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — B1.1 – B3.3 – All Strand B expectations reviewed and demonstrated
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

You have come an enormous distance since Lesson B1. You started by asking 'What is alive?' — a question philosophers have debated for millennia. Now you know about the 7 characteristics of life, animal and plant needs, habitats, food webs, life cycles, interdependence, seasons, and human impacts on the natural world.

You have not just learned facts. You have learned a way of looking at the living world. Now, wherever you go, you will notice things you would never have seen before: the insect on a flower and its role in pollination, the food chain connecting the bird to the worm to the soil, the changing colours of leaves telling you about the season, the animals adapting to survive winter. Science has given you new eyes.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Demonstrate understanding through creative communication

Strand B Celebration Day

The Big Lesson

Looking Back — What You've Learned

In Strand B, you have explored:

  • The 7 characteristics of life and what makes something truly 'alive'
  • How to observe living things in detail
  • The basic needs of animals (food, water, air, shelter) and how different animals meet them
  • The basic needs of plants (sunlight, water, CO₂, nutrients) and how photosynthesis works
  • Habitats — what they provide and why animals need specific habitats
  • How plants are adapted to different environments
  • Food chains and food webs — who eats whom and why this matters
  • Plant and animal life cycles
  • How living things depend on each other through mutualism, predation, and competition
  • How living things change with the seasons
  • How humans impact natural systems — and what we can do about it

You also carried out your own scientific investigation, following the full scientific method from question to conclusion.

Looking Forward — How This Connects

In Strand C, you will explore energy. Life and energy are inseparable — living things are powered by energy (from the sun or from food), they transform energy, and they depend on energy flows through ecosystems. The food chains and food webs you learned about in Strand B are really energy pathways — how energy flows from the sun, through plants, through animals, through decomposers, and back to the soil.

Reference · All Year

📚 Vocabulary Dictionary

All key science words for Grade 1
📖

Science Word Bank

Learn these important science words. Read each word and its meaning. Can you use the word in your own sentence?

Living thing
Any organism with all 7 characteristics of life (MRSGREN)
MRSGREN
The 7 characteristics of living things: Movement, Respiration, Sensitivity, Growth, Reproduction, Excretion, Nutrition
Observation
Something you notice directly with your senses
Habitat
The natural environment where an organism lives and finds everything it needs
Basic needs
The things every organism must have to survive
Photosynthesis
The process by which plants make sugar using sunlight, water, and CO₂
Chlorophyll
The green chemical in leaves that absorbs sunlight for photosynthesis
Herbivore
An animal that eats only plants
Carnivore
An animal that eats only other animals
Omnivore
An animal that eats both plants and animals
Food chain
A sequence showing who eats whom in a habitat
Food web
All the food chains in an ecosystem connected together
Predator
An animal that hunts and eats other animals
Prey
An animal that is hunted and eaten by a predator
Life cycle
The stages of development that an organism goes through from birth to death
Metamorphosis
A dramatic change in body form during an organism's life cycle
Mutualism
A relationship where both organisms benefit from each other
Decomposer
An organism that breaks down dead material and returns nutrients to the soil
Adaptation
A feature of an organism that helps it survive in its habitat
Dormancy
A period of reduced activity that helps an organism survive difficult conditions

Strand C · Matter & Energy
Week 19–20 · Strand C Opens

⚡ What Is Energy? The Force That Makes Everything Move

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — C3.1 – Identify different forms of energy and describe their effects
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Right now, as you sit reading these words, something invisible is flowing through you. It is keeping your heart beating. It is keeping your brain thinking. It is keeping your eyes moving. Without it, you would stop — just like a toy car with no batteries.

This invisible something has a name: energy. And here is what makes energy one of the most extraordinary things in the universe — it cannot be created and it cannot be destroyed. The energy in your body right now was once inside the food you ate. Before that, it was inside the plants. Before that, it was inside sunlight. The energy has been travelling through the universe for billions of years, taking different forms, before it arrived inside you.

Mind-bending fact: The energy inside a single chocolate chip cookie could, if perfectly converted, power a city for a moment. The energy inside the atoms of your fingernail contains enough potential energy to level a building. Energy is everywhere — and in unimaginably large quantities. The reason we cannot use all of it is a different story entirely.

Today we begin one of the most important investigations in all of science: What is energy? Where does it come from? How does it change? And how do we use it wisely?

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Define energy as the ability to cause change or do work
  • Identify at least six forms of energy: light, heat, sound, kinetic (movement), electrical, and stored (potential)
  • Understand that energy cannot be created or destroyed — it only changes form
  • Give examples of energy in everyday life
  • Begin to understand why energy matters for living things and for our planet
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials Needed

  • A flashlight
  • A pot of water and a stove or kettle
  • A musical instrument or two pots to bang together
  • A toy car or rolling ball
  • A battery-powered toy
  • Paper and crayons for drawings
  • Optional: a simple pinwheel or fan

Setting the Stage (Day 1)

Before opening the lesson, do this together: turn off all the lights, close the curtains so the room is dim. Sit quietly for 30 seconds. Then say: 'Imagine everything with energy suddenly stopped. The lights, the heat, your heartbeat, even your thoughts. What would the world be like?' Let your child answer freely — even silly answers are valid.

Then turn the lights back on. Ask: 'What changed when the lights came on? Something gave the lights the ability to shine. That something is energy.'

Teaching the Forms of Energy (Days 1–2)

Go through each form with a real demonstration. For light, shine the flashlight in a dark corner. For heat, carefully touch the side of a warm kettle (supervise). For sound, bang the pots together. For kinetic energy, roll the toy car. For electrical energy, turn on the battery toy. For stored energy, hold a ball up high and ask: 'This ball isn't moving, but it has energy stored in it — what kind? We'll find out when I drop it.' Drop it.

The Big Idea (Day 2)

Explain: 'Energy is sneaky — it likes to change. When the ball was held up, it had stored energy. When it fell, that became movement energy. When it hit the floor, that became sound energy and a tiny bit of heat. The energy didn't disappear — it just changed!'

If your child is struggling with the concept, compare energy to water that can be in different containers — same water, different shape. Energy is the same 'stuff' in different forms.

Discussion Questions for Days 1–3

  1. What kind of energy does a campfire give off?
  2. What kind of energy is in a wound-up spring toy?
  3. Where does the energy in food come from, originally?
  4. Can you think of something that uses MORE than one kind of energy at the same time?
📖

The Big Lesson

What Is Energy?

Energy is the ability to cause change or to do work. That might sound simple, but it is one of the most powerful ideas in all of science. Everything that happens in the universe involves energy in some way.

The Six Great Forms of Energy

1. Light Energy — Light is a form of energy that travels in waves. It can travel through empty space (that is how the Sun's light reaches Earth). Light lets us see. Plants use light energy to make their food. Light can even make electricity — that is how solar panels work.

2. Heat Energy — Heat is energy that causes things to get warmer. When you rub your hands together, movement energy turns into heat energy. The Sun sends heat energy to Earth. Heat can travel through solid materials (touch the handle of a warm pot!) or through air (like from a heater).

3. Sound Energy — Sound is energy that travels through the air as waves of pressure. When you bang a drum, the drum shakes (vibrates) and those vibrations travel through the air to your ears. You cannot see sound waves, but you can sometimes feel them — put your hand on a speaker and you will feel the vibrations.

4. Kinetic Energy (Movement Energy) — Any object that is moving has kinetic energy. A running cheetah has kinetic energy. A rolling ball has kinetic energy. Even the tiny atoms vibrating inside a rock have kinetic energy. The faster something moves or the heavier it is, the more kinetic energy it has.

5. Electrical Energy — Electricity is the flow of tiny particles called electrons. It can power lights, computers, motors, and much more. Lightning is a huge discharge of electrical energy. Electricity can be made from many other forms of energy — from burning fuel, from moving water, from wind, and from sunlight.

6. Stored (Potential) Energy — Some things have energy that is waiting to be released. A stretched rubber band has potential energy. A ball at the top of a hill has potential energy. The food in your lunchbox has chemical potential energy, waiting to be released when your body digests it.

Amazing fact: Sound travels through air at about 343 metres per second — fast! But light travels at 300,000,000 metres per second — almost one million times faster. That is why you see lightning before you hear the thunder, even though both happen at the same moment.

The Law of Conservation of Energy

Scientists have discovered something extraordinary: energy can never be created or destroyed. It can only change from one form to another. This is called the Law of Conservation of Energy.

Think about a bonfire. The energy in the burning wood was stored chemical energy. When the wood burns, that energy transforms into light energy (flames) and heat energy. After the fire is out, the energy is still there — it is now spread through the air as heat and through the ash as less-concentrated chemical energy. Nothing was lost. Nothing was gained. Energy was just transformed.

Big question to ponder: If energy cannot be created, where did all the energy in the universe come from? Scientists believe all the energy in the universe was created at the Big Bang, about 13.8 billion years ago. Since then, it has just been transforming — from form to form to form — in an endless, magnificent dance.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Investigation: Energy Transformation Chain

Scientists think in chains of cause and effect. Let's trace an energy transformation chain.

Step 1: Start with a ball at the top of a ramp (use books to make a ramp). Ask: What kind of energy does it have?

Step 2: Let it roll down. What kind of energy does it have now?

Step 3: It hits the wall with a bang. What new kind of energy appeared?

Step 4: After it stops, slightly touch it — is it warmer than before? A tiny bit of heat was generated too.

You just traced energy going from potential → kinetic → sound + heat. That is three transformations from one simple ball roll!
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Energy Hunt Journal

Walk through every room in your home together. In a notebook, write or draw every form of energy you can spot. Use this chart:

  • Room name | What I see | What kind of energy | How it helps us

Try to find at least 15 examples across the whole house.

Day 2: Energy Transformation Story

Draw a 6-panel comic strip that shows ONE form of energy transforming into TWO others. Example: A falling apple (potential energy) → hits the ground (kinetic energy + sound energy). Label each panel with the type of energy. Use colour!

Day 3: Energy Matching Game

Your parent will write 12 energy examples on cards. Sort them into six piles by energy type. Then describe to your parent WHY each one belongs in its pile.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Energy Diary

Every day for 5 days, keep an Energy Diary. Each day, record:

  1. One new example of energy you noticed today
  2. What form of energy it was
  3. What transformation happened (if any)
  4. Why that energy was useful or interesting
  5. One question it made you wonder about

At the end of the week, choose your most interesting entry and write a paragraph (4–6 sentences) explaining it. Illustrate it with a detailed drawing.

🏠

Homework Activities

Nightly Activities (choose one per night)

  • Energy in a Song: Listen to any song and identify all the energy forms involved in making that sound reach your ears.
  • Energy Transformation Chain: Make the longest possible energy transformation chain you can think of. Start with 'sunlight' and see how many steps you can add.
  • Interview a Gadget: Choose one object in your home (e.g., a toaster). Imagine it can talk. Write a short conversation asking it where its energy comes from and what it does with it.
  • Energy Around the World: Ask a parent to show you pictures of different countries. How do you think people in different places get their energy?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

These questions have no easy answers — think about them for days, weeks, even years:

  • If energy can never be destroyed, where will all Earth's energy eventually go?
  • The Sun will eventually run out of energy in about 5 billion years. Where will that energy go?
  • Could we ever build a machine that uses its own output energy to keep running forever? (Scientists call this a 'perpetual motion machine' — why is it actually impossible?)
  • Is thinking — using your brain — a form of energy use? How much energy does your brain use per day?
Week 20–21

☀️ The Amazing Sun — Our Star, Our Energy Source

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — C3.3 – Identify the Sun as a primary source of energy for the Earth
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Close your eyes and imagine: you are floating 150 million kilometres away from Earth. In front of you is the Sun — a sphere of superheated gas so enormous that one million Earths could fit inside it. At its core, the temperature is 15 million degrees Celsius.

Every single second, the Sun converts 600 million tonnes of hydrogen gas into helium gas. And in that conversion, a tiny bit of matter — about 4 million tonnes of it — turns directly into pure energy. That energy radiates outward as light and heat.

The astonishing thing: The energy leaving the Sun's surface right now began its journey at the Sun's core about 100,000 years ago. It took that long just to travel from the core to the surface! Then it took only 8 minutes and 20 seconds to cross 150 million kilometres of space and reach your eyes.

The Sun is the engine of almost all life on Earth. Every bite of food you eat, every breath of oxygen you take, ultimately traces back to the Sun. Today we find out why.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Identify the Sun as Earth's primary source of energy
  • Explain how the Sun's energy reaches Earth as light and heat
  • Understand how plants capture and store the Sun's energy (photosynthesis concept)
  • Explain how animals and humans get their energy from the Sun indirectly through food chains
  • Describe how the Sun drives weather, seasons, and the water cycle
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials

  • A magnifying glass (for a safe outdoor demonstration)
  • A black piece of paper and a white piece of paper
  • Two thermometers (or feel with hands)
  • A glass of water in sun vs. shade
  • Sunscreen for a short outdoor session

Opening Activity

Take your child outside on a sunny day. Ask them to put one hand in the shade and one in the sun. Ask: 'What do you feel? Where is that warmth coming from? It travelled 150 million kilometres to reach your hand. How long do you think that took?' (Answer: 8 minutes 20 seconds.)

Magnifying Glass Demo (Outdoor, Supervised)

Use a magnifying glass to focus sunlight on a black piece of paper. After 30–60 seconds, the paper will become visibly hot (do not let it catch fire). Explain: 'The magnifying glass is collecting light energy from a large area and concentrating it into a small spot. This shows how powerful the Sun's energy really is.'

The Energy Chain Discussion

Ask: 'You ate breakfast this morning. Where did that energy come from?' Guide them through the chain: food → plant → sunlight. Emphasize: 'Almost every bit of food energy on Earth originally came from the Sun.'

📖

The Big Lesson

The Sun: Our Energy Star

The Sun is a massive ball of gas — mostly hydrogen and helium — located 150 million kilometres from Earth. At its core, the pressure and temperature are so incredibly high that hydrogen atoms are crushed together to form helium atoms. This process is called nuclear fusion, and it releases enormous amounts of energy.

That energy travels outward through the Sun over about 100,000 years. When it finally reaches the Sun's surface, it shoots out into space as electromagnetic radiation — which includes visible light, heat (infrared radiation), and ultraviolet radiation (the kind that gives you a sunburn).

How the Sun's Energy Reaches Earth

The Sun's energy travels across space in waves. This is remarkable because space is mostly empty — there is no air, no water, no material of any kind. Yet light and heat can travel through it because they are waves of energy, not waves in a material.

When sunlight reaches Earth's atmosphere, some of it is reflected back into space by clouds. Some is absorbed by the atmosphere. And some reaches the ground, where it warms the land, the water, and the air.

Incredible scale: In one second, the Sun produces more energy than humanity has used in all of recorded history. The amount of sunlight that hits Earth in just one hour is more than enough to power all human civilization for an entire year — if we could collect and use it all.

Plants and the Sun's Energy

Plants are nature's solar panels. Through a process called photosynthesis, plants capture light energy from the Sun and use it to convert carbon dioxide (from the air) and water (from the soil) into sugar (their food) and oxygen (which they release).

This is one of the most important chemical reactions on Earth. Without photosynthesis, there would be no oxygen in our atmosphere, no food in the food chain, and no life as we know it.

The Sun Drives Everything

The Sun does not just feed living things. It drives the water cycle — it heats ocean water until it evaporates into clouds, which then rain back down. It drives weather — differences in temperature caused by uneven heating create wind. It drives seasons — because Earth is tilted on its axis, different parts get more or less direct sunlight at different times of year.

Deep connection: The coal, oil, and natural gas we burn for energy are made of ancient plants and animals. Those ancient organisms got their energy from the Sun. So when you turn on a light powered by electricity from a coal plant, you are releasing ancient sunlight that was stored underground for hundreds of millions of years.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Investigation: Which Colour Absorbs More Sunlight?

Scientists notice patterns and test them. Here is one to test:

Prediction: Which will get warmer — a black paper in sunlight or a white paper in sunlight?

Test: Place both papers in direct sunlight for 10 minutes. Then carefully feel each one. (Or use thermometers.) Record your results.

Explain: Dark colours absorb more light energy; light colours reflect more. This is why wearing dark clothing on a hot sunny day makes you hotter!

Why do houses in hot, sunny countries often have white walls and white roofs? How does this connect to what you just discovered?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Sun Energy Journey Map

Draw a large poster showing the journey of the Sun's energy. Start with a nuclear reaction in the Sun's core and trace the energy all the way to you eating breakfast. Include: Sun's core → Sun's surface → space → Earth's atmosphere → a plant → you eating the plant (or an animal that ate the plant).

Day 2: The Sun's Many Jobs

Make a 'Sun's Resume' — as if the Sun were applying for a job. List all the things the Sun does for Earth. Be creative! You can include: giving light, making weather, powering food chains, making shadows, causing seasons, keeping Earth warm. Try to find at least 10 jobs.

Day 3: Photosynthesis in Pictures

Draw a detailed diagram of a plant doing photosynthesis. Label: the roots (absorbing water), the leaves (absorbing sunlight and carbon dioxide), the 'factory' inside the leaf making sugar, and the oxygen being released. Add colour and labels.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Shadow Diary and Sun Position Study

Every day for 5 days, go outside at the same time and measure your shadow:

  1. Trace your shadow on paper (or measure it in footsteps)
  2. Record the direction your shadow is pointing
  3. Note the temperature: hot, warm, cool, cold
  4. Sketch where the Sun appears in the sky

At the end of 5 days, describe: Did your shadow change? Did the Sun move the same way every day? Write 5–8 sentences about your findings.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Sun Poem: Write a poem (at least 8 lines) about the Sun, describing what it does and why it matters. Use at least 4 scientific facts from today's lesson.
  • Ancient Humans and the Sun: Research (ask a parent to help you look up) how ancient people around the world worshipped the Sun. Why do you think they did?
  • Solar Cooking: Could you cook food using only sunlight? Research solar cookers — real devices that do exactly this. Draw one and explain how it works.
  • Sunset Observation: Watch the sunset one evening. Note the colours. What happens to the light as the Sun goes down? Why does the sky turn orange and red?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • If the Sun stopped shining right now, how long do you think life on Earth could survive? Would anything survive?
  • Some organisms on the ocean floor live near deep-sea vents and get their energy from hot rocks — not from the Sun at all. What does this tell us about life?
  • Scientists are trying to build 'fusion reactors' on Earth — machines that do what the Sun does. Why would this be so valuable?
  • The Sun is a medium-sized star. Some stars are 1,000 times bigger. What would happen to planets around those stars?
Week 21–22

💡 Light Energy — Seeing the Universe

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — C3.1 – Describe properties of light and how it interacts with materials
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

You are looking at this page. But how? Light bouncing off the page enters your eye, hits the back of your eyeball (the retina), and creates a tiny electrical signal. That signal travels along the optic nerve into your brain. Your brain — in a fraction of a second — assembles all those signals into a picture. You 'see' the page.

But here is the strange part: you don't actually see the page. You see light that has bounced off the page. If no light hit the page, your eye would receive no signal, and the page would be invisible — even though it is right there in front of you.

Light is so fast: Light travels at 300,000 kilometres per second. If you could travel at the speed of light, you could circle the entire Earth 7.5 times in just one second. You could go from London to Sydney in about 0.05 seconds. And yet, the nearest star to our Sun (Alpha Centauri) is so far away that even light takes 4.2 years to get here. We call that distance 4.2 light-years.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand that light travels in straight lines
  • Distinguish between transparent, translucent, and opaque materials
  • Understand how shadows are formed
  • Describe how light can be reflected and refracted
  • Understand the spectrum of visible light and how rainbows form
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials

  • A flashlight
  • Various materials: glass, wax paper, cardboard, plastic bag, tissue paper
  • A bowl of water
  • A white surface (paper or wall)
  • Optional: a prism or a CD (to make a rainbow)

Light and Materials Exploration
