Set up the flashlight in a dim room. Test each material by shining the light through it. Guide your child to sort them into: transparent (clear glass, see-through plastic), translucent (wax paper, tissue — some light passes but blurry), opaque (cardboard — no light passes). Ask: 'Can you see through it clearly? A little? Not at all?'

Making Rainbows

Fill a bowl with water and hold a CD in the sunlight at different angles until you see rainbow colours. Explain: White light is actually all colours mixed together. When light bends (refracts), the colours separate because each colour bends a slightly different amount.

📖

The Big Lesson

Light: Energy That Lets Us See

Light is a form of energy that travels as waves. Unlike sound waves, which need air to travel through, light can travel through completely empty space — that is how it reaches us from the Sun.

Light travels in straight lines. It cannot go around corners on its own (though it can be bounced around them with mirrors). This is why shadows are sharp-edged on one side.

Transparent, Translucent, and Opaque

Materials interact with light in three ways. Transparent materials let light pass through clearly — like glass or clean water. You can see through them perfectly. Translucent materials let some light through but scatter it — like frosted glass, wax paper, or your hand held up to a bright light (you can see the light but not what is on the other side). Opaque materials block all light — like wood, stone, or metal. They create shadows.

Shadows: The Shape of Light's Absence

A shadow forms when an opaque object blocks light. The shadow is the region where light cannot reach. Because light travels in straight lines, shadows have the same basic shape as the object that made them — just stretched, shrunk, or distorted depending on the angle of the light source.

Shadow science: On Earth, shadows have soft, blurry edges because the Sun is not a single point — it has some width. On the Moon, where there is no atmosphere and the Sun appears point-like, shadows have razor-sharp edges. Astronauts on the Moon found shadows confusing because they looked unnaturally sharp and dark.

Reflection

When light hits a surface and bounces back, we call it reflection. Smooth, shiny surfaces like mirrors reflect light very clearly — you can see a clear image. Rough surfaces scatter light in many directions, so you see a glow but no clear image.

Refraction and the Rainbow

When light passes from one material into another (like from air into water), it bends. This bending is called refraction. Refraction is what makes a straw look bent when you put it in a glass of water.

White light is actually a mixture of all colours — red, orange, yellow, green, blue, indigo, and violet. When white light refracts, each colour bends a slightly different amount, so they separate into the full spectrum of colour. This is exactly how a rainbow forms — sunlight is refracted inside millions of tiny raindrops.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Investigation: Shadow Size and Distance

Use a flashlight and your hand. Project a hand shadow on the wall.

Question: If you move your hand closer to the flashlight, does the shadow get bigger or smaller? Predict first, then test.

Explain: Light spreads outward in a cone. The closer the object is to the light source, the larger the cone of shadow it creates on the wall behind it. The further the object is, the smaller the shadow.

This is exactly how solar eclipses work. The Moon is much smaller than the Sun, but because it is much closer to Earth, it can cast a shadow big enough to block the Sun from certain spots on Earth. When will the next solar eclipse be visible from your part of the world?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Light Investigation Journal

Test 8 different materials from around your home with a flashlight. Sort them into transparent, translucent, or opaque. Draw each material and label it. Include one surprise result — something that was not what you expected.

Day 2: Shadow Art

Use a flashlight and objects to create three shadow art scenes on paper. Trace the shadows to make pictures — can you make a shadow that looks like an animal, a tree, or a building? Label what object made each shadow.

Day 3: Rainbow Investigation

Use a CD or bowl of water in sunlight to make a rainbow. Draw what you see using coloured pencils. Write the colours in order (ROYGBIV). Research: What is infrared light? What is ultraviolet light? Why can't humans see them?

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Light in My World Report

Investigate how light is used in 5 different contexts in your life:

  1. A window (what does it let in? why does it matter?)
  2. A lamp or light bulb (what form of light? how efficient?)
  3. A mirror (how does it reflect?)
  4. A shadow on the ground (what time of day? direction?)
  5. Colour (why do objects have colours — what is happening with light?)

Write a paragraph (4–6 sentences) for each of the 5 investigations. Illustrate two of them with detailed, labelled diagrams.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Pinhole Camera: Research how to make a pinhole camera from a cardboard box. Build one if possible. Explain how it shows that light travels in straight lines.
  • Colours of the Sky: Why is the sky blue during the day? Why is it red at sunset? Research and write an explanation in your own words.
  • Night-time Observation: Look at the Moon and stars tonight. The Moon is lit by reflected sunlight. Why can we see the Moon at night even though the Sun has set?
  • Bioluminescence: Some living things make their own light — fireflies, certain deep-sea fish, and some fungi. Research this and write about how you think they do it.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • We can only see a tiny part of the electromagnetic spectrum. What might the world look like if we could see X-rays, or radio waves, or gamma rays?
  • Scientists believe light is both a wave AND a particle (called a photon). How can something be two different things at once?
  • A black hole's gravity is so strong that not even light can escape it. So if light has no mass, how can gravity affect it?
  • Could you communicate using light? People have — with mirrors, with Morse code using flashes, with fibre-optic cables. What are the limits of using light for communication?
Week 22

🔥 Heat Energy — The Energy of Temperature

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — C3.1 – Identify and describe heat as a form of energy
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Put your hand on the table. Now put your hand on a metal spoon that has been sitting on the same table. The metal feels colder — but why? The table and the spoon are the same temperature (they have been in the same room for hours). The spoon isn't actually colder.

The answer reveals something deep about heat: Metal conducts heat away from your hand faster than wood does. Your hand loses its heat quickly to the metal, so your hand feels cold. Your body's temperature sense is measuring the rate of heat transfer, not the actual temperature of the object. Thermometers measure temperature — but your skin measures something more complex.

Heat is one of the most important and most misunderstood forms of energy. Today we investigate what it really is, how it travels, and why it matters.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Define heat as the energy of moving particles within a material
  • Distinguish between heat and temperature
  • Describe the three ways heat travels: conduction, convection, and radiation
  • Identify examples of each type of heat transfer in daily life
  • Explain how insulation keeps things warm or cold
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials

  • Metal spoon and wooden spoon
  • A cup of warm water (not boiling)
  • Ice cubes
  • A wool sweater and a plastic bag
  • Various materials to test as insulators: newspaper, bubble wrap, foil, cloth

Conduction Demo

Put both spoons in a cup of warm water (not boiling — just warm tap water). After 2 minutes, have your child touch the top of each spoon. The metal spoon will feel much warmer. Ask: 'Which one conducted (carried) heat better? Why might that matter — when would you want a good heat conductor? When would you want a bad one?'

Insulation Challenge

Wrap an ice cube in each of four materials: foil, newspaper, bubble wrap, cloth. Which keeps the ice from melting longest? This is the same principle that keeps your hot soup hot in a thermos and your cold drink cold in a cooler.

📖

The Big Lesson

What Is Heat?

Everything is made of tiny particles called atoms and molecules. These particles are always moving — vibrating, spinning, bouncing around. Heat is the energy of this particle movement. The faster the particles move, the more heat energy the material has, and the higher its temperature.

When you heat water on the stove, you are giving energy to the water molecules, making them move faster and faster until some of them move fast enough to escape as steam.

Heat vs. Temperature

These two things are often confused. Temperature is a measure of how fast the particles in a material are moving. Heat is the total energy of all the particles. A cup of boiling water (100°C) has a higher temperature than a swimming pool at 25°C. But the swimming pool contains far more total heat energy because it has billions and billions more water molecules.

How Heat Travels: Three Ways

Conduction is heat transfer through direct contact. When you touch something hot, heat conducts from it into your hand. Metals are excellent conductors (the particles are packed closely and pass energy quickly). Wood and plastic are poor conductors (we call them insulators).

Convection is heat transfer through fluids (liquids and gases). When you heat water in a pot, the hot water at the bottom becomes lighter and rises, while cooler water sinks. This creates a circular flow called a convection current. The same thing happens in the air — warm air rises, cool air sinks, creating wind.

Radiation is heat transfer through electromagnetic waves — the same kind of waves as light. The Sun heats Earth through radiation across 150 million kilometres of empty space. You feel the heat of a campfire even without touching it, because the fire radiates heat in all directions.

Body heat: Your body produces heat as a byproduct of all the chemical reactions keeping you alive. At rest, you generate roughly 80 watts of heat — about the same as an old-fashioned incandescent light bulb. When you exercise hard, you can generate 10 times that much!

Insulation

Insulators are materials that slow down the transfer of heat. A thermos uses a vacuum (empty space with no particles) between its walls, because heat cannot conduct or convect through a vacuum. Your winter coat traps air in fluffy fibres, and air is a poor heat conductor, so heat from your body stays near you instead of escaping into the cold air.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Investigation: The Best Insulator

Design your own investigation: Which material is the best insulator to keep an ice cube cold?

Hypothesis: Write your prediction and why.

Test: Wrap ice cubes in different materials. Check every 5 minutes and note which melts fastest.

Results: Make a table: Material | Melted by 5 min | 10 min | 15 min.

Conclusion: Which was best? Why? Was your hypothesis correct?

✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Heat Transfer Diagram

Draw three large diagrams — one for conduction, one for convection, one for radiation. In each diagram, show exactly how heat moves. Include arrows showing the direction of heat flow and label all parts.

Day 2: Insulator Investigation

Do the ice cube insulator investigation. Record your results in a data table and write a conclusion paragraph.

Day 3: Heat in My Home

Walk through your home and find one example of each: conduction, convection, radiation, and insulation. Write a description and draw each example.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Heat Engineering Project

Design and build a 'hand warmer' — a device that keeps your hands warm for as long as possible using only materials you have at home (no electricity). You may use: cloth, paper, foil, plastic bags, rubber bands, tape.

Document your design process: 1) Your initial design sketch 2) What materials you chose and why 3) How you tested it 4) What worked well 5) What you would change if you designed it again. Write a full design report, at least 1 page.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Convection in nature: Research how convection currents in the oceans (called thermohaline circulation) move heat around the entire planet. Draw a diagram of this 'ocean conveyor belt'.
  • Body temperature: Why does your body need to stay at about 37°C? What happens if it gets too hot or too cold? Research hypothermia and heat stroke.
  • Firewalking: Some people walk on hot coals without burning their feet. How is this possible? Research and explain the science.
  • Heat on other planets: Mercury is much closer to the Sun than Venus, but Venus is actually hotter. Research why. (Hint: it has to do with greenhouse gases.)
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • At absolute zero (-273.15°C), all particle movement would theoretically stop. Scientists have come very close — within billionths of a degree. What do you think happens to matter at that temperature?
  • Some fish live in Antarctic waters that are below 0°C — colder than the freezing point of water! How do they survive?
  • Heat pumps can move heat from cold places to warm places (your refrigerator does this). Is this breaking the laws of physics?
  • If you could see infrared radiation (heat radiation) the way your phone's night-vision camera can, what do you think the world would look like?
Week 23

🔊 Sound Energy — Vibrations That Tell Stories

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — C3.1 – Identify and describe sound as a form of energy produced by vibrations
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In 1977, two spacecraft called Voyager 1 and Voyager 2 were launched from Earth. By 2012, Voyager 1 had crossed into interstellar space — the space between the stars. If aliens found it, they would discover a golden record with sounds from Earth: the roar of the ocean, the songs of birds, music, and the voices of people from around the world.

But here is the thing: those sounds cannot be heard in space. Sound needs something to travel through. In the vacuum of space, there is nothing for sound waves to push against. The Voyager golden record carries the sounds but could never play them for anyone who happened to be in space nearby.

Think about this: When a massive star explodes as a supernova, releasing more energy than the Sun will in its entire lifetime, it makes no sound in space. The biggest explosion in the universe — and silence. Sound is something that only exists where there is matter to carry it.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand that sound is created by vibrations
  • Explain how sound travels as pressure waves through matter
  • Understand why sound cannot travel through a vacuum
  • Describe the properties of sound: pitch, volume (loudness)
  • Explore how different materials affect sound
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials

  • A ruler (hang over edge of table and twang it)
  • Rubber bands of different thicknesses
  • Two tin cans and a long piece of string
  • A bowl of water
  • Various materials to test sound absorption: carpet, foam, towels vs. hard surfaces

Vibration Demonstration

Twang the ruler on the edge of the table. Let your child see it vibrating. Then ask them to touch it — feel the vibration stop. The sound stops too. Explain: 'The sound stopped because the vibrations stopped. Sound IS vibrations.'

String Telephone

Make a string telephone with two tin cans and string. Talk into one end while your child listens from the other room. Explain: the vibrations travel along the string. Pinch the string in the middle — the sound disappears. The string carries the sound waves.

📖

The Big Lesson

Sound Is Vibration

Sound is created whenever something vibrates — moves rapidly back and forth. When you speak, your vocal cords vibrate. When a guitar string is plucked, it vibrates. When a drum is struck, the drum skin vibrates. These vibrations push the surrounding air molecules, which push other air molecules, creating a chain reaction of pressure waves spreading outward in all directions.

Sound Waves

Sound travels as waves of compressed and stretched air. Think of it like a 'slinky' — when you push one end, the compressed section travels along the slinky to the other end, even though the slinky itself doesn't move much. Sound waves work the same way: the air molecules don't travel with the sound, but the wave of compression and expansion does travel.

Pitch and Volume

Pitch is how high or low a sound is. It is determined by the frequency of vibration — how many times per second the object vibrates. High-frequency vibrations (many times per second) create high-pitched sounds. Low-frequency vibrations create low-pitched sounds. The rumble of thunder has a low pitch; a whistle has a high pitch.

Volume (or loudness) is how much energy the sound wave carries. A sound made by large, forceful vibrations will be louder than one made by small, gentle vibrations. We measure volume in decibels (dB). A whisper is about 30 dB. A rock concert can be 120 dB — which is loud enough to damage your hearing.

Sound speeds: Sound travels through air at about 343 metres per second. But sound travels much faster through water (1,480 m/s) and even faster through steel (5,100 m/s). Whales can communicate through ocean water over hundreds or even thousands of kilometres!

Sound Cannot Travel Through Vacuum

Sound needs matter to travel through — air, water, wood, metal, anything with particles that can bump into each other and pass the vibration along. In a vacuum (empty space with no particles), there is nothing to carry the vibration. This is why space is silent, and why submarines use sonar (sound in water) rather than radio waves to detect other objects underwater.

Echoes and Sound Absorption

When sound hits a hard, smooth surface, it reflects — we hear this as an echo. Mountains, caves, and empty rooms all create echoes. Soft materials like carpet, foam, and curtains absorb sound waves, preventing them from reflecting. This is why recording studios are lined with soft foam — to prevent echoes that would muddy the sound.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Investigation: Does Material Affect Sound?

Test how different surfaces affect sound. Tap a hard surface (table) and a soft surface (pillow) with the same force. Which one makes more sound? Which absorbs more?

Now put a phone playing music inside a box. Test with the box open, then covered with a towel, then wrapped in a jacket. Measure: does the sound seem louder, quieter, or the same? Why?

Concert halls are carefully designed so sound reaches every seat clearly and without harsh echoes. The shape of the ceiling, the angle of the walls, and the materials on the seats all matter. This field of science is called architectural acoustics. Can you figure out what makes a good concert hall?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Vibration Experiments Journal

Try at least 5 different ways to make sound using vibrations. For each one: describe what is vibrating, whether the pitch is high or low, whether it is loud or soft, and whether you can feel the vibration with your hands.

Day 2: Sound Drawing

Draw a large diagram showing how sound travels from a ringing bell to a person's ear. Show the air molecules, the waves of compression and expansion, the ear canal, and the eardrum. Label every part and use arrows to show the direction of energy travel.

Day 3: Design a Musical Instrument

Design and build a simple musical instrument using materials from home (rubber bands, bottles, combs, etc.). Play at least two different pitches on it. Write an explanation of how your instrument makes sound and how you change the pitch.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Sound Investigation Project

Investigate: How does the length of a vibrating object affect the pitch of the sound it makes?

Use rubber bands, ruler lengths, bottles with different water levels, or straw lengths. Test at least 5 different lengths. Record what you observe. Write a report including:

  1. Your question
  2. Your prediction
  3. Your method
  4. Your results (with a table)
  5. Your conclusion
  6. What surprised you
🏠

Homework Activities

  • Ear anatomy: Draw a diagram of the inside of the human ear. Label the eardrum, the three tiny bones (hammer, anvil, stirrup), and the cochlea. Write a sentence explaining what each part does.
  • Animal hearing: Which animal has the best hearing? Research ultrasonic and infrasonic hearing in animals — bats, elephants, dogs. What frequencies can they detect that we cannot?
  • Music therapy: Research how music can affect how people feel, or even help with healing. Why might sound have such a strong effect on emotions?
  • Sonic booms: What happens when a jet plane travels faster than the speed of sound? Research sonic booms and draw a diagram of the shock wave.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • If you were on the Moon with a friend two metres away, could you hear them shout?
  • Some people experience a condition called synesthesia, where they 'see' sounds as colours. What do you think this is like?
  • Scientists have used sound to create a tiny tornado in a glass of water, to levitate small objects, and even to disrupt cancer cells. What makes sound powerful enough to do these things?
  • Gravitational waves — ripples in the fabric of space itself — were detected for the first time in 2015. Are these 'sound waves'? How are they similar and how are they different?
Week 23–24

🏃 Movement Energy — Kinetic Energy in Action

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — C3.1 – Describe kinetic energy and how it relates to mass and speed
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

A bowling ball rolling at 5 km/h. A golf ball rolling at 5 km/h. Both are moving at the same speed. But if both hit your foot, which one hurts more?

Obviously the bowling ball. But why? Both balls have the same speed. The difference is mass — the bowling ball has much more mass, so it has much more kinetic energy.

Kinetic energy doubles when speed doubles — but quadruples when speed doubles. Wait, what? If a car doubles its speed (say, from 30 km/h to 60 km/h), it doesn't just double its kinetic energy — it quadruples it. This is why speeding is so much more dangerous than people think: going twice as fast means four times the energy in a crash. Physicists discovered this relationship about 300 years ago, and it changed how we design everything from cars to safety helmets.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Define kinetic energy as the energy of motion
  • Understand that kinetic energy depends on both mass and speed
  • Identify how kinetic energy transforms into other forms
  • Explore how simple machines use kinetic energy
  • Investigate how friction affects moving objects
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials

  • Toy cars or balls of different sizes and weights
  • A ramp (use books and a ruler or piece of cardboard)
  • A ruler for measuring distances
  • Sandpaper, carpet, wood — surfaces of different roughness
  • Optional: a pendulum (tie a weight to a string)

Ramp Investigations

Set up a ramp at a fixed angle. Roll the same ball from three different heights. Measure how far it rolls on the flat surface after leaving the ramp. Ask: 'Which height gave the ball more kinetic energy? How do you know?'

Then roll the same ball from the same height on three different surfaces (smooth floor, carpet, sandpaper). Ask: 'What happened to the kinetic energy on the rough surfaces? Where did it go?'

📖

The Big Lesson

Kinetic Energy: The Energy of Moving Things

Any object that is moving has kinetic energy. This includes obvious things like a rolling ball or a flying bird, but also less obvious things — like the air molecules vibrating in a sound wave, the electrons flowing through a wire, and even you breathing.

What Determines How Much Kinetic Energy?

Two things determine the kinetic energy of a moving object: its mass (how much matter it contains) and its speed (how fast it is moving). Specifically, kinetic energy = half the mass × the speed squared (KE = ½mv²). For Grade 1, the key ideas are: heavier things have more kinetic energy at the same speed, and faster things have much more kinetic energy than slower things.

Kinetic Energy Transformations

Kinetic energy loves to transform into other kinds of energy. When a ball rolls along the floor, friction transforms some kinetic energy into heat. When a ball hits a wall, kinetic energy becomes sound. When a turbine in a river is turned by flowing water, kinetic energy becomes electrical energy.

Car crash physics: In a car crash at 60 km/h, the car has four times more kinetic energy than at 30 km/h (because kinetic energy grows with speed squared). This means the crash is four times more violent. This is the physics behind speed limits on roads — they are not arbitrary; they are based on the mathematics of kinetic energy.

Friction: The Slowing Force

Friction is a force that opposes motion. When surfaces rub together, they create friction, which converts kinetic energy into heat. This is why your hands get warm when you rub them together. Friction allows you to walk without sliding, but it also slows down machines and makes them less efficient. Engineers are always looking for ways to reduce unwanted friction — that is why machines are oiled and wheels have ball bearings.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Investigation: How Does Height Affect Speed?

Roll a ball down a ramp from three different heights. After each roll, place a paper marker where the ball stops on the flat surface.

Measure: How far did the ball roll from the bottom of the ramp? Record in a table.

Reason: The greater the height, the more potential energy the ball starts with. As it rolls down, that potential energy converts to kinetic energy. More kinetic energy means more speed, which means the ball rolls farther before friction stops it.

✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Kinetic Energy Lab Report

Conduct the ramp investigation. Write a full lab report: question, materials, method, results table, graph (draw it!), conclusion.

Day 2: Kinetic Energy in Sports

Choose three different sports. For each one, identify: What is moving? What gives it kinetic energy? How does the kinetic energy transform when the object stops? Which sport involves the most kinetic energy — and how do you know?

Day 3: Roller Coaster Design

Design a roller coaster on paper. Show hills, valleys, loops. Label where kinetic energy is greatest and where potential energy is greatest. Explain why a roller coaster starts with the biggest hill first.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Vehicle Safety Engineering Report

Research how cars are designed to protect people in crashes by managing kinetic energy:

  1. What do crumple zones do? (Research and draw a diagram)
  2. How do seatbelts use kinetic energy science? (Explain in detail)
  3. Why are airbags useful?
  4. How are helmets designed to protect the brain?
  5. Design your own safety innovation for a bicycle or skateboard — explain the physics behind it

Write a full report (at least 1.5 pages) with illustrations for each safety feature.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Newton's Cradle: Research Newton's Cradle (the clicking ball desk toy). Explain how it demonstrates the conservation of kinetic energy.
  • Kinetic energy in nature: How do predators use kinetic energy in the hunt? Research how a cheetah, a falcon, and a barracuda use speed and mass to catch prey.
  • Regenerative braking: Electric cars recover kinetic energy when they brake. Research how this works and why it is efficient.
  • Wind energy: Wind is moving air — it has kinetic energy. How does a wind turbine convert that kinetic energy to electricity? Draw and label a diagram.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • If you could jump from a 10-metre diving board, how much kinetic energy would you have when you hit the water? How does that compare to the kinetic energy of a thrown baseball?
  • Some animals — like the mantis shrimp — can strike with such speed that the water around the strike briefly becomes as hot as the surface of the Sun. How is this possible?
  • Could a spacecraft be propelled just by the kinetic energy of sunlight particles (photons) hitting a 'solar sail'? Actually, yes — this technology exists. Research solar sails.
  • In a perfect vacuum with no friction, would a moving object ever slow down? What does this imply for objects in space?
Week 24

🏠 Energy at Home — Where Does Our Power Come From?

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — C3.2 – Identify common energy sources and how they are used at home
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Imagine you wake up tomorrow morning and nothing that uses energy works. No electricity. No natural gas. No gasoline. Think about what your morning would look like: you cannot turn on a light, you cannot heat water for a shower, you cannot cook breakfast, you cannot use any device.

How did people live 200 years ago? They used candles for light, wood fires for heat and cooking, and horses for transportation. The entire industrial revolution was about finding better, more powerful energy sources.

Energy use explosion: In 1800, the average person used roughly the same amount of energy as a strong horse can provide in a day. Today, the average North American uses the equivalent of having about 80 'energy servants' working for them around the clock — 80 horse-power-equivalents at their disposal 24/7. That energy runs our entire modern way of life.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Identify different energy sources used in homes: electricity, natural gas, oil, solar, wood
  • Trace where home electricity comes from (power plant → wires → home)
  • Understand the concept of renewable vs. non-renewable energy sources
  • Identify major energy uses in the home: heating, cooling, appliances, lighting, transportation
  • Begin to understand the environmental impact of energy choices
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Home Energy Audit

Before starting this lesson, do a walk-through of your home with your child and list every device, appliance, or system that uses energy. Include: lights, refrigerator, stove, furnace, hot water heater, washer/dryer, TV, computer, phone charger, car. This audit will become the basis of classwork and assignment.

Electricity Origin Exploration

Ask: 'When you plug something in and turn it on, where does the electricity come from?' Trace the path together: appliance → outlet → house wiring → electricity meter → transformer → power lines → power plant → fuel source. Help your child understand that electricity doesn't come from the outlet — it comes from somewhere, and that somewhere has consequences.

📖

The Big Lesson

Energy Sources We Use at Home

Our homes use energy in many forms, but most of it comes from one of two main types: electricity and combustion fuels (things that burn).

Electricity is generated at power plants and sent to homes through a network of power lines and transformers. In Ontario, electricity comes from several sources: nuclear power (using the energy inside atoms), hydroelectric power (using the kinetic energy of falling water), natural gas plants (burning gas), wind turbines, and solar panels.

Natural gas is a fuel burned directly in many homes for heating, hot water, and cooking. It is delivered through underground pipes.

Renewable vs. Non-Renewable

Some energy sources will eventually run out — we call them non-renewable. Coal, oil, and natural gas are non-renewable because they take millions of years to form and we are using them much faster than they form.

Other energy sources are renewable — they replenish naturally and will not run out. Sunlight, wind, water flow, and the Earth's internal heat (geothermal energy) are all renewable.

Ontario's grid: Ontario's electricity is remarkably clean compared to many places in the world. About 96% of Ontario's electricity comes from sources that produce no greenhouse gases: nuclear power, hydroelectric, wind, and solar. This is one of the reasons electric cars in Ontario are especially environmentally friendly.

What Uses the Most Energy at Home?

In a typical home, heating and cooling the building uses the most energy (about 50–60%). Hot water heating is next (about 20%). Appliances (refrigerator, washer, dryer) use about 15%. Lighting uses about 5% (and much less with efficient LED bulbs). Electronics and other devices make up the rest.

Energy and the Environment

When we burn fossil fuels (coal, oil, natural gas), we release carbon dioxide (CO₂) into the atmosphere. CO₂ traps heat in the atmosphere, causing Earth's temperature to rise — this is called the greenhouse effect. Burning fossil fuels also releases other pollutants that can harm air quality. This is why choosing renewable energy sources and using energy efficiently is so important.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Home Energy Audit Analysis

Using your home energy audit list, categorize each item:

  • What type of energy does it use? (electricity, natural gas, etc.)
  • Is it essential or optional?
  • Could we use less of it? How?
  • What would happen if we didn't have it?
Which item on your list do you think uses the most energy? Research or estimate how much. For example: a typical clothes dryer uses about 5,000 watts when running. If you run it 1 hour per day, that is 5 kWh per day, about 1,825 kWh per year.
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Energy Sources Poster

Create a large poster showing at least 6 different energy sources. For each one: draw an illustration, label it as renewable or non-renewable, explain how it makes electricity or heat, and describe one advantage and one disadvantage.

Day 2: Home Energy Map

Draw a floor plan of your home. Mark every room. In each room, draw and label every energy-using device you can find. Use colour coding: red for heating/cooling, blue for lights, green for appliances, yellow for electronics.

Day 3: Energy Bill Analysis

Ask a parent to show you last month's energy bill (electricity and/or gas). With their help, read it together: How many kilowatt-hours did you use? How much did it cost per kWh? How does your home's usage compare to an average home?

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Family Energy Plan

Work with your family to create a 5-day energy reduction plan. Each day, try ONE new way to use less energy:

  1. Turn off lights in every empty room for the entire day
  2. Take shorter showers or lower the water heater temperature
  3. Hang laundry to dry instead of using the dryer
  4. Keep windows closed when heating/cooling is on; wear a sweater instead of raising the heat
  5. Unplug devices when not in use ('vampire energy' drain)

Keep a diary. Write what you did each day, whether it was hard or easy, and any changes you noticed. At the end, estimate how much energy you might have saved.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Nuclear power: Ontario uses a lot of nuclear power. Research how a nuclear reactor works. Is it safe? What are the arguments for and against it?
  • Hydroelectric: Niagara Falls generates electricity. Research how. What are the environmental impacts of dams?
  • Energy poverty: About 800 million people on Earth have no access to electricity. Research how this affects their lives. What are some solutions being developed?
  • Smart grid: Research what a 'smart grid' is. How do smart thermostats and smart meters help us use energy more efficiently?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • If every home in Canada installed solar panels, could we generate all our electricity from the Sun alone? What are the obstacles?
  • Some scientists propose building a solar power satellite that would collect solar energy in space and beam it to Earth as microwaves. Would this work? What are the challenges?
  • Energy storage is one of the biggest challenges in moving to renewable energy. What are the most promising battery technologies of the future?
  • In a future powered entirely by renewable energy, what would you no longer be able to do that you can do today? What new things might become possible?
Week 25

⚡ Electricity — The Flow That Powers the World

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — C3.2 – Describe how electricity is generated and used
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

You are surrounded by electricity right now. The electrical signals in your brain are what make you think these words. The tiny sparks between nerve cells in your body control every heartbeat, every breath, every movement.

But electricity is also one of the strangest forces in nature. Electrons — the tiny particles that carry electric charge — move through a wire so slowly that a single electron in your household wiring might take hours to travel from the wall outlet to a lamp. Yet the effect of their movement reaches the lamp instantly. How?

The garden hose analogy: Imagine a garden hose already full of water. When you turn on the tap at one end, water comes out the other end almost instantly — even though the same water molecules didn't travel that fast. It is the 'pressure wave' that travels instantly. Electricity is similar: the electrons are already in the wire, and turning on a switch creates a pressure wave that causes electrons everywhere in the circuit to begin moving almost simultaneously.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand that electricity is the flow of electrons through a conductor
  • Learn the basic components of a circuit: battery, wires, switch, and load (e.g. bulb)
  • Distinguish between conductors and insulators
  • Understand static electricity as the build-up of charge
  • Learn basic electrical safety rules
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials

  • A simple circuit kit (battery holder, bulb, wires with clips) — available inexpensively at dollar stores or online
  • OR: use a 9V battery, a small LED bulb, and two pieces of wire with alligator clips
  • Balloon (for static electricity demo)
  • Various materials to test as conductors/insulators: coins, pencil graphite, rubber eraser, plastic, aluminium foil

Simple Circuit Demo

Build the simplest possible circuit together: battery → wire → bulb → wire back to battery. Watch the bulb light up. Ask: 'What happens if I disconnect one wire?' (Bulb goes out.) 'Why? The electrons need a complete path to travel around.'

Static Electricity Demo

Rub a balloon on hair, then hold it near a thin stream of water from a tap — the stream bends. Or stick the balloon to the wall. Ask: 'What force is making these things happen?'

📖

The Big Lesson

What Is Electricity?

Electricity is the flow of tiny particles called electrons. Electrons are part of every atom. In some materials — called conductors — electrons are loosely held and can flow freely from atom to atom. Metals like copper, silver, and gold are excellent conductors. In other materials — called insulators — electrons are tightly held and cannot flow. Rubber, plastic, glass, and wood are insulators. This is why electrical wires are made of copper (conductor) coated in plastic (insulator).

Electric Circuits

For electricity to flow, electrons need a complete, unbroken path to travel along — called a circuit. A basic circuit has: a power source (like a battery), a conductor (wire), and a load (the device using the electricity, like a bulb). The circuit must be complete — a break anywhere stops the flow. A switch is simply a controlled break in the circuit.

Static Electricity

You have probably experienced static electricity — the shock you sometimes get touching a metal door handle, or the way a balloon sticks to a wall after being rubbed on your hair. Static electricity is the build-up of electric charge on a surface, usually from friction rubbing electrons off one material onto another.

Lightning is a dramatic example of static electricity. As ice crystals and water droplets collide in a thundercloud, they create an enormous build-up of charge. When the charge difference between the cloud and the ground becomes large enough, it discharges in a massive spark — lightning — which can be billions of volts and tens of thousands of amperes.

Brain electricity: The human brain generates about 12–25 watts of electrical power. Scientists can detect the brain's electrical activity using a device called an EEG (electroencephalogram). The brain never stops generating electrical signals — even when you are asleep.

Electrical Safety

Electricity can be extremely dangerous. It can cause burns, heart attacks, and death. Safety rules: Never put objects in electrical outlets. Never touch electrical devices with wet hands. Keep electrical cords away from water. Never stick anything into a toaster. Never fly kites near power lines. If you ever see a downed power line, stay far away and tell an adult immediately.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Investigation: Conductors and Insulators

Test at least 8 materials from around your home. For each one, touch it to complete a simple circuit (battery → wire → material → wire → bulb). If the bulb lights up, the material is a conductor. If not, it is an insulator.

Create a data table: Material | Conductor or Insulator | Why (guess) | Surprise?

Test a pencil lead (graphite). Is it a conductor? Graphite is a form of carbon — an unusual conductor. This is the same carbon that diamonds are made of. Why does graphite conduct electricity but diamond does not?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Circuit Building

Build at least three different circuits: 1) A simple circuit with one bulb. 2) A circuit with a switch (use a paperclip as a switch). 3) A circuit with two bulbs in sequence (series). Draw and label each circuit.

Day 2: Electrical Safety Poster

Design a large, colourful electrical safety poster for a Grade 1 classroom. Include at least 8 safety rules with illustrations. Make it clear and easy to understand for a young child.

Day 3: How Electricity Reaches Our Home

Research and draw a detailed diagram showing how electricity gets from a power plant to the outlet in your home. Label: power plant, transformer, high-voltage transmission lines, local transformer, home electrical panel, outlets.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Electricity Innovation Report

Research one of these electricity innovations:

  • Solar panels (photovoltaic cells)
  • Wind turbines
  • Fuel cells
  • Nuclear fusion reactors

For your chosen innovation, write a report that answers: How does it generate electricity? What are its advantages? What are its challenges or limitations? Where is it being used today? What improvements are scientists working on? Include a labelled diagram. Aim for 1–2 pages.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Benjamin Franklin: Research how Benjamin Franklin's famous kite experiment proved that lightning is electricity. Why was this discovery so important?
  • Nikola Tesla: Tesla developed the alternating current (AC) system that powers most of the world today. Why was his approach better than Edison's direct current (DC) for long-distance power transmission?
  • How do batteries work? Research the chemical reaction inside a battery that creates electrical energy. Why do batteries eventually run out?
  • Electrical fish: Electric eels can produce up to 860 volts of electricity. How? What do they use it for? Research other electric animals.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Could we ever transmit electricity wirelessly — without wires — on a large scale? Nikola Tesla tried to do this 120 years ago. Research his Wardenclyffe Tower.
  • Superconductors are materials that have zero electrical resistance at very cold temperatures — electricity flows through them with no loss. If we could make superconductors that work at room temperature, how would this change the world?
  • The human heart beats because of electrical impulses. What happens if those impulses are disrupted? Research defibrillators.
  • Elon Musk's company Neuralink is developing brain-computer interfaces that could let people control computers with their thoughts. How does the brain's electrical activity make this possible?
Week 25–26

🔄 Energy Transformations — The Shape-Shifter

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — C3.1 – Describe how energy changes from one form to another
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Here is a challenge: trace the energy in your breakfast back to the Big Bang.

You ate cereal this morning. The cereal came from wheat. The wheat grew using sunlight energy. The sunlight is nuclear fusion energy from the Sun. The Sun formed from a cloud of gas that collapsed under gravity. The gas came from the Big Bang, 13.8 billion years ago.

The energy in your bowl of cereal has been transforming for 13.8 billion years to reach you.

The universe is an energy transformation machine. Every moment, in every corner of the cosmos, energy is changing form: nuclear to electromagnetic, electromagnetic to chemical, chemical to kinetic, kinetic to thermal, thermal back to electromagnetic… in an endless, beautiful cascade of transformation. Nothing is ever lost. Nothing is ever created. The same energy just keeps changing shape, forever.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Describe how energy changes from one form to another
  • Trace multi-step energy transformation chains
  • Understand that some energy is always 'lost' as heat in any transformation
  • Identify energy transformations in common devices and natural systems
  • Connect energy transformation to the concept of efficiency
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Transformation Chain Game

Play a verbal chain game: one person says a form of energy, the next person says what it can transform into. For example: 'Light' → 'heat' → 'kinetic (warm air rising)' → 'kinetic (wind)' → 'electricity (wind turbine)' → 'light (light bulb)' → … See how long you can go before repeating.

Device Investigations

Take three common devices in your home. For each one: identify what energy goes IN and what useful energy comes OUT — and also what 'wasted' energy comes out (usually heat). Example: a light bulb: electricity goes in, light comes out (the useful part), heat comes out (the 'wasted' part).

📖

The Big Lesson

Energy Transformation: The Rule of the Universe

We have already learned about many forms of energy: light, heat, sound, kinetic, electrical, chemical. Now here is the key insight: energy is always changing form. Every time anything happens in the universe — every event, every process, every reaction — energy is transforming.

Common Transformation Chains

A solar panel transforms light energy → electrical energy. A toaster transforms electrical energy → heat energy. A car engine transforms chemical energy (gasoline) → kinetic energy + heat energy. A microphone transforms sound energy → electrical energy. A speaker transforms electrical energy → sound energy. Your muscles transform chemical energy (food) → kinetic energy + heat energy.

Energy is 'Wasted' as Heat

In every energy transformation, some energy is converted to heat and 'lost' into the environment. A light bulb converts electrical energy into light — but also into heat (you can feel the warmth near a bulb). A car engine converts chemical energy into kinetic — but about 70% of the energy is lost as heat, which is why cars need radiators.

This 'wasting' of energy as heat is described by the Second Law of Thermodynamics — one of the most profound laws in all of science. It says that in any energy transformation, the universe becomes slightly more 'disordered' (scientists call this increase in disorder an increase in entropy). Perfect efficiency is impossible.

LED vs. incandescent: An old-fashioned incandescent light bulb converts only about 5% of electrical energy into light — the other 95% becomes heat. An LED light bulb converts about 40-50% into light. This is why LED bulbs last longer and use much less electricity — they are simply more efficient at the energy transformation.

Efficiency

Efficiency measures how much of the input energy becomes useful output energy. A perfectly efficient machine would convert 100% of input to useful output with no heat loss — but this is physically impossible. Engineers always try to make machines more efficient: better car engines, better power plants, better batteries.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Device Efficiency Investigation

Choose 3 devices in your home. For each one, research (with a parent's help) or estimate its energy efficiency. Create a table: Device | Input Energy | Useful Output | Wasted as Heat | Efficiency (%) | How could it be improved?

The human body converts food energy to movement with about 25% efficiency. A gasoline car engine is about 30% efficient. A bicycle is over 95% efficient for human-powered motion. Why is the bicycle so much more efficient than the car?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Transformation Chain Posters

Create two large posters, each showing a different energy transformation chain with at least 5 steps. Use arrows and coloured labels for each energy form. Include the 'wasted heat' at each step as a small orange arrow.

Day 2: Device Dissection (Imaginary)

Choose one complex device (e.g., a washing machine, a car, a computer). Research how it works. Draw a labelled diagram and trace all the energy transformations that happen inside it during normal operation.

Day 3: Design an Efficient Machine

Imagine you are an engineer. Design a new machine that does ONE useful task (e.g., moves water uphill, generates light, makes toast) but with much less waste than current devices. Draw your design, label all parts, and explain what energy transformations happen and how you reduced waste.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Energy Transformation Storybook

Write and illustrate a children's storybook (at least 8 pages) that follows a single unit of energy — let's call it 'Erica the Energy Particle' — through as many transformations as you can imagine. Start from sunlight hitting a leaf and follow Erica through at least 8 different forms and locations. Make it scientifically accurate but also fun and imaginative. Each page should have an illustration and 2–4 sentences of text.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Food energy: When you eat a biscuit, trace the full chain of energy transformations from the Sun to your muscle movement. How many steps can you identify?
  • Perpetual motion machine: Many inventors have tried to build a machine that keeps running forever without any energy input. Research why this is impossible according to the laws of physics.
  • Black holes: Black holes convert matter directly into energy with about 10% efficiency — far more efficient than nuclear bombs (0.7%). Research how and what this means.
  • Renewable vs. non-renewable revisited: Using what you know about energy transformations, explain in your own words why renewable energy is better for the environment.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • If energy cannot be created or destroyed, but some is always 'lost' as heat, what is the ultimate fate of all energy in the universe? (Research 'heat death of the universe')
  • Cold fusion — the idea of producing nuclear fusion energy at room temperature rather than millions of degrees — has been claimed but never replicated reliably. Why would this be such a world-changing discovery?
  • The brain uses about the same amount of energy whether you are doing complex math or daydreaming. What does this tell us about how the brain works?
  • Could we ever build a device that is 100% efficient? What physical law prevents this?
Week 26

🌱 Saving Energy — Our Responsibility and Our Future

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — C3.2 – Describe ways to conserve energy and explain why conservation matters
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In 1973, oil-producing countries suddenly reduced how much oil they would sell to the world. Gasoline prices quadrupled overnight. In some countries, people could only buy gas on certain days. Highways had speed limits reduced to save fuel. The crisis showed the world how completely dependent modern civilization had become on fossil fuels.

Today, we face a different but connected challenge: using fossil fuels releases carbon dioxide, which is warming the planet. The question is not just 'how do we find more energy?' but 'how do we use energy wisely — and transition to energy sources that don't harm the planet?'

The good news: In the past decade, the cost of solar and wind energy has fallen by 90%. In many parts of the world, building new solar panels is now cheaper than continuing to run old coal power plants. A clean energy revolution is already underway — and your generation will live in a world shaped by it.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand why conserving energy matters (environmental and economic reasons)
  • Identify practical ways to reduce energy use at home, school, and in transportation
  • Understand the concept of energy efficiency in products
  • Understand that individual actions add up to large collective impacts
  • Appreciate the importance of renewable energy transition
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Energy Audit + Action Plan

This lesson works best when connected directly to your child's own life. Do a second home energy audit, but this time focus on waste: lights left on in empty rooms, electronics on standby, appliances running longer than needed. Ask: 'How much energy do you think this wastes in a year? What if we fixed it?'

The Math of Small Actions

Help your child calculate a small example: if your family leaves three lights on 2 extra hours per day, and each light uses 10 watts, that is 3 × 10W × 2h = 60 watt-hours per day, or 21.9 kWh per year. At Ontario's electricity rate of about $0.12/kWh, that is about $2.63 per year wasted — from just three lights. Multiply this across every home in Canada...

📖

The Big Lesson

Why Saving Energy Matters

When we burn fossil fuels to generate energy, we release carbon dioxide (CO₂) and other greenhouse gases into the atmosphere. These gases trap heat, raising Earth's global temperature — a process called climate change. Climate change is causing more extreme weather events, melting ice caps, rising sea levels, and disruptions to ecosystems that support all life, including ours.

Saving energy reduces the amount of fuel we need to burn, which reduces greenhouse gas emissions. It also saves money and makes our energy supply more secure.

Ways to Save Energy at Home

Lighting: Switch to LED bulbs (use 75% less energy than incandescent). Turn off lights in empty rooms. Use natural daylight when possible.

Heating and cooling: This is the biggest energy user. Lower the thermostat by 2°C in winter — you will use 5–10% less heating energy. Seal drafts around windows and doors. Use window coverings to keep heat in at night.

Appliances: Wash clothes in cold water (heating water uses most of a washing machine's energy). Run dishwashers only when full. Unplug 'energy vampires' — electronics that use power even when switched off.

Transportation: Walk, cycle, or take public transit instead of driving. Carpool. Choose fuel-efficient or electric vehicles.

The most energy efficient action: Studies show that improving the insulation of buildings is one of the most cost-effective ways to reduce energy use. A well-insulated home can use 50–70% less heating energy than a poorly insulated one — saving both money and the environment.

Energy Efficiency Standards

Governments set energy efficiency standards for appliances, vehicles, and buildings. Products are rated with labels like ENERGY STAR to help consumers choose more efficient options. Modern refrigerators use 60% less electricity than refrigerators made in the 1970s — same function, much less energy.

The Power of Collective Action

Individual actions may seem small, but they add up. If every household in Canada turned off one light for one hour per day, it would save enough electricity to power thousands of homes for a year. When millions of people make small changes, the effect is enormous.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Carbon Footprint Exploration

A carbon footprint is a measure of how much greenhouse gas our activities produce.

Work with a parent to estimate your family's carbon footprint by thinking about: How do you usually travel? (car, bus, cycling) How is your home heated? What do you eat mostly? Do you buy new things often or try to reuse things?

Research and discuss: What are the three biggest sources of carbon emissions in most Canadian households?

If you could change only ONE thing about your family's energy habits, what would make the biggest difference to your carbon footprint? Research to find out if your intuition is correct.
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Energy Conservation Action Plan

Create a detailed, illustrated 'Family Energy Conservation Action Plan.' Include: 5 specific actions you will take, the estimated energy and money saved by each, who is responsible for each action, and a date to check how you are doing.

Day 2: Products Comparison

Compare two pairs of products: an LED bulb vs. an incandescent bulb, and an efficient appliance vs. a regular one (use ENERGY STAR information). Create a comparison chart showing: energy use, lifetime cost, and environmental impact.

Day 3: Energy of the Future Essay

Write an essay (at least 2 paragraphs) imagining what energy use will look like in 50 years. What will have changed? What energy sources will we use? How will homes, transport, and cities be different? Use real science to support your ideas.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Community Energy Awareness Campaign

Design a community awareness campaign about energy conservation. Create:

  1. A poster (printed or drawn) with key energy saving tips
  2. A one-page newsletter article explaining why energy conservation matters
  3. A short 'quiz' (5 questions) people can take to learn about their energy use
  4. A list of 3 local resources (e.g., city programs, Ontario rebates) for energy efficiency

Present your campaign materials to your family as if they were the community.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Ontario's energy plan: Research what Ontario's government is doing about energy and climate change. What targets have they set? Are they on track?
  • Greta Thunberg: Who is Greta Thunberg? What did she do, and why? Do you agree or disagree with her approach?
  • Energy justice: Not everyone can afford to make energy-efficient choices — for example, new electric cars or home insulation are expensive. How should society help lower-income families participate in the energy transition?
  • Net-zero buildings: Research 'net-zero' or 'passive houses' — buildings that generate as much energy as they use. Find an example in Canada and explain how it works.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Could the world run entirely on renewable energy by 2050? Some scientists say yes; others say no. Research the debate and form your own view.
  • What is the connection between energy use and poverty? Why do some argue that restricting energy access unfairly hurts developing countries?
  • If you could redesign your city from scratch to use as little energy as possible, what would it look like?
  • Energy conservation is about using less. But could we also find ways to use energy more intelligently — AI managing smart grids, for example? Research 'smart cities'.
Week 27

🔬 Energy Investigation — Design Your Own Experiment

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — C1.1–C1.4 – Apply full scientific investigation process to an energy question
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Every great scientific discovery began with someone who was curious enough to ask a question AND disciplined enough to design a rigorous test of that question. Albert Einstein asked: 'What would it be like to ride alongside a beam of light?' Marie Curie asked: 'Why do some materials emit mysterious rays without any energy source?' Charles Darwin asked: 'Why do animals on distant islands look slightly different from each other?'

Today, YOU are the scientist. You get to choose your question, design your test, gather your evidence, and draw your conclusions. Your question is smaller than Einstein's — but the process is exactly the same.

Science is not a collection of facts — it is a method of questioning. The facts we know today are just the best answers we have right now. Future scientists — maybe including you — will discover better answers that overturn what we currently believe. Science actively welcomes this. That is its power.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Apply the full scientific investigation process: question → hypothesis → method → data → conclusion
  • Design a fair test (controlled variables, independent variable, dependent variable)
  • Collect and record data in organised tables
  • Draw evidence-based conclusions
  • Communicate findings clearly with writing and diagrams
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Investigation Options

Your child should choose ONE question from the list below (or propose their own with your guidance):

  1. Which colour of material gets warmest in sunlight?
  2. Does the thickness of insulation affect how well it keeps ice from melting?
  3. Does the length of a straw affect how loud a straw whistle is?
  4. How does the height of a ramp affect how far a toy car travels?
  5. Which material is the best conductor of heat — metal, wood, or plastic?

Guiding the Process

Work through the steps together but let your child make the decisions. Your role is to ask questions, not give answers: 'What do you think will happen? How could we make sure our test is fair? Did the results surprise you?'

Emphasize: 'It is okay if your hypothesis was wrong. Wrong predictions are some of the most interesting scientific results! They teach us something unexpected.'

📖

The Big Lesson

The Scientific Investigation Process

Real scientists use a structured process to ask and answer questions about the world. Here are the steps:

Step 1: Ask a Question. A good scientific question is specific and testable. Not 'How does light work?' (too broad) but 'Does a darker colour absorb more light energy than a lighter colour?' (testable).

Step 2: Background Research. Before testing, find out what is already known. This helps you make a better prediction and avoid wasting time testing things that have already been answered.

Step 3: Hypothesis. Make a specific, testable prediction: 'I predict that darker colours will absorb more light energy because dark surfaces absorb more radiation, resulting in a higher temperature increase.' A hypothesis explains WHY you think what you think.

Step 4: Design the Experiment — Fair Testing. A fair test changes only ONE thing at a time (the independent variable) and measures ONE thing at a time (the dependent variable). Everything else stays the same (controlled variables). If you change two things at once, you cannot know which one caused any difference you observe.

Step 5: Collect Data. Carry out the experiment carefully. Record your measurements in a table. Do the test more than once to check your results are consistent.

Step 6: Analyze and Conclude. What patterns do you see in your data? Does the data support your hypothesis? If not, why might that be? What would you do differently next time?

Step 7: Communicate. Real scientists share their findings with others — through papers, presentations, and discussions. This allows others to check the work and build on it.

Peer review: Before a scientific discovery is accepted by the scientific community, other scientists test it and look for flaws. This process is called peer review. It is what makes science self-correcting — errors eventually get found and corrected.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Identifying Bias

Scientists also try to eliminate bias — anything that might make results inaccurate without realising it.

Think about your investigation: Did you measure as accurately as you could? Did you repeat the test to check? Did you record exactly what happened, not what you expected to happen?

One well-known bias is confirmation bias — the tendency to notice results that support your hypothesis and ignore results that don't. How could you design your investigation to minimise this bias?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Investigation Design

Complete an investigation planning sheet:

  • My question:
  • My background research (what I already know):
  • My hypothesis (prediction + reason):
  • Independent variable (what I will change):
  • Dependent variable (what I will measure):
  • Controlled variables (what I will keep the same — list at least 3):
  • Materials needed:
  • Procedure (step-by-step, numbered):
  • Data table (set up in advance)

Days 2–3: Conduct Investigation & Analyse

Carry out your investigation. Fill in your data table with actual measurements. Calculate averages if you have multiple trials. Make a graph of your results if appropriate (ask for help with graphing if needed). Write your conclusion: 4–6 sentences explaining what your data shows and whether your hypothesis was correct.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Full Scientific Report

Write a formal scientific investigation report with all seven sections:

  1. Title and Date
  2. Question
  3. Background Research (at least 5 facts about the topic)
  4. Hypothesis
  5. Method (complete materials list + step-by-step procedure)
  6. Results (data table + graph + observations)
  7. Conclusion (what you found, whether hypothesis was supported, sources of error, what you would do differently)

Illustrate your report with at least three labelled diagrams. Present your findings to your family.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Famous scientific investigations: Research one famous experiment in the history of science (e.g., Galileo's gravity experiment, Pasteur's germ experiment, Newton's prism experiment). Write about what question they asked, what they tested, and what they discovered.
  • Science failures: Many great scientific discoveries came from experiments that did not work as expected. Research one famous 'accidental discovery' in science (e.g., penicillin, X-rays, microwave ovens, Velcro). What does this tell us about science?
  • Citizen science: Research projects where ordinary people (not professional scientists) help gather scientific data — like tracking bird populations or classifying galaxies. How could you participate?
  • Women in science: Research two female scientists who made major contributions to energy science or physics (e.g., Marie Curie, Lise Meitner, Chien-Shiung Wu). What obstacles did they face?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Science is the most successful method humans have ever found for understanding reality. But it cannot answer every question. What kinds of questions does science NOT answer well?
  • Could an AI ever replace human scientists? Why or why not?
  • Some scientific questions are so complex that they may never be fully answered. What are some examples? How should scientists deal with deep, possibly unanswerable questions?
  • If you could ask one question that science has not yet answered, what would it be? How would you even begin to test it?
Week 27–28

🌟 Showcase & Review — Energy Masters

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — C – Consolidation of all Strand C expectations
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

You have travelled across the entire landscape of energy: from the nuclear fires of the Sun to the electrical pathways in your own brain. You have discovered that energy cannot be created or destroyed, only transformed. You have learned that everything that happens — every event, every heartbeat, every thought, every storm, every star — is energy changing form.

Today, you do not learn something new. Today, you show what you know. You have become an energy scientist. Now you will teach others.

The deepest wonder: Scientists are still discovering entirely new things about energy. In 1998, astronomers discovered that the universe's expansion is accelerating — speeding up rather than slowing down. Something is pushing it. They called it 'dark energy.' It makes up 68% of the total energy of the universe. We have absolutely no idea what it is. The greatest energy mystery is still unsolved.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Review and demonstrate understanding of all Strand C energy concepts
  • Apply energy knowledge to new, unseen scenarios
  • Communicate scientific understanding clearly through written and visual work
  • Reflect on what was learned and what questions remain
  • Celebrate the learning journey through Strand C
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Showcase Event Planning

Plan a 'Science Showcase' event — even if it is just for family members. Let your child present their work from Strand C: their investigation report, their storybook, their energy diary, their best drawings. Ask other family members to ask questions that your child must answer.

Review Strategy: Teach-Back

The best way to know if you truly understand something is to try to explain it to someone who doesn't. Ask your child to teach each of the major Strand C concepts to you, a sibling, or a toy, as if they know nothing about it. Note any gaps in understanding — these are areas to revisit.

📖

The Big Lesson

What We Learned in Strand C: A Summary

In Strand C, we explored Energy in Our Lives. Here is what you discovered:

Energy is the ability to cause change. It takes many forms: light, heat, sound, kinetic, electrical, chemical/stored. Energy cannot be created or destroyed — it only transforms from one form to another.

The Sun is Earth's primary energy source. Its nuclear fusion reactions send light and heat to Earth, driving photosynthesis, weather, seasons, and the water cycle.

Light travels in straight lines, reflects off surfaces, refracts when passing through different materials, and can be split into the spectrum of colours. Materials can be transparent, translucent, or opaque.

Heat is the energy of moving particles. It transfers by conduction (through contact), convection (through fluids), and radiation (through electromagnetic waves). Insulators slow heat transfer.

Sound is created by vibrations and travels through matter as pressure waves. It cannot travel through a vacuum. Pitch depends on vibration frequency; volume depends on vibration size.

Kinetic energy is the energy of motion. It depends on both mass and speed. It transforms into heat and sound when slowed by friction.

Our homes use energy from electricity (generated from nuclear, hydro, wind, solar, and gas sources) and combustion fuels. Renewable energy sources are sustainable; non-renewable sources are not.

Electricity is the flow of electrons through conductors. It requires a complete circuit. Conductors allow flow; insulators block it. Static electricity is charge build-up.

Energy transformations always produce some heat as a byproduct. Efficiency measures how much input energy becomes useful output. No transformation is 100% efficient.

Energy conservation reduces environmental harm from burning fossil fuels. Individual and collective action makes a real difference.

Your energy journey: You have now studied more about energy than many adults know. The concepts you understand — conservation of energy, entropy, renewable vs. non-renewable, energy transformation — are the foundations of physics, engineering, and environmental science. You are not just learning facts; you are learning to think like a scientist.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

The Big Energy Quiz — Challenge Level

Answer these questions in full sentences. Use the most detail you can. These questions require you to APPLY what you know, not just remember it.

  1. A roller coaster car starts at the top of the first hill. Trace its energy through the whole ride, naming every transformation.
  2. Why is it impossible to build a machine that runs forever without any energy input?
  3. How is the energy in a chocolate bar connected to the Sun?
  4. Why is it better for the environment to turn off a light you are not using, even if the difference seems tiny?
  5. If you were designing a new power plant for a city, which energy source would you choose and why?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Energy Mind Map

Create a large, comprehensive mind map of everything you learned in Strand C. Start with 'ENERGY' in the centre. Branch out to each form of energy, each major concept, and examples. Use colour and illustrations. Aim for at least 40 items on your mind map.

Day 2: Exhibit Display

Choose your three best pieces of work from Strand C and prepare them as if for a science exhibition. Write a label card for each one explaining: what it is, what it shows, and what you learned from making it.

Day 3: Energy Letter to Future Self

Write a letter to your future self (10 years from now) about what you learned about energy. Include: 3 facts that amazed you, 2 questions you still have, 1 thing you will do differently because of what you learned, and your thoughts about what energy science might discover in the next 10 years.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Energy Portfolio and Reflection

Compile a portfolio of your best work from all of Strand C. For each item, write a reflection (3–5 sentences) explaining: what you were learning when you made it, what you are most proud of, and what you would improve.

At the front, write a 1-page 'Strand C Summary Essay' in your own words. Do not just list facts — explain how the different forms of energy are connected, why energy matters for our planet, and what you think is the most important energy question for the next generation to solve.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Future energy prediction: Write a 'newspaper article' from the year 2076, reporting on how humans solved the climate change crisis. What energy technologies did they use?
  • Thank-you note to a scientist: Choose one scientist who made a major contribution to energy science and write them a thank-you letter explaining how their discovery has affected your life.
  • Energy art: Create a piece of art (drawing, collage, sculpture from recycled materials) that represents one energy concept from Strand C. Write a 'curator's note' explaining what it depicts.
  • Review quiz: Create a 10-question quiz for Strand C, write an answer key, and challenge a family member to take it.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Dark energy makes up 68% of the universe but we have no idea what it is. What are scientists' current best guesses?
  • If you could have unlimited clean energy for the world, what would you use it for first?
  • Energy is at the heart of some of the greatest challenges humanity faces: climate change, poverty, water access. Which challenge do you think is most urgent?
  • In 1 billion years, the Sun will be 10% brighter and Earth's oceans will evaporate. What would humans need to do to survive? Is this even possible?
Reference · All Year

📚 Vocabulary Dictionary

All key science words for Grade 1
📖

Science Word Bank

Learn these important science words. Read each word and its meaning. Can you use the word in your own sentence?

Energy
The ability to cause change or do work. Energy takes many forms and can transform from one to another but cannot be created or destroyed.
Kinetic Energy
The energy of motion. Any moving object has kinetic energy — the faster and heavier it is, the more it has.
Potential Energy
Stored energy that has the potential to become another form. A ball at the top of a hill has potential energy.
Light Energy
A form of electromagnetic energy that travels in waves. It allows us to see and can travel through empty space.
Heat Energy
The energy of particle movement within a material. The faster particles move, the more heat energy the material has.
Sound Energy
Energy created by vibrations that travels as pressure waves through matter. It cannot travel through a vacuum.
Electrical Energy
The energy of moving electrons through a conductor. It can power lights, motors, and many other devices.
Nuclear Energy
Energy released when the nucleus of an atom is split or fused. The Sun generates energy through nuclear fusion.
Photosynthesis
The process by which plants convert light energy and carbon dioxide into chemical energy (sugar) and oxygen.
Conductor
A material through which electricity or heat flows easily. Metals like copper are excellent electrical conductors.
Insulator
A material that resists the flow of electricity or heat. Rubber, plastic, and wood are common insulators.
Conduction
The transfer of heat through direct contact between materials. Heat flows from warmer areas to cooler areas.
Convection
The transfer of heat through the movement of fluids (liquids and gases). Warm fluid rises; cool fluid sinks.
Radiation
The transfer of heat through electromagnetic waves. The Sun heats Earth through radiation across empty space.
Renewable Energy
Energy from sources that naturally replenish and will not run out: solar, wind, water, and geothermal.
Non-renewable Energy
Energy from sources that take millions of years to form and can be used up: coal, oil, and natural gas.
Efficiency
The ratio of useful energy output to total energy input. No machine is 100% efficient — some energy is always lost as heat.
Circuit
A complete, closed path through which electricity can flow, including a power source, conductors, and a load.
Frequency
The number of vibrations per second. Higher frequency = higher pitch sound. Measured in hertz (Hz).
Conservation of Energy
The law that energy cannot be created or destroyed, only changed from one form to another.

Strand D · Structures & Mechanisms
Week 29–30 · Strand D Opens

🧱 What Are Materials? — The Building Blocks of Everything

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — D1.1 – Identify and describe common materials and their properties
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Before you build anything, you must choose your materials. But imagine building a bridge out of tissue paper. Or a raincoat out of cardboard. Or a frying pan out of chocolate. These seem obviously wrong — but why?

The answer is that every material has specific properties — characteristics that make it suitable or unsuitable for different jobs. The art and science of choosing the right material for the right job is called materials science, and it is one of the oldest and most important fields in all of engineering.

Materials science in history: The story of human civilization can be told through materials. The Stone Age, the Bronze Age, the Iron Age, the Steel Age, the Silicon Age — each era is named after the key material that defined it. Today we live in an age of extraordinary materials: carbon fibre stronger than steel at a fraction of the weight, aerogel so light it floats on air yet strong enough to hold 4,000 times its own weight, materials that repair themselves like skin, and graphene — a single layer of carbon atoms — stronger than anything else known.

Every object around you was made by someone who chose each material deliberately. Today, we start to understand how.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Identify common natural and manufactured materials
  • Describe materials using observable properties: colour, texture, hardness, flexibility, transparency
  • Compare materials by their properties
  • Explain that different materials are suited to different purposes
  • Introduce the concept of materials science as a field of study
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials Collection

Before this lesson, collect 12–15 small objects of different materials: a wooden block, a metal spoon, a plastic cup, a piece of cloth, a rubber band, a glass jar, a piece of paper, a rock, a cotton ball, some foil, a piece of leather, and so on. These will be used for observations and sorting activities throughout the lesson.

Day 1: Sorting Activity
