Spread all the objects on a table. Ask your child to sort them without any instructions — just 'put similar things together.' Ask: 'How did you decide what goes together? What did you notice about each object?' Then introduce the formal vocabulary for properties.

Day 2: Property Testing

Test each object systematically: Is it hard or soft (press it)? Is it flexible or rigid (try to bend it)? Is it rough or smooth (feel it)? Is it transparent, translucent, or opaque? Is it heavy or light for its size? Does it absorb water or repel it (test with a drop)?

📖

The Big Lesson

What Is a Material?

A material is any substance that objects are made from. Materials can be natural — found in the natural world, like wood, stone, cotton, wool, rubber, and clay — or manufactured (synthetic) — made by humans from natural materials, like plastic, glass, metal alloys, and nylon.

Properties of Materials

A property is a characteristic of a material that we can observe or measure. Different properties make materials useful for different purposes.

Hardness: How resistant the material is to being scratched or dented. Diamond is the hardest natural material. Butter is very soft.

Flexibility: How much a material can bend without breaking. Rubber is very flexible; glass is not (it breaks instead of bending).

Texture: How a material feels to the touch. Sandpaper is rough; silk is smooth.

Transparency: Whether light can pass through (transparent = glass; translucent = wax paper; opaque = wood).

Strength: How much force a material can withstand. Steel is very strong; paper is weak.

Density: How heavy a material is for its size. Lead is dense (heavy for its size); foam is not dense.

Absorbency: How well a material soaks up liquids. A sponge is very absorbent; wax is not.

Waterproofing: Whether a material repels water. Rubber and plastic are waterproof; cotton is not.

Conductivity: Whether a material conducts heat or electricity. Metals are good conductors; most non-metals are insulators.

Materials by the numbers: Scientists have discovered or created over 10 million different chemical compounds that could be materials. There are hundreds of different types of plastic alone. Steel comes in hundreds of formulations, each with different properties tailored to specific uses. Modern smartphones alone contain over 60 different elements from the periodic table.

Natural vs. Manufactured Materials

Natural materials come directly from the environment. Wood comes from trees. Wool comes from sheep. Cotton comes from plants. Stone comes from the ground. Manufactured materials are processed or created by humans. Glass is made by melting sand. Plastic is made from petroleum. Steel is made by combining iron with small amounts of carbon.

Choosing the Right Material

Engineers and designers always match materials to their purpose. A hammer head must be hard (so it can hit things without breaking) but not brittle (or it would shatter). A raincoat must be waterproof and flexible. A window must be transparent and strong. This matching of material properties to purpose is called materials selection — one of the most important decisions in engineering design.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Material Properties Testing

Design a systematic materials test. Create a table with your materials as rows and these properties as columns: hard/soft, flexible/rigid, smooth/rough, transparent/opaque, absorbs water/repels water, heavy/light.

Test each material for each property. Record YES, NO, or PARTLY. When done, look at your data and answer:

  • Which material has the most useful combination of properties?
  • Which material would you use to make a waterproof bag?
  • Which material would you use to make a window?
  • Which material would you use to make a cutting board?
Why do scientists need to test materials systematically rather than just guessing which property each one has? Could your senses alone reliably tell the difference between all materials?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Materials Collection and Observation

Collect 10 different materials from around your home. For each one: draw a detailed sketch, describe its colour and texture, rate its hardness (1–5 scale), and name at least 2 products where this material is used. Explain WHY it is used in those products.

Day 2: Property Testing Journal

Complete the systematic materials testing chart. Write a paragraph comparing the two most different materials in your collection. How are they different? How are they similar?

Day 3: Material Biography

Choose one material (e.g., glass, wool, or concrete). Write a 'biography' of that material: Where does it come from? How is it made? What are its main properties? What is it used for? What would the world be like without it?

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Materials Investigator Portfolio

Over the week, become a 'Materials Investigator.' Your portfolio will include:

  1. A 'Materials Passport' for 8 different materials (mini-profile of properties and uses for each)
  2. A 'Material of the Day' entry for 5 days (choose a different material each day, observe it deeply, write 5 facts about it)
  3. A 'Mystery Material' challenge: describe a material so precisely that someone else could identify it without you naming it
  4. A 'Design Brief' — a problem you have at home (e.g., 'need a waterproof cover for my books in the rain') — explain which material you would use and why
🏠

Homework Activities

  • Graphene: Research graphene — a material made of a single layer of carbon atoms. What are its remarkable properties? What could it be used for in the future?
  • Biomaterials: Scientists are developing materials inspired by nature — materials as strong as spider silk, as self-repairing as skin, as water-repellent as a lotus leaf. Research one of these biomaterials.
  • Ancient materials: What materials did people use in ancient Egypt to build the pyramids? How did they shape and move stone without modern equipment?
  • Materials you wear: What materials are in the clothes you are wearing right now? Trace each fibre to its source — plant, animal, petroleum, or mineral.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Scientists are working on 'programmable matter' — materials that can change their shape, stiffness, or other properties on demand. What could you do with a material that could be hard when you need it hard and soft when you need it soft?
  • Some animals make remarkable materials — spider silk is stronger than steel, abalone shells are harder than most ceramics. Can humans ever truly replicate biological materials?
  • What will the most important material of the 22nd century be? What properties would it need to have?
  • Materials science and medicine intersect in 'smart materials' for medical implants that respond to body temperature or pH. How might these change healthcare?
Week 30

🔬 Properties of Materials — Testing What Things Can Do

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — D1.1 – Investigate and compare properties of materials through testing
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In 1912, the RMS Titanic sank in the North Atlantic after hitting an iceberg. When investigators later examined the rivets holding the ship's steel plates together, they discovered they had been made from a lower-quality iron with high levels of slag (impurities). At the frigid temperatures of the North Atlantic, this material became brittle and shattered rather than bending. A small difference in material properties led to one of history's most famous disasters.

Every engineering failure in history is a failure of materials understanding. Engineers study these failures to learn. The Tacoma Narrows Bridge collapsed in 1940 because of resonance (the wind made it oscillate at its natural frequency). The Challenger space shuttle broke apart in 1986 because rubber O-rings became brittle in cold temperatures. Knowing how materials behave under different conditions is not just interesting — it saves lives.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Design and carry out fair tests to compare material properties
  • Use scientific vocabulary to describe material properties precisely
  • Record observations in organized data tables
  • Draw conclusions from property tests
  • Understand that the same material can have different properties under different conditions
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Testing Station Setup

Set up 4 testing 'stations' around the room:

  • Station 1 — Strength test: Stack books on top of different materials to see when they break or compress
  • Station 2 — Flexibility test: Try to bend strips of materials without breaking them; measure the maximum bend angle
  • Station 3 — Water test: Place drops of water on each material, watch what happens — does it absorb, bead up, or spread?
  • Station 4 — Scratch test: Try to scratch each material with a coin, a fingernail, and a metal nail — which leaves a mark?

Connecting to Real Design

After testing, ask: 'If you were designing a shoe sole, which material would you choose from your tested samples? What if you needed a window? What if you needed a waterproof bag?' Connect property testing directly to engineering decisions.

📖

The Big Lesson

Testing Materials — The Scientific Approach

A material's properties tell us how it will behave in different situations. But properties are not always obvious just from looking. We need to test them.

Strength Testing

Strength is a material's ability to resist being broken, stretched, squeezed, or torn. There are different kinds of strength:

  • Tensile strength: resistance to being stretched (a steel cable is strong in tension)
  • Compressive strength: resistance to being squeezed (concrete is strong in compression)
  • Shear strength: resistance to being cut or torn (a knife blade must have high shear strength)

Engineers must know which kind of stress a material will face in a structure, because a material that is strong in one way may be weak in another. Concrete is very strong when compressed (squeezed) but weak when stretched — that is why concrete buildings use steel reinforcement inside.

Flexibility and Elasticity

Flexibility is how much a material can bend without breaking. Elasticity is whether a material springs back to its original shape after bending. Rubber is both flexible and elastic. A plastic straw is flexible but not very elastic — it stays bent.

Durability

Durability is how well a material holds up over time and under wear. A road surface must be highly durable — it must resist the weight of millions of vehicles. A paper plate is not durable — it weakens when wet and cannot be reused many times.

Testing extremes: Materials are tested under extreme conditions to predict how they will behave in real use. Aircraft materials are tested at temperatures from -60°C (high altitude) to 400°C (near engines). Medical implants are tested to survive 30 million stress cycles — roughly 30 years of use — without failing. The NASA Mars Rover's wheels were tested on simulated Mars terrain for years before launch.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Fair Testing Design

Design a fair test for one material property of your choice. Your test must be:

  • Repeatable: You could do it the same way 10 times
  • Fair: Only one thing changes between trials
  • Measurable: The result can be measured, not just estimated

Write your test plan before starting. Then conduct it. Compare your prediction with your results.

Why is it important to do a materials test multiple times? What could happen to your results if you only test something once? How do scientists deal with inconsistent results?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Materials Testing Stations

Complete all four testing stations. Record all results in your data table. For each test, note what surprised you.

Day 2: Graphing and Analysis

Choose one test result and create a graph or chart to display your findings visually. Write a paragraph describing the pattern in your results and what it tells you about the materials tested.

Day 3: Materials Failure Report

Describe what happens when a material is used in the WRONG way — for example, using paper to build a bridge, or using glass to make a car tyre. For 5 different wrong choices, explain: what would fail? What property is wrong? What material should be used instead and why?

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Materials Testing Lab Book

Conduct a systematic investigation of 10 materials using at least 6 different property tests. Create a professional-looking 'Lab Book' that includes:

  • A materials data table (materials vs. properties)
  • 5 graphs or visual displays of your results
  • A 'best material for each job' section with recommendations for 5 different uses
  • A written conclusion discussing what you learned and what surprised you
🏠

Homework Activities

  • Concrete and steel: Why do bridges and buildings use both concrete and steel together (called reinforced concrete)? Research and draw a diagram.
  • Composite materials: A composite is made by combining two materials to get the best properties of both. Research carbon fibre composites — what are they made of and what makes them special?
  • Materials in sport: How has the use of new materials changed sports equipment? Research how swimsuits, tennis rackets, or running shoes have changed over the past 50 years.
  • Testing to destruction: Research how car manufacturers crash-test their vehicles. Why is it important to test materials to the point of failure?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • When a material is pushed beyond its limits, it fails — sometimes suddenly, sometimes gradually. How do engineers decide what the 'safety margin' should be — how far below the failure point a material is normally used?
  • Shape memory alloys are metals that 'remember' their original shape and return to it when heated. How might this property be useful?
  • Some plastics are stronger than steel and lighter than aluminium. Why aren't all structures made of plastic?
  • Could a material ever be made that is both perfectly rigid (doesn't bend) AND perfectly elastic (always returns to its shape)? Why or why not?
Week 31

⚙️ Choosing the Right Material — Matching Properties to Purpose

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — D1.2 – Evaluate and select materials appropriate for specific uses
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

The ancient Egyptians built pyramids from limestone and granite. The Romans built the Pantheon with concrete and brick. Medieval Europeans built cathedrals with stone. Then came steel, which allowed buildings to reach the sky. Today, architects are using new materials — transparent aluminium, aerogel insulation, and self-healing concrete — to build structures that would have seemed like science fiction 50 years ago.

Each of these choices was not arbitrary. Each civilization used the materials that were available, affordable, and had the right properties for what they needed to build.

The Eiffel Tower paradox: When it was built in 1889, the Eiffel Tower was the tallest structure in the world at 330 metres. It is made from iron — about 7,300 tonnes of it. But if you melted the iron down into a disc the same diameter as the Tower's base, it would only be about 6 centimetres thick. The vast majority of the Tower is empty air. This is a lesson in structural efficiency: the right design and arrangement of material matters as much as the material itself.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Apply knowledge of material properties to make informed material choices
  • Evaluate trade-offs in material selection (no material is perfect for every purpose)
  • Design simple objects using appropriate materials
  • Understand that material choices affect cost, sustainability, and performance
  • Appreciate that material selection is a core engineering skill
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Design Brief Activity

Present your child with 3 'design briefs' — real problems that need a materials solution:

  1. 'Design a container to keep sandwiches dry in the rain and fresh in the heat.'
  2. 'Design a floor covering for a muddy entrance that is easy to clean and comfortable to walk on.'
  3. 'Design a container for a hamster to sleep in that is safe, comfortable, and easy to clean.'

For each brief, first list the required properties, then evaluate available materials against those properties, then recommend the best choice with reasons.

The Trade-Off Lesson

No material is perfect. Reinforce this constantly: 'Glass is great for windows — but what's wrong with it? (Breaks, heavy, expensive.) How have engineers compensated? (Tempered glass, laminated glass, safety glass.)'

📖

The Big Lesson

Materials Selection: The Engineering Process

When engineers design anything — from a toothbrush to a rocket ship — one of the first and most important steps is choosing the right materials. This process is called materials selection, and it involves:

  1. Listing the required properties for the object (What must it do? What conditions will it face?)
  2. Ranking the properties by importance
  3. Evaluating available materials against those properties
  4. Choosing the material with the best overall match
  5. Testing and refining the choice

Trade-offs in Materials Selection

No material has the ideal combination of every property. Trade-offs are inevitable. Steel is strong but heavy. Aluminium is light but weaker. Carbon fibre is strong and light but very expensive. Plastic is cheap and lightweight but breaks down over time and pollutes. Engineers must decide which trade-offs are acceptable for each specific use.

Materials selection in action: The Boeing 787 Dreamliner aircraft uses 50% composite materials (carbon fibre reinforced polymer) by weight — more than any previous commercial aircraft. This made it 20% lighter than a comparable aluminium aircraft, improving fuel efficiency by 20%. But composites cost about 10 times more than aluminium per kilogram. The trade-off (higher cost, lower weight, better fuel economy) was judged acceptable.

Matching Material to Environment

Materials must be chosen for the conditions they will face. A material that works perfectly at room temperature might fail in extreme cold or heat. A material that is strong in dry conditions might become soft and weak when wet. A material that is light and convenient might deteriorate quickly in outdoor weather.

Cost and Availability

The best-performing material is not always chosen. Cost and availability matter enormously. Many buildings use concrete instead of steel for walls, even though steel is stronger, because concrete is much cheaper and the walls don't need to be as strong. In engineering, 'good enough' is often a virtue — an ideal solution that is unaffordably expensive is not actually a solution.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Materials Selection Challenge

You are an engineer. You have been asked to design a school water bottle. It must be: durable (survives being dropped), safe for food contact, easy to clean, lightweight, and reasonably cheap.

Evaluate these four materials against these five criteria. Give each a score of 1–5:

  • Glass
  • Stainless steel
  • Hard plastic (polypropylene)
  • Soft plastic (polyethylene)

Tally the scores. Which material wins? Does it match what most water bottles are actually made of? If not, why might the real world choose differently?

What happens to your recommendation if you add a sixth criterion: environmental impact? Does the 'winner' change?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Design Brief Challenges

Complete all three design briefs from the teaching guide. For each one: list required properties, evaluate 4 materials, recommend the best choice, and explain one trade-off your recommendation makes.

Day 2: Material Substitution Analysis

Choose 5 common objects (a cup, a chair, a window, a shoe, a book). For each one: What material is it made from? Why was that material chosen? What would happen if you substituted a different material? Try 2 different substitutions for each and predict the outcome.

Day 3: Innovative Material Research

Research one of these new materials and write a 'product proposal' explaining what it is, its remarkable properties, and one product you would design using it: aerogel, self-healing concrete, transparent wood, shape-memory polymer, or cellulose nanocrystals.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Materials Selection Engineering Journal

Document five real engineering decisions about materials in your home or community. For each one:

  1. Name the object and what it is made from
  2. List the properties that material must have for that object
  3. Explain why that specific material was likely chosen
  4. Describe one disadvantage of the chosen material
  5. Propose an alternative material and explain what would be gained and lost

Write at least 4 sentences for each object. Illustrate with drawings or photographs (drawn versions are fine).

🏠

Homework Activities

  • The Great Plastic Problem: Plastic is chosen for millions of products because it is cheap, lightweight, and versatile. But it causes enormous environmental harm. Research the plastic pollution problem and what alternatives exist.
  • Bamboo as a building material: In many parts of the world, bamboo is used as a structural material for buildings. Research its properties. Why is bamboo sometimes better than steel or concrete?
  • Materials in medicine: What materials are used for hip replacements, artificial heart valves, and dental fillings? What properties must these materials have that are unique to their use inside the human body?
  • Stone Age to Space Age: Create a timeline of the most important materials discoveries in human history, from earliest stone tools to modern carbon fibre. Label each with the date, the material, and why it was important.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Is there a 'perfect' material? Research metamaterials — engineered materials with properties not found in nature, like negative refractive index or negative density. What could these do?
  • The search for room-temperature superconductors has lasted 100 years. What would finding one mean for materials and technology?
  • Natural materials evolved over billions of years of selection. Are biological materials fundamentally better than anything humans can make? Or can we eventually surpass nature?
  • Additive manufacturing (3D printing) can create objects with internal structures that would be impossible to make by traditional methods. How does this change materials selection?
Week 31–32

🏗️ Structures Everywhere — What Is a Structure?

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — D2.1 – Identify and describe different types of structures in the natural and built environment
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

A spider's web. The Eiffel Tower. A bird's nest. A human skeleton. A beaver dam. The Arc de Triomphe. A honeycomb. A termite mound. What do all of these have in common?

They are all structures — assemblies of materials arranged to serve a function while standing up against forces that try to pull them down, push them apart, twist them, or crush them.

The termite mound surprise: Termite mounds in Africa can be 9 metres tall and have been standing for decades. Inside, they have a sophisticated ventilation system of tunnels that maintains a constant temperature of 31°C regardless of outside temperature — in climates that swing from 3°C to 42°C. Architects have studied this to design energy-efficient buildings. Nature has been building structures for 400 million years; we are still learning from her.

Today we open our eyes to the world of structures — and you will never look at a building, a bridge, or a tree the same way again.

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Define structure and identify examples in natural and built environments
  • Classify structures as solid, frame, or shell structures
  • Identify the purpose and main features of different structures
  • Observe how structures are designed to withstand forces
  • Appreciate the diversity of structural solutions in nature and human engineering
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Structure Walk

Take a walk around your neighbourhood (or look out the window if you cannot go out). Identify every structure you can see. Challenge: can you find examples of all three types? After the walk, ask: 'What do all of these structures have in common? What forces are they resisting?'

Natural vs. Built Structures Comparison

Choose one natural structure and one built structure that serve a similar purpose (e.g., a bird's nest vs. a house; a tree vs. a column; a skull vs. a helmet). Compare them: How are they designed similarly? How are they different? What can human engineers learn from the natural structure?

📖

The Big Lesson

What Is a Structure?

A structure is an arrangement of materials that is put together for a purpose and is designed to withstand forces (pushes and pulls). Structures can be natural (built by living things or geological processes) or built (made by humans).

Three Types of Structures

Solid structures are made mostly of solid material with no empty spaces inside. They are very strong but also very heavy and use a lot of material. Examples: a brick wall, a concrete dam, a cliff, a tree trunk.

Frame structures are made of connected pieces (frame members) that form a rigid skeleton, with open spaces between the members. They use material very efficiently — strong but not heavy. Examples: a building's steel skeleton, a bicycle, a spider's web frame, your skeleton.

Shell structures are made of a thin outer layer (the shell) that encloses and protects a space. The curved shape gives strength without requiring much material. Examples: an egg, a tortoise shell, a helmet, a dome building, a car body.

The egg as engineering marvel: An egg shell is only 0.3 mm thick, yet it can withstand the weight of a sitting hen (several kilograms). When you hold an egg between your palms and squeeze from the ends, you are applying compressive force evenly around the shell — and it is almost impossible to crush. The curved shape distributes force evenly around the surface. But poke it from one side and it breaks easily — the force is not distributed.

Forces on Structures

All structures must resist forces — pushes and pulls from loads (the things they are designed to hold or support) and from the environment (wind, water, earthquakes, temperature changes).

A bridge must resist the weight of vehicles (downward force), wind (sideways force), and the forces of expansion and contraction as temperature changes. A building must resist its own weight, the weight of everything inside, and wind forces.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Structure Observation and Classification

Look around your home and identify:

  • Two solid structures
  • Two frame structures
  • Two shell structures

For each one: What forces is it resisting? What material is it made from? What would happen if one part of it failed?

Your own skeleton is a frame structure. Your skull is a shell structure. Your teeth are solid structures. How has evolution designed your body to use all three types efficiently?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Structure Catalogue

Create an illustrated catalogue of structures in your home, neighbourhood, and the natural world. Include at least 12 different structures. For each: type (solid/frame/shell), material, purpose, and what forces it withstands. Use drawings or detailed descriptions.

Day 2: Design a Structure

Design a structure for ONE of these purposes: a shelter for a small animal, a bridge across a small stream, or a tower for a bird to nest in. Draw your design from at least two angles. Label all parts and explain what type of structure it is and what forces it must withstand.

Day 3: Natural Structures Report

Research one of these natural structures in detail: a termite mound, a honeybee hive, a beaver dam, a coral reef, or a bird's nest. Write a report: How is it built? What materials are used? What structural type is it? What can human engineers learn from it?

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Structures of the World Research Project

Research 5 famous structures from different parts of the world and different time periods (e.g., the Colosseum, the Sydney Opera House, the Great Wall of China, the Millau Viaduct, the Burj Khalifa). For each structure:

  1. When and where was it built?
  2. What structural type is it?
  3. What materials are used?
  4. What engineering challenges did builders face?
  5. What makes it remarkable or innovative?

Present your research as a 'World Structures Field Guide' with illustrations for each structure. At least 1 page per structure.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Gothic cathedrals: Medieval cathedral builders developed flying buttresses to support their extraordinarily thin stone walls. Research what a flying buttress is and why it was such an important structural innovation.
  • The Sagrada Família: Research this unusual cathedral in Barcelona, designed by Antoni Gaudí. How did Gaudí use mathematics and nature to design its structure? What makes it so distinctive?
  • Skyscrapers: How do skyscrapers resist wind forces? Research 'tuned mass dampers' — huge pendulums inside skyscrapers that counteract swaying.
  • Earthquake engineering: How do engineers design buildings to survive earthquakes? Research base isolation systems.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • The Pantheon in Rome was built in 125 CE and still stands — nearly 2,000 years later, with no steel reinforcement. How did Roman engineers build such a durable concrete dome without modern materials or computers?
  • Many ancient structures are still standing, while some modern structures fail within decades. What makes some structures so durable?
  • Could you build a structure on Mars? What challenges would you face? (Hint: Mars gravity is 38% of Earth's, there is no liquid water, and there are huge dust storms.)
  • The concept of 'tensegrity' (tensional integrity) creates structures from tension and compression members. Research Buckminster Fuller and tensegrity structures — why do they seem to defy gravity?
Week 32

💪 What Makes Structures Strong? — Forces, Loads, and Supports

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — D2.2 – Investigate how structures are designed to withstand forces
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

On March 11, 2011, a magnitude 9.0 earthquake struck off the coast of Japan — one of the most powerful ever recorded. The shaking was so intense that it moved the island of Honshu 2.4 metres eastward and shifted Earth's axis slightly.

Yet many modern Japanese buildings survived the earthquake with minimal damage. Some older structures collapsed. The difference? Engineers had designed the modern buildings to resist exactly these forces.

Japan's engineering lesson: Japan experiences about 1,500 earthquakes per year. This has made Japanese structural engineers among the most innovative in the world. The same forces that destroy buildings can be managed, redirected, and absorbed — if the structure is designed correctly. Engineering is not about avoiding forces; it is about understanding them and designing to survive them.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Identify the main forces that act on structures: compression, tension, torsion, shear, bending
  • Understand how structural design responds to these forces
  • Investigate how base, joints, and symmetry affect structural strength
  • Test how adding supports or triangulation improves stability
  • Apply force knowledge to explain structural failures and successes
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Forces Vocabulary Introduction

Use physical demonstrations for each force type:

  • Compression: Press a sponge. 'Squeezing forces are called compression.'
  • Tension: Pull a rubber band. 'Stretching forces are called tension.'
  • Torsion: Twist a cloth. 'Twisting forces are called torsion.'
  • Shear: Hold a stack of papers and push the top sideways. 'Sliding forces are called shear.'
  • Bending: Press the middle of a ruler over a gap. 'Bending forces combine tension and compression.'

Triangulation Demo

Make a square from 4 straws and tape. Push one corner — it easily deforms. Now add a diagonal straw across one corner — try again. Much stiffer! The triangle is the strongest shape in 2D because it cannot change shape without changing the length of its sides.

📖

The Big Lesson

Forces That Act on Structures

Every structure faces forces — pushes and pulls from various directions. Understanding these forces is the first step in designing a structure that can survive them.

Compression is a squeezing force. The pillars and columns of a building experience compression from the weight of the floors above them. Concrete is excellent at resisting compression.

Tension is a stretching force. The cables of a suspension bridge experience tension — they are being pulled apart by the weight of the deck. Steel is excellent at resisting tension.

Torsion is a twisting force. A car axle experiences torsion as the engine turns it. Engineers design axles to be circular and hollow to resist torsion efficiently.

Shear is a sliding force, where one part of a structure tends to slide past another. Bolts and rivets at the joints of structures must resist shear.

Bending occurs when a force is applied to the middle of a beam supported at its ends. The bottom of the beam is pulled into tension, the top into compression, and the middle (the 'neutral axis') experiences neither.

How Structures Resist Forces

Structural engineers choose materials, shapes, and arrangements specifically to resist the expected forces:

  • Triangles are used in frames because they cannot change shape without changing member lengths
  • Arches distribute compression forces down and outward along their curve
  • Cables in suspension bridges carry tension forces efficiently
  • Hollow tubes and I-beams resist bending with much less material than solid beams
The I-beam insight: A steel I-beam (shaped like the letter I) uses about 40% less material than a solid rectangular beam of equal strength. The flanges at the top and bottom (the horizontal parts of the I) resist bending efficiently, while the thin web (the vertical part) resists shear. This insight, developed in the 19th century, transformed construction.

Joints and Connections

A structure is only as strong as its weakest connection. Joints (where pieces meet) must be designed to transfer forces without failing. In wooden structures, joints may use glue, nails, screws, or mortise-and-tenon connections. In steel structures, joints may be bolted, riveted, or welded.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Triangulation Investigation

Build three structures from straws and tape or toothpicks and marshmallows:

  • Structure 1: A square frame (4 sides)
  • Structure 2: A square with one diagonal
  • Structure 3: A triangle

Test each one by pressing gently on the sides. Which deforms most? Which is most rigid?

Now build a free-standing tower using ONLY triangles. How tall can you make it before it falls?

Bridges, radio towers, cranes, and bicycles all use triangulated frames. Why do engineers keep returning to this shape?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Forces Drawing

Draw 5 different structures (a house, a bridge, a chair, a tent, a tree). For each one, draw arrows to show where the main forces act. Label each arrow with the type of force (compression, tension, shear, bending, torsion).

Day 2: Tower Build Challenge

Using only 20 straws and 1 metre of tape, build the tallest free-standing tower you can. Test it by placing a small weight (like a rubber eraser) on top. Document: your design strategy, how many times it fell down, your final height, and what structural principles you used.

Day 3: Structural Failure Analysis

Research or imagine two structural failures (e.g., a bookshelf that collapsed, a bridge that swayed). For each one: Describe what failed. Identify what forces caused the failure. Explain how the design could be improved to prevent future failure.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Structural Strength Investigation

Investigate: How does adding triangles change the load-bearing capacity of a flat paper structure?

Build paper bridges using only paper and tape, spanning a 20 cm gap. Test Version 1 with a flat sheet of paper. Test Version 2 with the paper folded into a corrugated (zigzag) shape. Test Version 3 with paper tubes (roll strips into tubes). Test Version 4 with your own triangulated design.

Place small weights (coins) on each bridge until it fails. Record the number of coins it held. Write a full investigation report.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Arch structures: Roman aqueducts used arches to span long distances and still stand 2,000 years later. Research how an arch distributes forces and why it was such an important invention.
  • Tensegrity: Research tensegrity structures. Why does a tensegrity structure feel like it is floating?
  • Earthquake resistance: Research base isolation systems. How does allowing a building's foundation to move actually make it stronger in an earthquake?
  • Biomimicry in structures: Research the lotus effect, the nacre effect (abalone shell), and the gecko foot effect. How are these being applied to building materials?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Topology optimization — using computers to evolve an ideal structural shape for a given set of forces — produces shapes that look organic and strange. Why does the computer-optimized solution look like a bone?
  • Structures on Earth are designed for Earth's gravity. How would you design a skyscraper on the Moon (gravity 1/6 of Earth)? What would change?
  • The Wright Brothers' first aeroplane had to be both a structure (resisting forces) and a mechanism (moving its parts). How do modern aircraft wings balance being strong structures while flexing in flight?
  • If you were made of steel, how would your skeleton need to be different?
Week 33

🔺 Shapes and Strength — Geometry Meets Engineering

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — D2.2 – Investigate how shape affects structural strength
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

A flat sheet of paper cannot support even a pencil. But fold that same sheet of paper into a tube and it can support a book. The paper hasn't changed. Not one gram of material has been added. Only the shape has changed.

Shape is the secret of structural engineering. The Great Pyramid of Giza has stood for 4,500 years partly because the pyramid shape distributes its enormous weight in the most efficient possible way. The arch — which Roman engineers used brilliantly — can span gaps that a flat beam of the same material and weight could never cross. The honeycomb hexagonal pattern uses the least amount of material to divide a flat surface into compartments — a mathematical proof, and nature discovered it millions of years before humans.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Investigate how folding, rolling, and bending changes a material's strength
  • Identify strong shapes in nature and engineering: triangles, arches, cylinders, hexagons
  • Explain why certain shapes are stronger than others using force concepts
  • Test the load-bearing capacity of different cross-section shapes
  • Apply shape knowledge to design a stronger structure
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Paper Shape Challenge

The core experiment: give your child 10 identical strips of paper. Challenge them to use different shapes (flat, folded, rolled, corrugated, triangular prism, etc.) to see which shape can support the most weight (use coins as weights). This single experiment teaches more about structural geometry than many pages of text.

Shape in Nature Observation

Look for geometric shapes in structures around you: hexagons in a beehive or soccer ball, triangles in a bridge or roof truss, arches in bridges and windows, cylinders in columns and trees.

📖

The Big Lesson

Why Shape Matters

When a force is applied to a structure, the shape of that structure determines how the force is distributed throughout the material. A well-chosen shape can distribute forces widely and efficiently; a poor shape concentrates forces at one point, leading to failure.

The Power of the Triangle

A triangle is the only polygon that cannot change shape without changing the length of its sides. When you push on a corner of a triangle, the force is distributed through both sides. This is why triangles appear everywhere in engineering: roof trusses, bridge frames, bicycle frames, radio towers, building frameworks.

Cylinders and Tubes

A tube or cylinder is excellent at resisting both compression (squeezing from above) and bending (forces from the side). Under compression, the force is distributed evenly around the circumference of the tube. This is why columns have been cylindrical or rectangular for thousands of years, and why bones — which must resist bending — are hollow tubes of dense bone with a lighter core.

The Arch

An arch works by converting the downward weight (compression) into forces that push outward and downward along the arch's curve. At every point in the arch, the material is in compression. Stone and concrete — which are very strong under compression — work perfectly in arches. The Romans understood this intuitively and built arches, vaults, and domes that have stood for 2,000 years.

The Hexagon

The regular hexagon is the most efficient way to divide a flat surface into equal cells using the least amount of shared wall material. Bees discovered this millions of years ago and build their combs in hexagons. The hexagonal pattern also appears in basalt rock formations (like the Giant's Causeway in Northern Ireland) formed by the natural cooling and cracking of lava.

Corrugation: Corrugated cardboard (the zigzag layer inside a cardboard box) gains its stiffness and strength from its shape alone. The zigzag creates triangular cross-sections that resist bending. Corrugated metal roofing works the same way. A material that would be easily bent when flat becomes remarkably strong when corrugated.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Cross-Section Shape Investigation

Make four different cross-section shapes from identical strips of paper or card: flat, L-shaped, U-shaped, and tubular. Place each over a gap and load it with weights until it collapses.

Record results: shape → weight before collapse. Graph your results.

Conclusion: Which shape is strongest per unit of material? Why?

The I-beam used in construction is like two flat strips connected by a thin web. How does this shape manage both compression (top flange) and tension (bottom flange) simultaneously?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Shape Engineering Lab

Complete the paper shape investigation. Record all results in a data table. Draw diagrams of each shape and label why it is strong or weak.

Day 2: Shape Spotting in Nature and Architecture

Create an illustrated guide to 'Strong Shapes in the World.' Find and draw at least 8 examples of strong geometric shapes from both nature and human-made structures. For each one, explain why the shape makes it strong.

Day 3: Arch Construction Activity

Build a simple arch using sugar cubes, cardboard 'keystones,' or cut pieces of styrofoam. Once the arch is complete, test how much weight it can hold at the top. Write about what you discovered about how arches work.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Strongest Shape Engineering Challenge

Using only 30 index cards and 1 metre of tape, design and build the structure that can support the greatest weight over a 25 cm span (bridge challenge). Document your design process fully: 5 different designs you considered, the materials selection and why, your building process with diagrams, your test results, and what you would change next time.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • The Geodesic Dome: Research Buckminster Fuller's geodesic dome. Why is this shape extraordinarily strong and efficient?
  • Islamic architecture: Islamic builders created extraordinarily complex vaulted ceiling patterns using intersecting arches. Research muqarnas. How were they built without computers?
  • The hexagon in science: Hexagons appear in snowflakes, honeycombs, basalt columns, and even the molecular structure of benzene. Why does nature keep returning to this shape?
  • Eggshell engineering: Calculate how many eggs you could stack on top of each other before the bottom one cracks. Research this experiment and try to predict the answer before finding out.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Topology optimization (computer-designed structures) often produces forms that look like bones or coral. Why does optimal engineering design converge on shapes similar to what nature evolved?
  • What is the most structurally efficient shape for a container (same volume, least surface area)? (Answer: a sphere — this is why bubbles are round.) Why don't humans make spherical buildings?
  • Could you build a structure using only triangles that could survive a meteor impact? What are the practical limits of triangulation?
  • A murmuration of starlings (thousands of birds flying together in flowing shapes) has been compared to a fluid. Is the flock itself a kind of structure? What forces hold it together?
Week 33–34

🌉 Bridges — Spanning the Gap

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — D2.3 – Design and build a bridge structure; test and evaluate its load-bearing capacity
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

The Millau Viaduct in France is the tallest bridge in the world — its tallest mast rises 341 metres above the valley below, taller than the Eiffel Tower. Cars drive across it at highway speed, 270 metres above the ground, surrounded by clouds. It spans 2.46 kilometres across the Tarn Valley.

Before the bridge was built, the only way to drive through this valley was along a winding mountain road through the town of Millau, causing massive traffic jams every summer. Engineers spent years designing a structure that could cross this enormous gap efficiently, safely, and beautifully.

Bridges as civilization: A bridge is one of humanity's oldest and most fundamental technologies. Without bridges, cities could not grow, trade could not flow, armies could not march. The ancient Romans built bridges across rivers throughout their empire — and some still stand. The modern world's infrastructure of roads, railways, and pipelines crosses thousands of rivers and valleys on bridges that most people never think about.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Identify the main types of bridges: beam, arch, truss, suspension, cable-stayed
  • Explain how each bridge type handles forces
  • Understand the key engineering considerations in bridge design: span, load, aesthetics, cost
  • Design and build a model bridge with specific performance criteria
  • Test and evaluate the bridge against the criteria
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Bridge Type Tour

Using images from a book or online resource, show your child examples of each bridge type. For each one, ask: 'Where do you think the weight goes? What shape keeps it up?' Then explain the force distribution.

Bridge Build Challenge

Design Challenge Brief: Build a bridge using only the following materials — 20 wooden craft sticks, 1 metre of masking tape, and 4 sheets of card — that can span exactly 30 cm and support the most coins possible. Set up the gap using two stacks of books. Test each bridge design carefully.

📖

The Big Lesson

The Bridge: Humanity's Greatest Structural Achievement

A bridge must solve one problem: span a gap while supporting a load. But solving this problem elegantly — efficiently, safely, affordably, and beautifully — is one of engineering's greatest challenges.

Types of Bridges

Beam bridges are the simplest — a horizontal beam supported at each end. The beam resists bending: the top is in compression, the bottom in tension. Beam bridges work well for short spans but need more and more material as the span increases.

Arch bridges convert the load into compression forces that travel down and outward along the arch's curve. They are self-supporting (the arch pushes outward against its supports) and very efficient for medium spans. The Roman aqueducts used arch bridges.

Truss bridges use a triangulated frame (truss) to efficiently distribute forces. Trusses use much less material than a solid beam of equal strength. The pattern of triangles creates both tension and compression members that balance each other.

Suspension bridges hang the deck from cables attached to tall towers. The cables are in tension — they are being pulled from both ends. Suspension bridges can span very long distances because the cables carry the load so efficiently. The Golden Gate Bridge in San Francisco (1,280 m span) is a famous example.

Cable-stayed bridges use cables attached directly from the tower to the deck — a different arrangement from suspension bridges. They are faster to build than suspension bridges and are now the most common type for medium-long spans.

Bridge records: The Akashi Kaikyō Bridge in Japan holds the record for the longest suspension bridge span: 1,991 metres between towers. It was designed to withstand earthquakes of magnitude 8.5 and 80 m/s winds. During its construction in 1995, the Great Hanshin earthquake (magnitude 7.3) moved the two towers 1 metre further apart — engineers simply adjusted their plans.

Bridge Engineering Considerations

Real bridge engineers consider: the distance to span, the loads to carry (pedestrians, cars, trains, wind, snow), the ground conditions (solid rock vs. soft soil), the available materials and budget, and the aesthetic impact. No single type of bridge is best for all situations.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Bridge Load Analysis

Look at a photograph of a real bridge. Identify: What type of bridge is it? Where are the main load-carrying members? Where is compression occurring? Where is tension? What would happen if one member failed?

The I-35W bridge in Minneapolis collapsed in 2007, killing 13 people. Investigators found that inadequate gusset plates (the metal connectors at joints) had failed. Why is every joint in a truss bridge so critical? What does this tell us about the importance of materials and connections?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Bridge Types Comparison Chart

Create a detailed comparison chart for all 5 bridge types. For each: draw and label a diagram, explain force distribution with arrows, name a famous real-world example, and list advantages and limitations.

Days 2–3: Build and Test Bridge

Complete the bridge build challenge from the teaching guide. Document: your initial design sketch, why you chose that design, problems you encountered and how you solved them, your final bridge diagram, and your test results (number of coins held).

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Famous Bridge Research and Presentation

Research one of these famous bridges in detail: Brooklyn Bridge, Golden Gate Bridge, Tower Bridge (London), Millau Viaduct, or the Forth Bridge (Scotland). Create a detailed presentation (at least 5 illustrated pages) covering:

  1. History: when, why, and how it was built
  2. Engineering: what type, what materials, what innovations
  3. Challenges: what obstacles had to be overcome during construction
  4. Impact: how did it change the region it serves?
  5. Future: is it still adequate for current traffic? What modifications have been made?
🏠

Homework Activities

  • Bridge disasters: Research the collapse of the Tacoma Narrows Bridge in 1940. What caused it to collapse? What engineering principle did engineers learn from this failure?
  • Human bridge: Is it possible to build a bridge out of living people — a chain of people supporting each other? Research 'human bridge' world records.
  • Roman aqueducts: Roman aqueducts are essentially long bridges carrying water. Research how Roman engineers built them without modern technology. How did they maintain the precise slope needed for water to flow by gravity?
  • Biobridge: In Meghalaya, India, people have been growing living bridges from the roots of rubber fig trees for centuries. Research these 'living bridges' and how they are grown.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • The Øresund Bridge between Denmark and Sweden transitions into an underwater tunnel midway. Why? (Ships must be able to pass.) What are the engineering challenges of an underwater tunnel?
  • What are the theoretical limits of a suspension bridge's span? Could you build a bridge across the Strait of Gibraltar (14 km)? The English Channel (33 km)?
  • A bridge must last decades or centuries. How do engineers account for fatigue — the weakening of materials over millions of load cycles?
  • Could you build a bridge on Mars? What design considerations would change in low gravity with no liquid water?
Week 34

🗼 Towers — Reaching for the Sky

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — D2.3 – Design and build a tower structure; investigate stability and height
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Why do humans build towers? They are expensive, difficult, and unnecessary for basic shelter. Yet every culture throughout history has built them: the Tower of Babel (ancient legend), the Lighthouse of Alexandria (wonder of the ancient world), the great minarets of Islamic architecture, the medieval church spires of Europe, and today's glass-and-steel skyscrapers.

The world's tallest building today is the Burj Khalifa in Dubai — 828 metres tall, with 163 floors. Engineers had to solve a unique problem: the Sun rises on the east side hours before the west side. Because different parts of the tower are heated by the Sun at different times, different parts expand and contract by different amounts. The building actually 'breathes' with the Sun as it moves across the sky. This thermal expansion of a 828-metre tower can move the top by several centimetres.

What forces are pushing DOWN on a tower? What forces are trying to push it SIDEWAYS? And how do engineers keep something 828 metres tall from falling over?

🎯

Learning Goals

  • Identify the forces that act on towers: gravity (downward), wind (lateral), foundation forces
  • Understand the importance of a wide base for stability
  • Investigate how tower design changes as height increases
  • Design and build a tower for maximum height and stability
  • Understand how skyscrapers dampen swaying
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Stability Investigation

Before building towers, explore base stability. Stack identical blocks in a column, then make a pyramid shape. Which is more stable? Why? Ask: 'If you were a building, you would want to be wide at the base and narrow at the top — why?'

Tower Challenge

Challenge Brief: Using only 30 index cards, 40 cm of tape, and scissors, build the tallest free-standing tower you can. It must: stand independently for at least 30 seconds, withstand one gentle push, and support a coin on top. Document each design attempt.

📖

The Big Lesson

The Challenge of Height

Any structure must resist gravity — the downward force of its own weight. But as a structure gets taller, it faces an additional challenge: any sideways force (like wind) creates an increasing moment — a tendency to tip over — as the structure gets taller. The taller the structure, the longer the 'lever arm' of the wind force, and the greater the tipping moment.

The Base

The most fundamental principle of tower design is: a wide, heavy base increases stability. The Eiffel Tower and the Great Pyramid both have very wide bases that taper as they rise. Modern skyscrapers achieve stability through deep foundations and wide floor plates.

How Skyscrapers Resist Wind

Modern skyscrapers can sway several metres at their top in strong winds. This is actually intentional — a building that is too rigid might crack under wind loads. But the sway must be controlled, or it would be nauseating for occupants.

Engineers use several strategies: tuned mass dampers (massive pendulums or weights inside the building that counteract swaying), shear walls (stiff concrete walls that resist lateral forces), outrigger systems (connections from the building's core to its outer columns), and aerodynamic shaping (designing the building's profile to reduce wind loads).

Taipei 101's ball: The Taipei 101 skyscraper in Taiwan contains a massive steel sphere weighing 660 tonnes, hanging from the 92nd to 87th floor. When the building sways in typhoon winds or earthquakes, this 'tuned mass damper' swings in the opposite direction, counteracting the motion. Visitors can see it from observation decks. During Typhoon Soudelor in 2015, the ball was observed swinging 100 centimetres to counteract the building's sway.

Foundations

A skyscraper is only as good as its foundation. Before construction begins, engineers study the ground to understand: how deep solid rock is, how much weight the soil can carry, and whether there is groundwater. Many skyscrapers in cities like Singapore or Shanghai are built on soft clay — they require hundreds of deep concrete piles driven down to bedrock.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Stability Testing

Build three towers: one with a narrow base, one with a medium base, and one with a wide base. Make them all the same height. Test each by gently pushing on the side with a finger.

Which falls most easily? Which is most stable?

Now: can you make the wide-base tower taller before it becomes as unstable as the narrow-base tower?

A tall tree is like a tower — it has a wide base (trunk), a branching frame, and enormous flexibility (it can bend in the wind without breaking). How has evolution solved the same structural problems that building engineers face?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Famous Towers Research Chart

Research 6 famous towers from history and today. For each: name, location, height, year built, main structural feature, what makes it remarkable. Present as an illustrated comparison chart.

Days 2–3: Tower Build and Evaluate

Complete the tower challenge. Try at least 3 different designs. For each attempt: draw the design, record the height, record whether it passed the stability test. Write a final reflection comparing your best design to a professional skyscraper design.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Skyscraper Design Project

Design your own skyscraper for a fictitious city. Your design must include:

  1. An exterior elevation drawing (side view) from at least two angles
  2. A floor plan (top view) for a typical floor
  3. A foundation cross-section diagram
  4. A written description of the structural system
  5. An explanation of how it resists wind forces
  6. The building's purpose (offices, apartments, hotel, mixed-use?)
  7. An explanation of the materials chosen for the main structure

Present as an architect's proposal package — your best artistic and technical work.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • The Leaning Tower of Pisa: The Tower of Pisa leans because its foundation settled unevenly. How do modern engineers straighten it without it falling? (Research the ongoing stabilization project.)
  • Supertall skyscrapers: Research the Jeddah Tower (under construction in Saudi Arabia) — planned to be 1 km tall. What unique engineering challenges does going over 1 km tall create?
  • Ancient towers: The ziggurats of ancient Mesopotamia and the towers of medieval Italian city-states were both status symbols. Why did these cultures build towers? What did height mean to them?
  • Radio and communications towers: The tallest structures in the world are actually radio/TV broadcast towers, not buildings. Why are they taller than skyscrapers, and what structural challenges does a communications tower face?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Would it ever be possible to build a 'space elevator' — a tower extending all the way to geostationary orbit, 36,000 km above Earth? What would it need to be made of? (No known material is strong enough yet.)
  • The Burj Khalifa sways a few metres at its top during high winds. Occupants rarely notice because the movement is so slow. How much sway is too much — what is the human body's threshold for perceiving motion?
  • Trees routinely survive hurricane-force winds that would knock over many human-built towers. What structural principles allow trees to survive? Why don't engineers simply copy trees exactly?
  • If you could build a tower anywhere on Earth, where would you build it and why?
Week 35

♻️ Materials and the Environment — The Impact of Our Choices

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — D1.3 – Describe the environmental impact of materials use and disposal
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In 2013, a clothing factory called Rana Plaza in Bangladesh collapsed, killing 1,134 people. The collapse was caused by structural failures — building on unsafe ground, using poor-quality materials, and ignoring safety warnings. But deeper than the structural cause, the disaster revealed a global economic system: the clothes worn by millions of people in wealthy countries are manufactured in buildings like this one, by workers earning very little, with few safety protections.

The materials we use have stories — where they come from, who made them, what impact they had on the environment. Understanding these stories is one of the most important things a citizen of the modern world can do.

The hidden life of a T-shirt: A typical cotton T-shirt requires about 2,700 litres of water to produce — enough drinking water for one person for 900 days. It may involve farmers in India, spinners in Pakistan, weavers in China, dye workers in Bangladesh, and retailers across the world before it reaches your wardrobe. Then many people wear it only 7 times before throwing it away. Is there a better way?
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand the environmental impacts of material extraction, manufacturing, use, and disposal
  • Understand the concept of a material's 'life cycle'
  • Distinguish between biodegradable and non-biodegradable materials
  • Identify problems caused by waste materials, especially plastics
  • Recognize the importance of reducing material use and choosing sustainable options
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Life Cycle Analysis Activity

Choose one common object from your home — a plastic water bottle or a cardboard juice box. Together, trace its complete life cycle: raw material extraction → manufacturing → transportation → use → disposal/recycling. At each stage, ask: What energy is used? What waste is created? What impact is there on the environment?

Waste Audit

Spend one day saving all your non-food waste in a bag (wash it first if necessary). At the end of the day, sort it: recyclable paper, recyclable plastic, recyclable metal, recyclable glass, non-recyclable plastic, food waste, other. Weigh each pile if possible. Discuss: How much of this could have been avoided? What could be composted, reused, or chosen differently?

📖

The Big Lesson

Where Do Materials Come From?

Every material has a history. Wood comes from trees — forests must be logged and transported. Metal comes from ore — rock must be mined, processed, and refined at enormous energy cost. Plastic comes from petroleum — which must be drilled, refined, and processed chemically. Even 'natural' materials like cotton require vast farmland, water, and pesticides to produce.

The extraction of raw materials often damages ecosystems: forests are cleared, rivers are polluted by mine runoff, habitats are destroyed. Understanding where materials come from is the first step in using them responsibly.

The Life Cycle of a Material

A material's life cycle includes: extraction from the earth → processing and manufacturing → transportation → use → end of life (recycling, landfill, or decomposition). At every stage, energy is used and waste is produced.

Biodegradable vs. Non-biodegradable

Biodegradable materials can be broken down by microorganisms (bacteria and fungi) back into natural elements. Wood, paper, cotton, and food waste are biodegradable. Non-biodegradable materials cannot be broken down by microorganisms. Most plastics take 400–1,000 years to decompose. Glass takes perhaps 1 million years. Some materials essentially never decompose under natural conditions.

The Great Pacific Garbage Patch: There is a swirling area in the North Pacific Ocean containing an estimated 80,000 tonnes of plastic — mostly microplastics (tiny fragments). It covers an area twice the size of Texas. Plastics break into smaller and smaller fragments but never truly decompose. These microplastics are now found in fish, in drinking water, in the blood of humans, and even in remote Arctic snow.

The Problem of Plastic

Plastic is an extraordinary material — cheap, lightweight, strong, and versatile. Since plastic was invented in 1907, it has transformed modern life. But because it is non-biodegradable and produced in enormous quantities, plastic waste has become one of the greatest environmental challenges of the 21st century. About 8 million tonnes of plastic enter the ocean every year.

What Can Be Done?

Solutions include: reducing the amount of material we use (fewer things, simpler packaging), reusing items rather than disposing after one use, recycling materials at the end of their use, choosing sustainable materials that have lower environmental impact, and redesigning products so they are easier to repair, reuse, and recycle — called circular design.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Life Cycle Comparison

Compare the life cycle of a paper cup vs. a ceramic mug. Draw a table: Stage | Paper Cup | Ceramic Mug.

Consider: extraction, manufacturing, weight/transportation, use (how many times?), end of life.

Which has a lower environmental impact over 1 year of use? Over 10 years?

A ceramic mug requires more energy to make than a paper cup. But it can be used thousands of times. At what point (how many uses) does the ceramic mug become more environmentally efficient?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Material Life Cycle Poster

Create a large poster tracing the complete life cycle of one material (your choice). Include every stage from raw material to disposal. At each stage, note: what energy is used, what pollution is created, and what could be done to reduce the impact.

Day 2: Waste Audit Analysis

Complete the waste audit from the teaching guide. Write a report: What was your family's biggest waste category? What was most surprising? List 5 specific changes your family could make to reduce waste. Research: What happens to recyclables after they leave your blue bin?

Day 3: Biodegradable Experiment Setup

Bury 4 small items in separate pots of soil: a piece of apple, a piece of paper, a piece of plastic bag, a small piece of aluminium foil. Water them like plants. Check every week for 4 weeks. Predict: Which will decompose? In what order?

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Circular Economy Design Challenge

Choose one product that currently ends up in landfill (e.g., a disposable coffee cup, a plastic straw, a single-use plastic bag). Research how it is currently made and what happens when it is thrown away.

Then redesign the product or system so it fits the circular economy:

  1. Design the product to be made from sustainable/recycled materials
  2. Design it for longevity (lasts longer)
  3. Design it for repairability (can be fixed instead of thrown away)
  4. Design it for recyclability (easy to break into recyclable components)
  5. Propose a 'take-back' system for the product

Present your redesign with drawings, descriptions, and an explanation of how it improves the environmental impact.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • The circular economy: Research the concept of the circular economy. How is it different from the current 'take-make-dispose' economy?
  • Fast fashion: Research the environmental impact of the fashion industry. What is 'fast fashion' and why is it a problem? What alternatives exist?
  • Recycling reality check: Research what actually happens to recycling. Is recycling always the best environmental choice? When is it better to reuse than recycle?
  • Bioplastics: Research bioplastics — plastics made from plants rather than petroleum. Are they the solution to the plastic problem? What are their limitations?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Some scientists believe that all materials we use should eventually be able to return to the natural world as nutrients — nothing should ever be 'waste.' Is this practically possible?
  • Could we ever clean up the Great Pacific Garbage Patch? Research the Ocean Cleanup Project — what approach are they using, and is it working?
  • In a world of 10 billion people all wanting modern lifestyles, can the planet provide enough materials sustainably? What would need to change?
  • If you could redesign the way humans make and use materials from scratch — knowing what you know now — what would you change most fundamentally?
Week 35–36

🌿 Recycling and Redesign — Engineering a Better Future

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — D1.3 – Describe strategies for reducing material waste; redesign using recycled materials
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In 1972, a scientist named William McDonough was designing a building when he realized something: nature produces no waste. When a leaf falls from a tree, it becomes food for fungi and bacteria, which become nutrients in the soil, which feed the tree's roots. Everything is cycled back. Nothing is wasted.

McDonough began asking: what if we designed buildings — and eventually all manufactured products — the same way? What if, instead of planning for a product's end in a landfill, we planned for its end as the beginning of another product?

The Cradle to Cradle concept: McDonough and chemist Michael Braungart developed a design philosophy called 'Cradle to Cradle' — every material in a product is designed to be either safely returned to nature (biological nutrients) or safely returned to industry (technical nutrients). No waste. No toxins. No landfill. Some companies have already created products using this principle — including some Nike shoes and Herman Miller office chairs. The question is: can we scale this to the entire economy?
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand why redesigning products and systems is more effective than just recycling
  • Learn about upcycling and repurposing as strategies
  • Apply creative and engineering thinking to redesign a product using waste materials
  • Understand the Reduce-Reuse-Recycle hierarchy
  • Connect materials design choices to long-term sustainability
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

The 3Rs Hierarchy

Clarify the hierarchy: Reducing (using less in the first place) is always better than Reusing (using something again for the same or different purpose), which is always better than Recycling (breaking down materials to make new things). Recycling still uses energy and produces some waste — it is the last resort, not the first solution.

Junk Modelling Activity

Collect a bag of clean 'junk': cardboard tubes, bottle caps, tin cans, boxes, plastic bottles, newspaper, egg cartons. Challenge: build a structure or model that demonstrates one of the engineering principles from Strand D (strong shapes, stable base, etc.) using ONLY these waste materials. No buying new materials.

📖

The Big Lesson

The Reduce-Reuse-Recycle Hierarchy

When thinking about materials and waste, there is a clear priority order:

Reduce (best): Use less material in the first place. Buy fewer things. Choose products with less packaging. Repair instead of replacing. Each product not made is pollution not created.

Reuse: Use things again — either for the same purpose (refillable water bottle instead of single-use) or a different purpose (turning an old tire into a garden planter).

Recycle (last resort): When something cannot be reduced or reused, recycle it — break it down so its materials can be used again. This is better than landfill but still requires energy and produces some waste.

Upcycling

Upcycling is turning waste materials into something of higher value or quality — not just breaking them down, but creatively transforming them. Examples: turning old jeans into a bag, making art from bottle caps, or converting shipping containers into buildings.

Design for Disassembly

Many products are difficult to recycle because they are made of many materials glued or bonded together that cannot be separated. Design for disassembly means designing products so they can be easily taken apart at the end of their life, with each material cleanly separated for recycling. A phone designed this way would have components that snap apart rather than being glued together.

Cradle to Cradle in practice: Steelcase's 'Think' chair is 99% recyclable by weight. Patagonia's 'Worn Wear' program repairs old Patagonia clothing rather than selling new items. LEGO is transitioning to bio-based plastics made from sugarcane rather than petroleum. These companies are designing for the end of a product's life before it is even made.

The Future of Materials

Scientists and engineers are developing new materials that are designed from the start to work within natural cycles: biodegradable electronics, algae-based packaging that dissolves in water, fungal (mushroom) insulation that can be composted at end of life, and paint made from limestone that absorbs CO₂ from the air. The future of materials is not about endless production and disposal — it is about designing materials that work with nature, not against it.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Redesign Challenge

Choose one common product that creates a lot of waste: a disposable plastic bag, a single-use coffee cup, a chip packet, or a plastic bottle.

Research its current design. Then redesign it using what you know about:

  • Sustainable materials
  • Minimal material use
  • Ease of reuse
  • Biodegradability or recyclability

Draw your redesigned product and write an explanation. What is better about your design? What trade-offs did you have to make?

Would your redesigned product cost more or less than the original? How do we make sustainable design affordable for everyone?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Junk Modelling Build

Build your junk model structure (from the teaching guide). Document: what materials you used, what structural principles you applied, what challenges you faced, and what the finished structure can do (what load can it hold? how stable is it?).

Day 2: Product Lifecycle Redesign

Complete the redesign challenge from the Think section. Present your redesign as an engineering drawing with labels and a written design brief.

Day 3: Reduce-Reuse-Recycle Audit

Audit your home for 5 items that are currently being disposed of that could be: reduced in the first place (buy less), reused (repurposed), upcycled (made into something new), or repaired (instead of replaced). Write an action plan.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Upcycling Project

Choose waste materials from your home (cardboard, plastic bottles, fabric scraps, paper, tin cans — whatever you have). Design and build a useful or beautiful object that gives these materials a new life.

Document your project in a Design Journal:

  1. Inspiration: Why this object? What problem does it solve or what beauty does it create?
  2. Materials Inventory: What waste materials did you collect and what are their properties?
  3. Design Sketches: Draw at least 3 design ideas before choosing one
  4. Construction Process: Step-by-step photos or drawings of how you built it
  5. Reflection: What worked well? What would you do differently? How does this connect to what you learned about sustainable design?
🏠

Homework Activities

  • Cradle to Cradle book: Research the book 'Cradle to Cradle' by William McDonough and Michael Braungart. What is its central argument? Do you find it convincing?
  • Repair café: Research the global 'Repair Café' movement. What is it and how does it fight planned obsolescence?
  • Planned obsolescence: Some companies intentionally design products to stop working or become 'outdated' so people will buy new ones. Research planned obsolescence. Is it ethical?
  • Zero waste homes: Research families who try to produce zero waste. How do they manage it? What lifestyle changes do they make? Could your family do it?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Could an entire city be designed to produce zero waste? What would need to change — in manufacturing, in consumption, in infrastructure?
  • Some scientists argue that recycling, as currently practiced, is inefficient and that we should focus instead on using much less material in the first place. Do you agree? Why or why not?
  • Fungi (mushrooms) can decompose almost any organic material and even some plastics. Could we engineer fungi to decompose specific waste products? Research mycoremediation.
  • If materials were more expensive to buy and cheaper to dispose of (the opposite of today), how would consumer behaviour change?
Week 36–37

🏆 Design Challenge — Build Your Best Structure

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — D1.1, D2.1–D2.3 – Apply all Strand D knowledge to a full engineering design challenge
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Elisha Otis is not a household name, but he changed cities more than almost any other inventor. In 1852, he invented the safety elevator — a device that would catch if the cable broke, preventing a deadly fall. Before Otis, buildings rarely went above 5 or 6 stories because no one would walk that many flights of stairs. After the safety elevator, buildings could go to any height. The modern skyscraper was made possible not by a bold new structural idea — but by a small safety device in an elevator.

Real engineering is full of stories like this: the critical innovation is not always the most dramatic one. Today you will go through the full engineering design process, and you will discover that the best solutions often come from careful thinking about the problem, not just building the most impressive-looking thing.

What is the Design Process? Great designers follow a process, not a guess. They understand the problem deeply, research existing solutions, generate many ideas (including wild ones), select the most promising, prototype it (build a rough version), test it against criteria, improve it, and test again. They repeat this cycle — called 'iteration' — until the design meets all criteria. This is exactly what professional engineers, architects, and product designers do every day.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Apply the full engineering design process: problem → research → design → build → test → evaluate → improve
  • Demonstrate understanding of material properties, structural types, forces, and shapes
  • Develop and document multiple design ideas before committing to one
  • Test a design against specific criteria and improve based on results
  • Communicate the design clearly through drawings and written explanations
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Challenge Options — Choose ONE

Present all three options. Let your child choose the one that most excites them (or propose their own with your approval):

  1. The Earthquake-Proof Building: Build a structure at least 30 cm tall that can stand on a wobbly jelly/jello base during 'simulated earthquake' (shake the base back and forth for 10 seconds).
  2. The Weight-Bearing Bridge: Build a bridge spanning exactly 40 cm that supports the maximum possible weight using only 30 craft sticks and 60 cm of tape.
  3. The Tallest Tower: Build the tallest free-standing tower possible using 20 index cards, 40 cm of tape, and 10 paperclips.

Design Process Support

Guide your child through each stage. The most important stage that children often want to skip is multiple design ideas — insist on at least three different designs on paper before building anything. Ask: 'What is strong about this design? What could fail? How could you improve it?'

📖

The Big Lesson

The Engineering Design Process

Engineers use a structured process to solve problems and design solutions. This process has been refined over centuries and is used by engineers around the world:
