Step 1: Define the Problem. What exactly needs to be built? What criteria must it meet? What constraints are there (materials, time, cost, size)?

Step 2: Research. What do we already know? What have others built? What worked and what failed?

Step 3: Brainstorm and Sketch Ideas. Generate as many ideas as possible without judging them yet. Wild ideas are welcome. Sketch each idea.

Step 4: Select the Best Design. Evaluate each idea against the criteria. Consider trade-offs. Choose the most promising design.

Step 5: Build a Prototype. Construct the design from the specified materials. Focus on making it as designed, not improvising as you go.

Step 6: Test. Test the prototype against the specified criteria. Record exactly what happened.

Step 7: Evaluate and Improve. What worked? What didn't? Why? How could you improve it? Go back to Step 3 or 4 and repeat.

Iteration is not failure — it IS the process. The engineers at NASA do not design a rocket once and send it to space. They design, test, redesign, and test again — hundreds or thousands of times. The Wright Brothers flew their first powered aeroplane on December 17, 1903 — but they had been designing and testing gliders for 3 years before that. Thomas Edison tested over 3,000 materials before finding the right filament for his light bulb. Iteration is how engineering actually works.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Reflection and Iteration Plan

After your first build and test, sit down and honestly evaluate:

  • Did your structure meet the criteria? Exactly? Partially? Not at all?
  • What was the weakest part of your design?
  • What would you change in Design Version 2?
  • What principle from Strand D could you apply to improve it?

Now build Version 2. Test again. Did it improve? By how much?

James Dyson built 5,127 prototypes of his cyclone vacuum cleaner before finding the one that worked. How does knowing this change the way you think about 'failure' in your own design process?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Design Development

Complete Steps 1–4 of the design process. Draw at least 3 different design concepts. Evaluate each against the criteria. Select the best and create a final design drawing with labels, dimensions, and material notes.

Days 2–3: Build, Test, and Improve

Build your prototype (Day 2). Test it and document results (Day 2). Analyse what failed and why, then build Version 2 (Day 3). Test again and document the improvement.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Full Engineering Design Report

Produce a professional engineering design report documenting your complete design challenge. The report must include:

  • Title page with your name and challenge title
  • Problem Statement: what you were trying to build and the criteria
  • Research section: relevant structural principles you researched before designing
  • Design sketches: all 3+ concepts with evaluation notes
  • Final design: detailed labelled drawing
  • Build process: step-by-step account of construction
  • Test results: quantitative data (weight held, height achieved, etc.)
  • Evaluation: what worked, what failed, and why
  • Improvements: what you changed in Version 2 and why
  • Conclusion: what you learned about the design process

This is a professional document — neatness, diagrams, and clear writing matter.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Biomimicry in design: Research how one engineering innovation was inspired by nature (e.g., Velcro from burdock seeds, bullet train shape from a kingfisher's beak, swimsuits from shark skin). How exactly did the natural model inspire the engineering solution?
  • Design thinking: Research 'design thinking' as a process used in technology companies. How does it compare to the engineering design process you used?
  • Failed designs that led to success: Research one famous design failure that led to an important discovery or improvement (e.g., the Tacoma Narrows Bridge, the Hubble Space Telescope's flawed mirror, early airplane designs).
  • Design for all: Research 'universal design' — designing products and spaces to be usable by as many people as possible, including people with disabilities. Give three examples.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • The engineering design process is about solving defined problems. But who decides what the problem is? Could better-defined problems lead to better solutions for society's real challenges?
  • Artificial intelligence can now generate thousands of design iterations in seconds, testing each computationally. Will AI replace human designers, or will human creativity and judgment always be needed?
  • Some of the most important technologies started as 'solutions looking for a problem' — inventions before anyone knew what to use them for. The internet and the laser both started this way. What technology today might find its true purpose 20 years from now?
  • What is the most important design problem facing humanity right now? How would you approach solving it?
Week 37

🌟 Showcase & Review — Structural Engineers in the Making

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — D – Consolidation of all Strand D expectations
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

You have been on a journey through the world of structures and materials. You have held materials in your hands and understood their secret properties. You have built towers and bridges and tested them to their limits. You have traced where materials come from and where they go. You have thought like an engineer.

Now here is a thought: everything human-made in the world — every building, every road, every phone, every ship, every chair, every cup — was designed and built by someone using the principles you have studied. The world is made of designed things. And designed things can always be redesigned — better, more sustainably, more beautifully.

The greatest structures not yet built: Imagine a city floating on the ocean. A bridge crossing the Strait of Gibraltar. A space elevator. A solar panel array the size of a small country in the Sahara. These are not impossible — they are engineering challenges waiting for the next generation of materials, the next generation of engineers. That generation might be yours.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Review and demonstrate mastery of all Strand D concepts
  • Connect materials and structures learning to real-world applications
  • Communicate understanding through presentation and portfolio
  • Reflect on the engineering design process and personal growth
  • Celebrate achievement and prepare for future engineering thinking
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Showcase Event

Organise a mini 'Engineering Exhibition.' Set up displays of the child's best work from Strand D — their strongest bridge or tower, their design reports, their material samples collection, their upcycling project. Invite family members as 'visitors.' Your child presents each exhibit, explains the science behind it, and answers questions.

Engineer's Portfolio Review

Review the portfolio of work together. Ask: 'Which piece of work are you most proud of? What did you find hardest? What would you build next if you could?'

📖

The Big Lesson

What We Learned in Strand D: A Summary

In Strand D, we explored Structures & Mechanisms: Materials, Objects, and Everyday Structures. Here is what you discovered:

Materials are the substances objects are made from — natural (wood, stone, cotton) or manufactured (glass, plastic, steel). Every material has specific properties — hardness, flexibility, strength, transparency, absorbency, conductivity — that make it suitable for certain purposes.

Materials selection involves matching material properties to the requirements of an object, always involving trade-offs because no material is perfect for every purpose.

Structures are arrangements of materials designed to withstand forces. They can be solid, frame, or shell structures. They exist in both the natural world (skulls, honeycombs, tree trunks) and the built world (bridges, buildings, towers).

Forces acting on structures include compression (squeezing), tension (stretching), shear (sliding), torsion (twisting), and bending. Structural design must address all expected forces.

Shape matters enormously: Triangles are the strongest 2D shape; arches distribute compression efficiently; cylinders resist both compression and bending; hexagons tile a surface with minimal material. Folding, corrugating, and rolling dramatically increase a material's strength.

Bridges come in five main types — beam, arch, truss, suspension, and cable-stayed — each suited to different spans and force conditions.

Towers must resist gravity (downward forces) and lateral forces (wind). Wide bases, deep foundations, and tuned mass dampers are key strategies for tall structures.

Materials and the environment: Every material has a life cycle with environmental impacts. Non-biodegradable materials (especially plastics) create lasting pollution. The Reduce-Reuse-Recycle hierarchy prioritizes reducing use above all else. Circular design envisions products that generate no waste.

The engineering design process: Problem definition → research → brainstorming → design → prototype → test → evaluate → improve. Iteration is not failure — it is the process.

Engineers change the world: The engineers who designed the water treatment systems that made drinking water safe arguably saved more lives than all the doctors in history. The engineers who designed the Green Revolution agricultural technology fed billions. The engineers working on clean energy and water are working on the most important challenges of the next century. This is what structural and materials engineering is — not just building things, but solving humanity's greatest problems, one material and one structure at a time.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

The Grand Engineering Quiz

  1. You are designing a building for a city that experiences severe earthquakes. List 5 structural features you would include and explain why.
  2. Why is a hollow cylinder (tube) stronger per unit of material than a solid cylinder of the same weight?
  3. Explain why the Great Pacific Garbage Patch is so difficult to clean up. What would you do about it?
  4. A client wants a bridge that is cheap, strong, and easy to build. But these three goals are in tension. How do you manage trade-offs in engineering?
  5. Your school wants to build a new outdoor reading area. You have been appointed as the engineer. Walk me through your design process, from problem definition to final design.
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Engineering Portfolio Mind Map

Create a visual mind map of everything you learned in Strand D. Use at least 50 items. Organize it clearly with branches for: Materials, Structures, Forces and Shapes, Bridges, Towers, Environment, and Design Process. Make it beautiful — this is a document to keep.

Day 2: Exhibition Preparation

Prepare your engineering exhibition: select 4 pieces of your best work, write exhibit cards for each (title, what it is, what science it demonstrates, what you learned from making it), and practise explaining each one in 2 minutes.

Day 3: Engineering Letter to Future Self

Write a letter to your future self (in 10 years) about what you learned as a structural engineer. Include: 3 things that surprised you, 2 questions you still have, 1 change you will make in how you use materials, and your vision for what future engineering could accomplish.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Strand D Culminating Project

Choose ONE of the following culminating projects:

  • The Model City: Design and build a model neighbourhood using sustainable principles. Include at least 5 buildings of different structural types, a bridge, and a materials guide explaining what each building is made of and why.
  • The Engineer's Manifesto: Write a 2-page 'manifesto' about the future of building and materials. What principles should guide 21st-century engineers? What materials should we develop? How should we build for sustainability? Support every point with specific examples from what you learned.
  • The Innovation Pitch: Design a new product or structure that solves a real problem in your community. Present it as a pitch to imaginary investors: problem, solution, materials, structural design, environmental impact, and cost estimate.
🏠

Homework Activities

  • Engineering biography: Research an engineer who changed the world: Isambard Kingdom Brunel, Nikola Tesla, Emily Roebling (who completed the Brooklyn Bridge), or James Watt. Write a biography focusing on their most important engineering achievement.
  • Materials of the future: Research 'metamaterials' — engineered materials with extraordinary properties not found in nature. What could they be used for?
  • Engineering ethics: Sometimes engineers are pressured to cut corners on safety to save money. Research the Columbia Space Shuttle disaster (2003). What went wrong? Who knew? What ethical lessons does it teach engineers?
  • Thank you note to an engineer: Write a thank-you note to an unnamed engineer who designed something you use or live with every day. Be specific: what did they design, what engineering challenges did they solve, and how has it improved your life?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • If you had unlimited resources and could build any structure anywhere in the world, what would you build and why?
  • Some futurists propose building 'arcologies' — enormous self-contained structures that house entire cities in a single building. Is this a good idea? What engineering and social challenges would it face?
  • The most important engineering challenge of the next 100 years may be adapting coastal cities to rising sea levels. How would you engineer a flooded city?
  • Engineering and art have always been connected — think of the Sydney Opera House, the Eiffel Tower, the Guggenheim Museum Bilbao. Should engineers be taught art? Should artists be taught engineering?
Reference · All Year

📚 Vocabulary Dictionary

All key science words for Grade 1
📖

Science Word Bank

Learn these important science words. Read each word and its meaning. Can you use the word in your own sentence?

Material
Any substance that objects are made from. Materials can be natural (wood, stone) or manufactured (plastic, glass).
Property
A characteristic of a material that can be observed or measured: hardness, flexibility, strength, absorbency, transparency.
Hardness
How resistant a material is to being scratched or dented. Diamond is the hardest natural material.
Flexibility
How much a material can bend without breaking. Rubber is very flexible; glass breaks instead of bending.
Conductivity
How well a material allows heat or electricity to pass through it. Metals are good conductors; rubber is a good insulator.
Structure
An arrangement of materials designed to withstand forces and serve a purpose. Structures can be natural or built.
Solid Structure
A structure made mostly of solid material with no empty spaces, such as a brick wall or a dam.
Frame Structure
A structure made of connected pieces forming a rigid skeleton with open spaces between, like a building frame or bicycle.
Shell Structure
A structure whose strength comes from a thin, curved outer surface, such as an egg, dome, or helmet.
Compression
A squeezing force that pushes parts of a structure together. Pillars and columns resist compression.
Tension
A stretching force that pulls parts of a structure apart. Suspension bridge cables resist tension.
Torsion
A twisting force acting on a structure. Car axles and drill bits must resist torsion.
Shear
A sliding force where one part of a structure tends to slide past another. Bolts at structural joints resist shear.
Triangulation
Using triangles in a structural frame to create rigidity, because a triangle cannot change shape without changing side lengths.
Arch
A curved structural element that converts downward loads into compression forces along its curve, requiring no tension.
Life Cycle
The complete journey of a material: extraction → manufacturing → use → disposal/recycling.
Biodegradable
Able to be broken down by microorganisms into natural substances. Paper and wood are biodegradable; most plastics are not.
Circular Design
Designing products so that all materials can be safely returned to nature or industry with no waste.
Upcycling
Creatively transforming waste materials into something of higher value, rather than simply recycling them.
Prototype
An early test model of a design, built to test ideas and identify improvements before the final version.

Strand E · Earth & Space Systems
Week 1–2 · Strand E Opens

🌅 Day and Night — Earth's Greatest Rhythm

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E3.1 – Explain how Earth's rotation causes day and night
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Before you go to sleep tonight, consider this: as you lie in your bed, you are hurtling through space at 107,000 kilometres per hour. Your planet is orbiting the Sun at that speed, and you are spinning on its surface at about 1,670 km/h (at the equator). You never feel this speed because everything around you is moving with you.

And as you sleep, the other side of Earth — Asia, Europe, the Pacific — is bathed in sunlight. Billions of people are eating breakfast, going to school, sitting in traffic, while you sleep in darkness. Day and night are not just times of day. They are a dance of spinning and orbiting that has been happening for 4.5 billion years.

The longest day in Earth's history: When Earth first formed, it was spinning much faster — a day was only about 6 hours long. The Moon's gravity has been gradually slowing Earth's rotation ever since. In the distant future, Earth will slow to where one side always faces the Sun — just as the Moon always shows us the same face. Every day you experience is fractionally longer than the day before. By about 1 billionth of a second per year.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Explain that Earth rotates on its axis once every 24 hours
  • Understand that day and night result from Earth's rotation (not the Sun moving)
  • Identify that the Sun appears to rise in the east and set in the west
  • Understand that when it is day on one side of Earth, it is night on the other
  • Use a model (globe and flashlight) to demonstrate day and night
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials

  • A globe or ball (marked with your location)
  • A flashlight
  • A dark room
  • A sticker or dot to mark your location on the globe

Day 1: The Great Misconception

Ask your child: 'Does the Sun move across the sky?' They will almost certainly say yes — because it looks like it does. Explain: 'Actually, the Sun barely moves at all. It is Earth that is spinning. The Sun looks like it moves because WE are moving, like when you're on a moving train and trees seem to rush past.'

Demonstrate with the globe and flashlight. Shine the flashlight at the globe (Sun) — one side is lit (day), one is dark (night). Slowly spin the globe — watch as your sticker moves from light to dark to light again. That is one day.

Day 2: Tracking the Sun

Go outside at 9am, 12pm, and 3pm (or look through a window). Mark the position of a shadow cast by a vertical stick at each time. Ask: 'Did the shadow move? What direction? Why?'

📖

The Big Lesson

Earth's Rotation: The Cause of Day and Night

Earth spins on an imaginary line called its axis — a line running from the North Pole to the South Pole through the centre of Earth. Earth makes one complete spin every 24 hours. This spinning is called rotation.

As Earth rotates, the side facing the Sun receives sunlight — that side is experiencing day. The side facing away from the Sun is in shadow — that side is experiencing night. As Earth rotates, your location moves from day to night and back to day again every 24 hours.

The Sun Appears to Move

From where you stand on Earth's surface, the Sun appears to move across the sky from east to west over the course of each day. But this apparent motion is an illusion — it is Earth rotating from west to east that creates this appearance.

Think of it this way: if you are on a merry-go-round, everything around you appears to spin — but you are the one actually moving. When you stand on Earth's surface, everything in the sky appears to move from east to west — but it is you (on Earth) moving from west to east.

Navigation by the Sun: For thousands of years before GPS, sailors and travelers navigated using the Sun. In the Northern Hemisphere, the Sun is always roughly in the south at noon (its highest point). By knowing the time and the Sun's position, navigators could determine their location. Indigenous peoples around the world developed sophisticated systems for reading the sky.

Time Zones

Because Earth is a sphere and different parts face the Sun at different times, the whole world cannot use the same time. When it is noon in Toronto (Sun directly overhead), it is midnight in Moscow. This is why the world is divided into 24 time zones — one for each hour of the day. When you travel east, you move to a later time zone; when you travel west, you move to an earlier one.

The Length of a Day

A day is 24 hours because that is how long Earth takes to make one complete rotation on its axis. But the amount of daylight in a day changes with the seasons and your location. Near the equator, day and night are always roughly 12 hours each. Closer to the poles, the length of day varies dramatically — in northern Canada in summer, the Sun might be up for 20+ hours; in winter, it might barely rise.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Earth Rotation Model Investigation

Use your globe and flashlight model to investigate:

  1. When it is noon in Toronto, what time is it on the opposite side of the world? (Where is that, roughly?)
  2. If Earth rotated in the opposite direction (east to west instead of west to east), where would the Sun appear to rise and set?
  3. If Earth stopped rotating, what would happen to day and night? How would this affect life on Earth?
Many planets rotate much faster or slower than Earth. Jupiter rotates in just 10 hours; Venus rotates in 243 Earth-days — and in the opposite direction! Mercury's day is longer than its year. How would life on these planets (if there were life) be different from life on Earth?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Day and Night Diagram

Draw a large diagram showing Earth in space with the Sun to one side. Label: the axis, North Pole, South Pole, the equator, the side experiencing day, the side experiencing night, and the direction of rotation. Add a small figure representing yourself on the daytime side. Write 5 sentences explaining what is happening.

Day 2: Shadow Tracking Journal

Track shadows at 3 different times of day (morning, noon, afternoon). For each observation: draw the shadow position, measure its length (in cm or steps), note the direction, and record the time. Write a conclusion: How did the shadow change? Why?

Day 3: Time Zone Exploration

Research the world's time zones. Find at least 5 cities and their time zones. Create a chart: if it is 12:00 noon in Toronto, what time is it in each city? Explain in your own words why time zones exist.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Earth's Rotation Observation Diary

For 5 consecutive days, make these observations at the same time each morning and evening:

  1. Morning (8am): Where is the Sun in the sky? (low/high, which direction?) Record the temperature.
  2. Noon: Where is the Sun? What is the temperature?
  3. Evening (6pm): Where is the Sun? Record when it sets (approximately).
  4. Each night: What time did it get dark? What was the first star or planet you saw?

At the end of 5 days, write a 1-page summary: Did the Sun follow the same path every day? Did darkness come at the same time every day? What patterns did you notice?

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Midnight Sun: In some parts of Canada and Scandinavia, the Sun never sets in summer (called the 'midnight Sun'). Research why this happens and what life is like for people there.
  • Ancient timekeeping: Before clocks, people used sundials to tell time. Research how a sundial works and try to make one. How accurate is it?
  • Earth's rotation speed: If Earth stopped rotating suddenly, what would happen? (Hint: think about inertia — everything on the surface would keep moving at 1,670 km/h.) Research this thought experiment.
  • How astronauts experience day and night: The International Space Station orbits Earth every 90 minutes. How many 'sunrises' do astronauts see per day?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Earth's rotation is gradually slowing because of the Moon's gravitational pull (tidal friction). Eventually, Earth will show the same face to the Moon always — just as the Moon shows the same face to Earth. What would an 'Earth day' be like then?
  • Some blind people navigate entirely by sound, smell, and memory rather than sight. How much harder would this be if day and night were not regular? What does the regularity of Earth's rotation give us?
  • Exoplanets (planets around other stars) have been found with 'days' ranging from a few hours to hundreds of Earth-years. What would life on such a planet need to adapt to?
  • Could humans eventually live on a rotating space station that creates artificial gravity? How would day and night work there?
Week 2

🔄 Why the Sun 'Moves' — Earth's Orbit and the Year

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E3.1 – Explain Earth's revolution around the Sun
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

You are not just spinning. You are also orbiting. Right now, Earth is traveling around the Sun in a vast ellipse that takes exactly 365.25 days to complete. One year. In that time, you travel nearly 940 million kilometres through space.

The 0.25 extra day is why we have leap years. Every four years, we add one extra day (February 29) to keep our calendar in step with Earth's orbit. Without leap years, our calendar would slowly drift out of alignment with the seasons — and in a thousand years, December would be in summer and June in winter!

The calendar challenge: Ancient civilisations struggled to build accurate calendars. The Mayans developed a calendar accurate to within one day per 5,000 years — without telescopes or calculators. The Julian calendar was slightly too long, leading to a drift that accumulated over centuries; by the 1500s, the calendar had drifted 10 days out of sync with the seasons. In 1582, Pope Gregory XIII issued the Gregorian calendar we use today — and simply deleted 10 days from October to catch up. People went to sleep on October 4 and woke up on October 15.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand that Earth orbits (revolves around) the Sun once per year
  • Distinguish rotation (spinning on axis, daily) from revolution (orbiting the Sun, yearly)
  • Understand that Earth's tilted axis causes the seasons
  • Explain why different parts of Earth experience different amounts of sunlight throughout the year
  • Connect the year and the calendar to Earth's orbit
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Orbit Demonstration

Place a lamp (the Sun) on a table in the middle of a room. Have your child walk slowly in a circle around the lamp — this is Earth's orbit. Notice: as they orbit, they always face slightly inward (toward the lamp), but different parts of the room are visible as they go around.

Now add the tilt: have them hold a ball (Earth) tilted at an angle as they walk. Notice how the Northern Hemisphere tilts toward the Sun at one side of the orbit (summer) and away from the Sun at the other side (winter). This is the key to understanding seasons.

Key Misconception to Address

Most people incorrectly believe seasons are caused by Earth being closer to the Sun in summer. In fact, Earth is slightly CLOSER to the Sun in January (Northern Hemisphere winter) than in July (Northern Hemisphere summer)! The seasons are caused entirely by Earth's axial tilt, not distance from the Sun.

📖

The Big Lesson

Earth's Revolution: One Year

Earth not only spins on its axis (rotation) — it also travels around the Sun in a path called an orbit. This journey around the Sun is called revolution. Earth's orbit is nearly circular (actually a very slight ellipse) and takes 365.25 days to complete — we call this period one year.

Rotation vs. Revolution — Two Different Motions

It is easy to confuse these two motions. Rotation is Earth spinning on its axis — this takes 24 hours and causes day and night. Revolution is Earth orbiting the Sun — this takes 365.25 days and is related to the year. Both motions happen simultaneously, all the time.

Earth's Tilt — The Cause of Seasons

Earth's axis is not straight up and down relative to its orbit. It is tilted at 23.5 degrees. This tilt is the cause of the seasons.

When the Northern Hemisphere is tilted toward the Sun (around June), the Sun appears higher in the sky, days are longer, and sunlight hits the ground more directly — we get more energy and feel warmer. This is summer in the Northern Hemisphere.

When the Northern Hemisphere is tilted away from the Sun (around December), the Sun appears lower in the sky, days are shorter, and sunlight hits the ground at a shallower angle — we get less energy and feel cooler. This is winter in the Northern Hemisphere.

When neither hemisphere is tilted more toward or away from the Sun (around March and September), days and nights are roughly equal length everywhere on Earth. These are the spring and autumn equinoxes.

Axial tilt on other planets: Earth's tilt of 23.5° gives us our moderate, life-sustaining seasons. Uranus has an axial tilt of 98° — it essentially orbits on its side, so one pole faces the Sun for half a Uranian year, then the other pole does. The resulting seasons are extreme: 42 Earth-years of constant sunlight, then 42 years of darkness. Venus has almost no tilt (2.6°) and almost no seasons. Mars's tilt (25.2°) is similar to Earth's and gives it seasons similar to ours — and even polar ice caps that grow and shrink with the seasons.

The Solstices

The summer solstice (around June 21 in the Northern Hemisphere) is the longest day of the year — the Sun reaches its highest point in the sky and is above the horizon for the most hours. The winter solstice (around December 21) is the shortest day — the Sun barely rises and sets quickly.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Hemisphere Investigation

Think about this: when Ontario is experiencing winter, what season is it in Australia and South Africa (the Southern Hemisphere)?

Use your lamp and tilted globe model to demonstrate: when the Northern Hemisphere points away from the lamp, the Southern Hemisphere points toward it. So when it is winter in Canada, it is summer in Australia.

Christmas is in December — which is summer in Australia. How do you think Australians celebrate a summer Christmas? Research Australian Christmas traditions. How does the season affect their celebrations differently from ours?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Orbit and Rotation Diagram

Draw a large diagram of Earth's orbit around the Sun. Show Earth's position at four points (the two solstices and two equinoxes). For each position, draw Earth with its tilted axis and label: which hemisphere is having summer/winter, the date, and the approximate day length for Ontario.

Day 2: Tilt and Temperature Investigation

Test why angle of sunlight matters: shine a flashlight straight down onto paper, and at a 45-degree angle. Trace the beam's footprint. Which footprint is bigger? The same amount of light hits a larger area when tilted — so each part of that area gets less light. This explains why angled winter sunlight is less warming.

Day 3: Calendar Research

Research three different calendar systems used by different cultures: the Gregorian calendar (what we use), the Islamic calendar (lunar), and the Chinese calendar (lunisolar). How does each one track the year? What are the key differences?

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Seasons and the Earth Science Journal

Create a detailed illustrated science journal about Earth's motions. Include:

  1. A diagram showing rotation vs. revolution with clear labels
  2. An explanation of why Earth has seasons (with diagrams)
  3. A comparison of daylight hours in Ontario in June vs. December
  4. An explanation of what an equinox and solstice are
  5. Research: What would happen to life on Earth if Earth's tilt were 0 degrees? What if it were 90 degrees?
🏠

Homework Activities

  • Foucault Pendulum: In 1851, French scientist Léon Foucault hung a massive pendulum and watched it slowly rotate over the course of a day — proving Earth is rotating. Research how this works and why it was so significant.
  • Seasons on other planets: Mars has seasons like Earth's. Research how long a Martian year is and how long each Martian season lasts.
  • Ancient solstice celebrations: Stonehenge is precisely aligned with the summer solstice sunrise. Research what we know about why it was built. Find two other ancient monuments that align with solstices or equinoxes.
  • Leap years: Research why leap years exist and how they were figured out. What would happen if we stopped having leap years?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Earth's axial tilt itself changes slowly over thousands of years (from about 22° to 24.5°), in a cycle of about 41,000 years. How does this affect long-term climate?
  • Some astronomers think that if Jupiter were not in the solar system, its gravity would not stabilize Earth's axial tilt — the tilt could vary wildly, causing catastrophic climate swings. Could complex life exist without a stable axial tilt?
  • Astronomers have found 'rogue planets' — planets that were ejected from their star systems and now drift through interstellar space with no Sun. What would life (if any) on such a planet be like?
  • If Earth's orbit were a perfect circle instead of a very slight ellipse, would there be any difference to life on Earth?
Week 3

🌓 Light and Shadows — The Sun's Daily Dance

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E3.2 – Observe and describe how shadows change throughout the day
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In Ancient Egypt, the obelisk was not just a monument. It was a timekeeper. The shadow of an obelisk's tip moved across a calibrated circle throughout the day, marking the hours. The Great Obelisk of Thutmose III at Karnak, built in 1450 BCE, was essentially a giant sundial — and it worked on the same principle that a school ruler held vertically in sunlight would work today.

Shadow clocks across history: Before mechanical clocks, civilizations around the world independently discovered that shadows could measure time. The Chinese gnomon, the Greek polos, the Egyptian shadow clock, the Roman sundial — all different forms of the same idea. Humans have been reading shadows for at least 5,500 years. The sky above you has been a giant clock for all of human history.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand that shadows are formed when an object blocks light
  • Observe and record how shadow length and direction change throughout the day
  • Understand the relationship between sun position and shadow position/length
  • Use shadow observations to infer the time of day
  • Investigate how the season affects shadow length at noon
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Shadow Tracking Activity

Set up a simple gnomon (a vertical stick, at least 30 cm tall, fixed upright in a piece of clay or stuck into soil outside). Mark its position carefully. Track the shadow every hour from morning to evening — or at least 3–4 times throughout the day. Measure shadow length and record shadow direction.

Key Questions to Ask

'Why is the shadow longest in the morning and evening, and shortest at noon?' (Because the Sun is low in the sky in morning/evening, and highest in the sky at noon — shadows are longest when the Sun is low.)

'Can you predict what time it is by looking at the shadow? Try it without looking at a clock.'

📖

The Big Lesson

Shadows and Light

A shadow forms when an opaque object blocks a light source. The shadow appears on the opposite side of the object from the light source. Because the Sun is our primary light source during the day, shadows always point away from the Sun.

How Shadows Change Through the Day

As Earth rotates, the Sun appears to move across the sky. In the morning, the Sun is in the east (low in the sky), so shadows point westward and are long. At noon, the Sun is in the south and is highest in the sky, so shadows point northward and are shortest. In the afternoon, the Sun is in the west (low in the sky), so shadows point eastward and are long again.

Shortest noon shadow: On the summer solstice (around June 21), the Sun is at its highest noon point in the sky. This is when your noon shadow is shortest all year. On the winter solstice (around December 21), the Sun is at its lowest noon point — your noon shadow is longest all year. If you marked your noon shadow every month for a year, you would trace the changing height of the Sun through the seasons.

Shadow Length and Sun Height

There is a direct mathematical relationship between the Sun's angle above the horizon and the length of a shadow. When the Sun is at 90° (directly overhead — only possible near the equator), there is no shadow. When the Sun is at 45°, the shadow is exactly the same length as the object that cast it. When the Sun is very low (near the horizon, like sunrise or sunset), shadows can be hundreds of times longer than the object itself.

Using Shadows to Tell Time and Direction

Because shadows always point away from the Sun, and the Sun rises in the east and sets in the west, you can use shadows to find direction at any time of day. At noon, shadows point roughly north (in the Northern Hemisphere). You can also estimate the time by shadow length and direction — this is the principle of a sundial.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Shadow Investigation: Comparing Noon Shadows Across Seasons

If you have access to observations from different times of year (or can look back at any shadow tracking done earlier in the year), compare noon shadow lengths in summer and winter.

Alternatively: shine a flashlight at a high angle (representing summer sun) and measure the shadow of a pencil. Then shine it at a low angle (winter sun). How do the shadows compare?

Using the angle of a shadow, ancient Greek astronomer Eratosthenes calculated the circumference of Earth in about 240 BCE — to within 1% accuracy. He used shadows in two cities at the same time and a bit of geometry. Research how he did it.
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Shadow Tracking Diagram

Create an artistic diagram showing the same tree (or stick figure or building) at four times of day: 8am, 11am, 2pm, and 5pm. Draw the shadow correctly for each time, showing its direction and length. Colour the sky appropriately for each time. Label the position of the Sun for each scene.

Day 2: Build a Sundial

Make a simple sundial using a piece of card and a pencil (gnomon). Set it up in a sunny spot and mark the shadow every hour for as many hours as possible. Label each mark with the time. Can you use it the next day to tell time?

Day 3: Shadow Science Report

Write a science report explaining how shadows change through the day. Include: why they form, how their length changes, how their direction changes, and how this knowledge can be used to tell time and find direction. Include at least 2 labelled diagrams.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Seasonal Shadow Study

Every day for 5 days at exactly noon (or as close as you can), measure your shadow:

  1. Measure your shadow length (or trace it)
  2. Record what direction it points (north, northeast, etc.)
  3. Note the weather and temperature
  4. Note approximately how high the Sun looks in the sky

At the end of 5 days, write a conclusion. Do you see any variation? Research: how would your noon shadow compare if you did this same experiment in December?

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Eratosthenes's experiment: Research in detail how Eratosthenes calculated Earth's circumference using shadows. Try to reproduce his calculation using the geometry he used.
  • Shadow puppets: In many cultures, shadow puppetry is an ancient art form. Research the Indonesian Wayang Kulit shadow puppet tradition. How does it connect to the science of shadows?
  • Lunar shadows: What causes a solar eclipse? What causes a lunar eclipse? How are these related to shadows? Draw and label diagrams for both.
  • Shadow photography: Go outside at two different times of day and photograph (or draw) the same shadow. How is the shadow different? Can someone looking at just your two shadow pictures guess what time each was taken?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • On the Moon, shadows are razor-sharp (no atmosphere to scatter light). On a planet with a thick atmosphere, shadows would be very soft and fuzzy. What does this tell you about the role of atmosphere in what we see?
  • Some animals use shadow as camouflage (countershading — they are darker on top to compensate for the shadow under their belly, making them appear flat and hard to see). How does this connect to the science of light and shadow?
  • Could you build a calendar using only shadows — no numbers, no writing, just marks on the ground? What would it look like?
  • The shadow of a solar eclipse travels across Earth at over 1,700 km/h. Why does the shadow move so fast?
Week 4

🌙 The Moon — Earth's Constant Companion

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E3.3 – Describe the Moon's appearance and explain its phases
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

The Moon is 384,400 kilometres away. Impossibly far, it seems — yet you can see every crater on its face with binoculars, because the Moon has no atmosphere to obscure the view, and because craters cover its entire ancient surface.

The Moon has been with Earth for 4.5 billion years — nearly as old as Earth itself. And scientists now believe the Moon was created in a catastrophic collision: about 4.5 billion years ago, a planet roughly the size of Mars crashed into the young Earth. The impact was so violent that it melted both bodies. The debris thrown into orbit around Earth gradually coalesced to form the Moon. The two worlds share the same rocks.

The Moon is moving away from us: The Moon is currently moving away from Earth at a rate of about 3.8 centimetres per year — roughly the same speed that your fingernails grow. When the Moon formed, it was 15 times closer than it is today. In billions of years, it will be far enough away that total solar eclipses will no longer be possible — the Moon will be too small in the sky to fully cover the Sun. We live in a special cosmic moment when the Moon appears almost perfectly the same size as the Sun in our sky.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Describe the appearance of the Moon: craters, plains, size relative to Earth
  • Understand that the Moon orbits Earth, and this orbit causes the phases
  • Name and identify the main phases of the Moon
  • Understand that the Moon reflects sunlight rather than producing its own light
  • Connect Moon phase observations to a monthly cycle of observations
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Materials

  • Binoculars (optional but strongly recommended for moon observation)
  • A bright lamp or flashlight (representing the Sun)
  • A ball representing the Moon

Moon Phase Demonstration

This is one of the most important demonstrations in the whole guide. Darken the room. Place a bright lamp on one side. Have your child stand in the middle with a ball held at arm's length at head height, representing the Moon. Walk slowly in a circle (orbiting the lamp). Observe: as they orbit, the 'lit side' of the ball (the side facing the lamp) is sometimes facing them (full Moon), sometimes facing away (new Moon — they see only the dark side), and sometimes they see part of the lit side (crescent, quarter, gibbous phases).

Monthly Moon Journal

Start a Moon journal tonight (or whenever this lesson falls). Every clear night, sketch the Moon — what shape does the lit part appear? Record the date. Do this for a full month (28 days) and watch the complete cycle of phases.

📖

The Big Lesson

Our Moon

The Moon is Earth's only natural satellite — a body that orbits our planet. It is about one-quarter of Earth's diameter and has about 1/6 of Earth's gravitational pull (which is why astronauts could bounce around on the lunar surface).

The Moon has no atmosphere, no weather, no liquid water, and almost no geological activity. Its surface is covered in craters formed by asteroid and meteorite impacts over billions of years. Without weather to erode them, these craters remain perfectly preserved — some are billions of years old. The footprints left by Apollo astronauts in the 1960s–70s are still there, undisturbed.

The Moon's Orbit

The Moon orbits Earth roughly every 29.5 days — this is the basis of the month (the word 'month' comes from 'Moon'). As the Moon orbits Earth, it is also rotating on its own axis — but its rotation period is the same as its orbital period. This means the Moon always shows the same face to Earth. We have never seen the far side of the Moon from Earth. (We have seen it from spacecraft.)

Phases of the Moon

The Moon does not produce its own light — it reflects sunlight. As the Moon orbits Earth, we see different amounts of the lit side, which creates the familiar phases:

  • New Moon: The Moon is between Earth and the Sun. The lit side faces away from us. We cannot see the Moon.
  • Waxing Crescent: A small sliver of the lit side becomes visible in the west after sunset.
  • First Quarter: Half the Moon's face is lit (the right half, in the Northern Hemisphere).
  • Waxing Gibbous: More than half of the Moon is lit.
  • Full Moon: Earth is between the Moon and the Sun. We see the entire lit side. The Moon rises at sunset and sets at sunrise.
  • Waning Gibbous, Third Quarter, Waning Crescent: The lit portion decreases as the Moon continues around.
The Moon and tides: The Moon's gravity pulls on Earth's oceans, creating tides. There are two high tides and two low tides each day in most ocean locations. At a new Moon and full Moon, the Sun and Moon are aligned, and tides are especially high (spring tides). At first and third quarter, they are perpendicular, and tides are smaller (neap tides). The difference between high and low tide at the Bay of Fundy in Nova Scotia can be 16 metres — the highest tidal range in the world.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Phase Observation and Prediction

After observing the Moon for at least a week, try to predict what phase it will be in 7 more days. Write your prediction and your reasoning.

After another week, check: were you right? If you were wrong, what would you need to know to make a better prediction?

Many cultures around the world have lunar calendars (like the Islamic calendar, the Chinese calendar, and the Hebrew calendar) that keep track of months by the Moon's phases. What are the advantages and disadvantages of a lunar calendar compared to a solar calendar?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Moon Phase Diagram

Draw a large diagram showing the Moon's orbit around Earth with all 8 phases labeled and illustrated. For each phase, show: where the Moon is in its orbit, what the lit portion looks like, and what time of night it is typically visible.

Day 2: Moon Facts Presentation

Research 10 fascinating facts about the Moon. Present them as an illustrated infographic or poster. Include facts about: size, distance, craters, gravity, the Apollo missions, tides, and phases.

Day 3: Moon Phase Journal Start

Begin your monthly Moon journal. Tonight (or the next clear night): observe the Moon, sketch its exact shape, record the date and time, and note its approximate position in the sky. Set a reminder to repeat this every 3 days for the next month.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Apollo Mission Report

Research the Apollo 11 mission — the first time humans landed on the Moon (July 20, 1969). Write a detailed report covering:

  1. Why did the USA want to go to the Moon? (The Space Race)
  2. Who were the three astronauts? What were their roles?
  3. How did the spacecraft work (very simplified)?
  4. What did they do on the Moon? How long were they there?
  5. What did they bring back?
  6. How did the mission change our understanding of the Moon and Earth?

Illustrate your report with diagrams and drawings. Aim for at least 2 pages.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Lunar landings: Research all 6 Apollo Moon landings (Apollo 11, 12, 14, 15, 16, 17). Create a comparison chart: year, location, astronauts, and key discoveries.
  • The dark side of the Moon: We never see the far side of the Moon from Earth. In 2019, China landed the Chang'e 4 probe on the far side. Research what the far side looks like and why it is so different from the near side.
  • Moon and life on Earth: Research the hypothesis that the Moon stabilizes Earth's axial tilt, making Earth's climate more stable. What evidence supports this?
  • Future Moon missions: Research NASA's Artemis program — the plan to return humans to the Moon. What is the goal? Who is going? What are they planning to do there?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • If the Moon were twice as far away as it is now, what would change? (Hint: tides, solar eclipses, night brightness, etc.)
  • Many astronomers believe that without the Moon's stabilizing effect on Earth's tilt, complex life on Earth might never have evolved. What do you think?
  • A 'super Moon' occurs when the Moon is at its closest point to Earth in its orbit (perigee) and appears full. How much larger and brighter does it appear? Why?
  • Some scientists have proposed building a permanent base on the Moon. What would be the main challenges? What would it be useful for?
Week 5

⭐ Stars and the Night Sky — Windows to the Universe

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E3.4 – Identify stars and constellations; describe the Sun as a star
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

On a clear, dark night far from city lights, you can see about 2,500 stars with your naked eye. On a really excellent night, maybe 5,000. But the Milky Way galaxy — our home galaxy — contains about 250 billion stars. And there are an estimated 2 trillion galaxies in the observable universe.

Every single star you can see in the night sky is a sun — many of them larger, hotter, and brighter than our own Sun. The faint smear of light across the sky called the Milky Way is our galaxy seen edge-on — you are looking at 250 billion stars, most too distant to see individually.

The light from dead stars: Light travels at 300,000 km/s, but space is so vast that the light from distant stars takes thousands or even millions of years to reach us. When you look at a star 1,000 light-years away, you are seeing how that star looked 1,000 years ago. Some of the stars you see in the sky may have already exploded and died — but their light is still traveling toward us. You are literally looking back in time.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand that the Sun is a star
  • Describe the appearance of the night sky and identify key constellations
  • Understand why stars appear to move across the sky (Earth's rotation)
  • Understand what a star is (a massive ball of gas undergoing nuclear fusion)
  • Appreciate the vast scale of the universe
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Night Sky Activity

On a clear night, go outside away from bright lights. Bring: a red flashlight (red light doesn't ruin night vision), a star chart for your location and date, or a stargazing app (Stellarium is free and excellent).

Find at least 3 constellations together. Look for: the Big Dipper (always visible from Ontario), Orion (visible in winter), Cassiopeia (W-shaped, always visible from Ontario). Ask: 'Are these stars near each other in space, or do they just appear near each other from our point of view?'

Key Concept: Stars as Suns

Ask your child: 'What is the difference between our Sun and the stars we see at night?' The answer: distance. Our Sun is 'only' 150 million km away. The next nearest star (Alpha Centauri) is 4.2 light-years away — over 40 trillion km. That is why stars look like tiny pinpoints, while our Sun fills the sky.

📖

The Big Lesson

The Sun Is a Star

Our Sun is a star — a massive ball of hydrogen and helium gas undergoing nuclear fusion in its core. What makes our Sun different from the stars we see at night is simply distance. The Sun is so much closer to Earth (150 million km) that it dominates our sky. If the Sun were as far away as Alpha Centauri (the next nearest star), it would look like a faint star to the naked eye.

What Are Stars?

Stars are enormous spheres of hot gas held together by gravity. In their cores, the intense pressure and temperature cause hydrogen atoms to fuse together to form helium, releasing enormous energy in the process. This nuclear fusion has been powering our Sun for about 4.6 billion years and will continue for another 5 billion years.

Stars vary enormously. The smallest stars (red dwarfs) are barely larger than a big planet. The largest stars (hypergiants) are so enormous that if our Sun were replaced by one of them, it would extend past Jupiter's orbit. Our Sun is a medium-sized star.

Constellations

From Earth, stars appear to form patterns called constellations. Ancient cultures around the world named these patterns after animals, people, and objects. Today we use 88 official constellations to map the entire sky.

But constellations are not real groups of stars in space — they are just projections onto our sky. The stars in a constellation may be at very different distances from Earth, only appearing near each other from our perspective. If you viewed the same 'constellation' from a different part of the galaxy, it would look completely different.

Ontario constellations to find: Ursa Major (the Great Bear, contains the Big Dipper) is circumpolar — it never sets below the horizon from Ontario and is visible every clear night year-round. Cassiopeia (a W shape) is also circumpolar. In winter, Orion (three belt stars in a row) dominates the southern sky. In summer, Scorpius is low in the south, and the Summer Triangle (three bright stars: Vega, Altair, Deneb) is high overhead.

Why Do Stars Seem to Move?

Stars appear to rise in the east and set in the west — just like the Sun and Moon. This apparent motion is caused by Earth's rotation. Stars actually move very little relative to each other — any motion we can detect requires years of precise measurement. What we see as star movement is Earth spinning beneath us.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Star Distance Challenge

To understand how far away stars are, try this scale model:

If the Sun were the size of a basketball (diameter ~24 cm), Earth would be a tiny ball bearing (diameter ~2 mm) placed 26 metres away. On this scale, the next nearest star (Alpha Centauri) would be 7,200 kilometres away — roughly the distance from Toronto to London, England.

Write out this scale in your own words. What does it tell you about the emptiness of space?

The light from Alpha Centauri takes 4.2 years to reach us. If you wanted to send a radio message to any civilization around Alpha Centauri and wait for a reply, the shortest possible exchange would take 8.4 years. What are the implications for ever 'talking to' aliens, even if they exist nearby?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Star Map Creation

Create a star map of the night sky visible from Ontario in the current season. Mark at least 10 stars and 5 constellations. Use a star atlas or app for reference. Include a legend showing how star brightness is represented (bigger dot = brighter star). Describe three of your constellations: their name, the myth behind them, and how to find them.

Day 2: The Solar Family

Draw a diagram comparing our Sun to other stars: show the Sun, a red dwarf, a blue giant, and a red supergiant on the same scale (they will be dramatically different sizes). Research and label: the colour, approximate temperature, and lifetime of each type.

Day 3: Life Cycle of a Star

Research and draw the complete life cycle of a star like our Sun: nebula → protostar → main sequence star → red giant → planetary nebula → white dwarf. Write a sentence explaining each stage. Then show the (very different) life cycle of a massive star that ends in a supernova.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Stargazing Journal and Report

Over 5 clear nights, keep a detailed stargazing journal:

  1. The date, time, and weather conditions
  2. The constellation(s) you identified and how you found them
  3. Any stars you can identify by name (research star names)
  4. A sketch of what you saw
  5. One interesting fact you learned or observed

At the end of the week, write a 1-page astronomy report summarizing what you learned from direct observation. Were there things you expected to see but didn't? Things that surprised you?

🏠

Homework Activities

  • The James Webb Space Telescope: The most powerful telescope ever built (launched December 2021) can see galaxies formed just 300 million years after the Big Bang. Research one of its major discoveries.
  • Exoplanets: Astronomers have now found over 5,000 planets around other stars. Research the Kepler Space Telescope and how it found them. Are any of these planets similar to Earth?
  • The Milky Way: Draw and label a diagram of the Milky Way galaxy. Where is our solar system located in the galaxy? How long does it take the solar system to orbit the galactic centre?
  • Indigenous star knowledge: Research how one Indigenous people of Canada (e.g., the Cree, Anishinaabe, or Haudenosaunee) used the stars for navigation, timekeeping, and storytelling.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Our Sun will eventually expand into a red giant and swallow the inner planets (including Earth) in about 5 billion years. How much of our solar system will survive?
  • When a massive star explodes as a supernova, it can briefly outshine an entire galaxy. The elements formed in supernovas — including the iron in your blood — were scattered across space and eventually became part of you. How does this make you feel about stars?
  • If there are 2 trillion galaxies, each with hundreds of billions of stars, and many of those stars have planets — does it seem likely that intelligent life exists somewhere besides Earth? What do you think?
  • Scientists believe space itself is expanding, carrying galaxies away from each other. In the very distant future, the night sky will be empty — all other galaxies will have moved beyond the observable universe. What would it be like to be the only galaxy in sight?
Week 6

🍂❄️🌸☀️ The Four Seasons — Earth's Annual Story

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E3.5 – Describe characteristics of each season; explain why seasons occur
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

If you could watch Earth from space for an entire year, you would see something beautiful: the colours of the land shifting in waves. In spring, green sweeps northward from the equator as plants awaken after winter. In summer, the north glows green, while the south begins to brown. In fall, the north blazes with colour before going dark and white with snow. In winter, the snow line creeps south.

Seasons as a global wave: The seasons are not just a local experience. They are a planetary-scale phenomenon — a wave of energy from the Sun sweeping back and forth across Earth as it orbits. Everything alive on Earth responds to this wave: trees, flowers, birds, insects, fish, bears, bacteria. The seasons are one of the most important forces shaping all of life on Earth. Billions of living things — including you — have ancient biological rhythms built around the four-season cycle.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Identify and describe the four seasons: fall, winter, spring, summer
  • Explain that seasons are caused by Earth's tilted axis during its orbit
  • Describe the characteristic weather, day length, and temperature of each season
  • Observe and record seasonal changes in the local environment
  • Connect seasonal changes to the behaviour of living things
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Seasonal Photos or Nature Walk

If possible, gather photographs (or create drawings) of your neighbourhood in each of the four seasons. Compare them side by side: What changes? What stays the same? Focus on: trees, animals, clothing, activities, sky colour, snow, grass.

Seasonal Data Analysis

Look up historical weather data for your city (Environment Canada has free historical data). Find the average temperature for each month of the year. Plot them on a simple graph. Ask: Which months are warmest? Coldest? What pattern do you see? When do the seasons transition?

📖

The Big Lesson

The Four Seasons of Ontario

Ontario experiences four distinct seasons — fall, winter, spring, and summer — driven by Earth's tilted axis as it orbits the Sun. Each season has characteristic temperatures, day lengths, weather patterns, and changes in the living world.

Why Do We Have Seasons? (Review)

Earth's axis is tilted 23.5° from vertical. As Earth orbits the Sun, the Northern Hemisphere is tilted toward the Sun for half the year (spring and summer) and away from the Sun for the other half (fall and winter). This tilt determines how much solar energy each location receives — more energy means warmer temperatures and longer days.

The Four Seasons Described

Summer (June–August in Ontario): The Northern Hemisphere is tilted toward the Sun. Days are long (up to 15+ hours of daylight), the Sun is high in the sky, and temperatures are warmest. Plants grow vigorously, animals raise their young, and outdoor life is at its most active.

Fall/Autumn (September–November): The Northern Hemisphere begins tilting away from the Sun. Days become shorter, temperatures cool, and there is less sunlight. Deciduous trees change colour and drop their leaves. Many animals prepare for winter by storing food or fattening up.

Winter (December–February): The Northern Hemisphere is tilted away from the Sun. Days are short (as few as 8–9 hours), the Sun is low in the sky, and temperatures are coldest. Snow covers the ground. Many animals hibernate; plants are dormant.

Spring (March–May): The Northern Hemisphere begins tilting toward the Sun again. Days lengthen rapidly, temperatures rise, and snow melts. Plants begin to bud and leaf out. Animals emerge from hibernation, migrate back, and begin breeding.

Ontario's climate extremes: Ontario's climate is quite continental — large land masses don't moderate temperatures the way oceans do. Winter temperatures in northern Ontario can drop to -40°C, while summer temperatures can reach 40°C. In the same city, over the course of a year, temperatures can swing 80 degrees Celsius. This enormous range shapes everything about Ontario ecology — the plants, animals, and Indigenous practices that evolved here are all exquisitely adapted to this dramatic seasonal cycle.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Seasonal Changes Observation Walk

Go on a nature walk in your current season. Observe and record:

  • What are the trees doing? (Full leaves, bare, budding, colourful?)
  • What insects can you see or hear? What birds?
  • What is the light like — bright and high, or low and golden?
  • What does the air smell like?
  • What are people wearing? What are children doing?

Write a detailed 'Seasonal Portrait' of your neighbourhood as it is right now.

Why do you think ancient peoples and modern cultures around the world celebrate at the solstices and equinoxes? (Examples: Diwali, Christmas, Nowruz, Midsummer, the Chinese New Year, the harvest festivals of many cultures.) What do these celebrations have in common?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Four Seasons Science Journal

Create a four-page seasonal journal, one page per season. For each season, write: average temperature range, day length (sunrise to sunset), characteristic weather, what plants do, what animals do, and the outdoor activities humans do. Illustrate each page beautifully.

Day 2: Seasonal Data Graph

Plot monthly average temperatures for your city on a line graph. Label each season. Identify: the warmest month, the coldest month, the fastest warming period (spring), and the fastest cooling period (fall).

Day 3: Seasonal Changes Cause-Effect Diagram

Create a 'cause and effect' diagram for the seasons. Start with 'Earth's axial tilt' and show how it causes: changed day length → changed solar energy → changed temperature → changed weather → changes in living things. Use arrows and labels.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Full Year Seasonal Research Project

Research what happens in your local environment during each season:

  1. Spring: List 5 plants that bloom and 5 animals that arrive or wake up. When do they typically appear?
  2. Summer: What crops grow? What outdoor activities are typical? What wildlife is most active?
  3. Fall: What plants change? Which birds migrate south? What food is harvested?
  4. Winter: What animals hibernate and which stay active? How do plants survive?

Present as a beautifully illustrated 'Year in Our Neighbourhood' guide. Aim for at least 1.5 pages per season.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Indigenous seasonal knowledge: Research how Anishinaabe or Haudenosaunee people of Ontario traditionally organized their year around the seasons — what they hunted, planted, harvested, and celebrated in each season.
  • Seasonal foods: Make a seasonal food calendar for Ontario — what fruits and vegetables grow in which season? Why is eating locally and seasonally better for the environment?
  • Seasons on Mars: Mars has seasons like Earth's. Research how long they last and what they are like. How is a Martian winter different from Earth's?
  • Do plants count the days? Research photoperiodism — how plants 'sense' the seasons by measuring day length. How do plants know when to bloom or drop their leaves?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Scientists have found evidence in rock layers that Earth has experienced at least 5 major ice ages, where glaciers covered most of the planet. What causes ice ages? Are we currently heading toward another one?
  • Some scientists believe that life on Earth migrated from around hydrothermal vents (no sunlight, no seasons) and was not originally dependent on the seasonal cycle at all. How does this affect ideas about life elsewhere in the universe?
  • As climate change causes temperatures to rise, some places on Earth will see their seasons shift — spring arriving weeks earlier, fall staying later. How will this affect plants and animals that evolved to respond to specific seasonal cues?
  • Could a planet with no axial tilt have a form of life? What might drive the rhythms of that life?
Week 7

🍂 Fall — A Season in Detail

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E3.5 – Describe seasonal changes in the natural environment: fall
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Every autumn in Ontario, something extraordinary happens: the green disappears. Millions of trees simultaneously halt their green chemistry and reveal the hidden pigments underneath — blazing reds, oranges, and yellows that were always there, masked by the chlorophyll that powered summer's photosynthesis.

Why do leaves change colour in fall? As days shorten and temperatures cool, trees prepare to shed their leaves (which would be damaged by winter). They withdraw the valuable green chlorophyll from the leaves before they fall — and without chlorophyll, the red and orange pigments that were always present in the leaf but hidden become visible. The vivid reds are newly made — the tree produces them as a kind of sunscreen to protect the leaf while it withdraws the last of its valuable nutrients. Leaf colour is the tree working, all the way to the end.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Describe in detail the characteristics of fall in Ontario
  • Identify seasonal changes in plants during fall
  • Identify seasonal changes in animals during fall
  • Describe fall weather patterns and how they affect daily life
  • Connect fall changes to the concept of adaptation
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Best Fall Activities

Take your child outside to directly experience fall if you are studying it in the right season, or use photographs and research if the season has passed. Direct sensory experience of the season is invaluable — what it looks, sounds, smells, and feels like cannot be captured in words alone.

Living Things Interview

Imagine you can interview a tree, a robin, a bear, and a honey bee about what they are doing in fall. What would each one say? This imaginative exercise develops deep understanding of how different living things respond to the same season in very different ways.

📖

The Big Lesson

Fall in Ontario

Fall in Ontario (September, October, November) is a dramatic transition from the warmth and abundance of summer to the dormancy of winter. This is one of the most beautiful and active periods in the natural world.

What Plants Do in Fall

Deciduous trees (trees that lose their leaves) go through a complex chemical process as fall begins. The shortening days trigger the tree to stop producing chlorophyll (the green pigment used in photosynthesis). As chlorophyll breaks down, previously hidden yellow and orange pigments (carotenoids) become visible. Trees also produce new red and purple pigments (anthocyanins) in the final weeks.

After the leaves reach peak colour, the tree seals off each leaf at the base of its stem (this seal is called the abscission layer) and the leaf drops. This is not loss — it is conservation. The tree withdraws precious nutrients from the leaf before dropping it, and the fallen leaves decompose into rich soil nutrients that the tree will absorb through its roots next spring.

What Animals Do in Fall

Fall is a time of frantic preparation. Animals that migrate — particularly birds and some insects like monarch butterflies — begin their journey south. Animals that hibernate (bears, groundhogs, some bats) eat voraciously to build the fat reserves that will sustain them through months without food. Squirrels, chipmunks, and blue jays hide seeds and nuts ('caching') that they will find and eat in winter.

Many insects die in fall, leaving only their eggs to survive winter. Monarch butterflies make an astonishing migration of up to 4,500 km to overwintering sites in Mexico — guided by the Sun, Earth's magnetic field, and inherited instinct.

Ontario's fall colours: The fall colours in Ontario (particularly in Algonquin Park and the Muskoka region) are world-famous and attract millions of visitors each year. Sugar maples produce the most vivid scarlet reds; red maples turn brilliant red-orange; aspens turn gold; and birches turn yellow. The best colours happen when warm, sunny days follow cool (but not freezing) nights — warm days allow sugar production, and cool nights trigger anthocyanin formation.
🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Scientific Observation: Fall Signs

Over 3 days, record as many signs of fall as you can observe. Create categories: changes in plants, changes in animals, changes in weather, changes in the sky and light, changes in human activity.

Which sign surprised you most? Which was most beautiful?

Animals and plants have evolved to respond to seasonal cues. But climate change is shifting the timing of seasons. If spring arrives 3 weeks earlier than it did 50 years ago, but some animals rely on a precise signal (like day length, which hasn't changed) to time their fall behaviour — what problems could arise?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Detailed Season Journal

Write a detailed, illustrated description of fall in your neighbourhood. Include: changes in trees and plants, which animals are active and what they are doing, the quality of light and sky, characteristic sounds and smells, and what humans are doing differently. Aim for at least 1 page with 3 illustrations.

Day 2: Living Things in This Season

Research 5 different animals and 5 different plants. For each, explain exactly what they do in fall and why. Use specific examples from Ontario species.

Day 3: Adaptation Report

Choose one animal that has a remarkable adaptation for surviving fall (e.g., a bird that migrates, a bear that hibernates, a ptarmigan that turns white). Write a detailed report explaining the adaptation, how it helps the animal survive, and how long the adaptation took to evolve.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Fall Nature Investigation

Spend the week investigating fall in your immediate environment:

  1. Day 1: Plant Survey — record every plant you can see and what stage of its fall cycle it is in
  2. Day 2: Animal Watch — record every animal (including birds and insects) you see or hear for one hour
  3. Day 3: Weather Recording — record temperature, cloud cover, wind, and any precipitation
  4. Day 4: Photograph (or draw) the most beautiful thing you see that says 'Fall is here'
  5. Day 5: Write a nature essay (at least 1 page) describing fall in your words — try to make someone who has never experienced this season understand exactly what it is like
🏠

Homework Activities

  • Indigenous seasonal knowledge: Research what traditional activities Indigenous peoples of the Great Lakes region engaged in during fall. What did they eat? What ceremonies did they hold? What knowledge of nature guided their actions?
  • Literary fall: Find 3 poems or short passages from literature that describe fall. What images do the writers use? Do these match your own observations?
  • Climate comparison: How does fall in Ontario compare to fall near the equator? Near the poles? Research and compare.
  • Animal Preparation: Research how one specific Ontario animal (your choice) prepares for, survives through, and emerges from fall.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Could you tell what season it was in a place you'd never been before by looking at photographs taken there? What clues would you look for?
  • Some animals have evolved so precisely for one season that they cannot survive if that season changes dramatically. Name one example and explain the risk it faces from climate change.
  • Before modern heating and food storage, how did people survive fall in a place like Ontario? Research pre-industrial winter survival.
  • Imagine a future where seasons are radically different from today due to climate change. How might human culture, food systems, and cities need to adapt?
Week 8

❄️ Winter — A Season in Detail

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E3.5 – Describe seasonal changes in the natural environment: winter
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

A snowflake begins as a single speck of dust in a cloud. A water molecule freezes around the dust particle, forming a tiny ice crystal with a hexagonal structure. As the crystal falls through different temperature and humidity layers in the cloud, it grows in different directions — and because no two paths through the cloud are identical, no two snowflakes are identical.

Snowflake mathematics: A snowflake has six-fold symmetry — all six arms are (approximately) the same shape. This happens because all six arms are experiencing the same air conditions at the same time. But the conditions change moment by moment as the crystal falls, creating ever more elaborate branching. Each snowflake records its entire journey through the cloud in its unique shape. Every snowflake you have ever seen is a one-of-a-kind record of a unique journey.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Describe in detail the characteristics of winter in Ontario
  • Identify seasonal changes in plants during winter
  • Identify seasonal changes in animals during winter
  • Describe winter weather patterns and how they affect daily life
  • Connect winter changes to the concept of adaptation
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Best Winter Activities

Take your child outside to directly experience winter if you are studying it in the right season, or use photographs and research if the season has passed. Direct sensory experience of the season is invaluable — what it looks, sounds, smells, and feels like cannot be captured in words alone.

Living Things Interview

Imagine you can interview a tree, a robin, a bear, and a honey bee about what they are doing in winter. What would each one say? This imaginative exercise develops deep understanding of how different living things respond to the same season in very different ways.

📖

The Big Lesson

Winter in Ontario

Winter in Ontario (December, January, February) is defined by cold, reduced sunlight, snow, and ice. Average temperatures range from about -15°C in northern Ontario to -5°C in southern Ontario. The short days (as few as 8–9 hours in December) mean less solar energy reaches the ground.

Snow and Ice

Winter in Ontario is characterized by snow — frozen water crystals that accumulate into a reflective white blanket. Snow is actually an excellent insulator: the air trapped between snowflakes keeps the ground beneath it warmer than the air above. This is why small animals like mice and voles can survive winter by tunneling beneath the snow, where temperatures remain near 0°C even when air temperatures drop to -30°C or below.

Ice forms when water reaches 0°C. Lakes and rivers in Ontario freeze over in winter — a significant change that affects aquatic life. Fish slow their metabolism in cold water and some, like lake trout, actually become more active and feed in winter.

How Plants Survive Winter

Deciduous trees survive winter in a dormant state — their leaves are gone, their metabolism is slowed, and they are protected by thick bark. The water in their cells contains dissolved sugars that act like antifreeze, lowering the freezing point. Evergreen trees keep their needles (which have thick, waxy coatings that prevent drying and protect against cold). Ground-level plants die back to their roots, which survive below the frost line in the soil.

How Animals Survive Winter

Animals have three main strategies for surviving winter in Ontario: hibernation (bears, groundhogs, bats — deep dormancy with dramatically slowed heart rate and metabolism), torpor (chipmunks — a lighter dormancy with occasional waking), and active survival (wolves, deer, foxes, crows, ravens — staying active, using various strategies to find food and stay warm).

Wood frog: The wood frog, common throughout Ontario, survives winter by freezing solid. Ice crystals actually form inside its cells. Its heart stops, its brain stops, its blood stops flowing. When spring returns, it thaws completely — and hops away as if nothing happened. Special proteins and high concentrations of glucose prevent cell damage during the freeze. Scientists are studying the wood frog to develop better techniques for preserving human organs for transplantation.
🧪
