Week 9

🌸 Spring — A Season in Detail

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E3.5 – Describe seasonal changes in the natural environment: spring
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In Ontario, spring does not arrive gradually. It arrives in explosions. One week the landscape is brown and silent. The next, peepers (tiny frogs) are calling from every wetland. Then the red-winged blackbirds arrive. Then robins. Then trilliums push through the leaf litter, and suddenly — within days — the entire forest canopy goes from bare sticks to full green leaves.

The green wave: Each spring, a wave of green sweeps northward across North America as plants respond to warming temperatures. Scientists track this 'green wave' using satellites. Migrating birds ride the wave northward — following the emerging insects and plant material that the wave creates. The entire spring migration of North American birds is synchronized with this wave. A warbler in Costa Rica senses the changing day length and begins flying north, arriving just as the caterpillars emerge from the new leaves in Ontario. This synchrony, evolved over millions of years, is now being disrupted by climate change.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Describe in detail the characteristics of spring in Ontario
  • Identify seasonal changes in plants during spring
  • Identify seasonal changes in animals during spring
  • Describe spring weather patterns and how they affect daily life
  • Connect spring changes to the concept of adaptation
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Best Spring Activities

Take your child outside to directly experience spring if you are studying it in the right season, or use photographs and research if the season has passed. Direct sensory experience of the season is invaluable — what it looks, sounds, smells, and feels like cannot be captured in words alone.

Living Things Interview

Imagine you can interview a tree, a robin, a bear, and a honey bee about what they are doing in spring. What would each one say? This imaginative exercise develops deep understanding of how different living things respond to the same season in very different ways.

📖

The Big Lesson

Spring in Ontario

Spring (March, April, May) is the most dramatic transition in Ontario's annual cycle — from the frozen stillness of winter to the explosive growth and activity of summer. Temperatures rise rapidly, day length increases quickly (gaining about 4 minutes of daylight per day in March), and the natural world awakens in a cascade of events.

The Spring Melt

The defining event of Ontario's spring is the melt of snow and ice. This happens gradually, beginning in February in some years, continuing through April. As snow melts, the water flows into streams and rivers, causing spring flooding. Water tables rise. Wetlands fill. This rush of fresh, cold water is critical for aquatic ecosystems — it oxygenates the water and flushes out accumulated debris from winter.

The Return of Light

Spring means rapidly lengthening days. The amount of sunlight in March increases more quickly than in any other month. Plants respond to this increase in day length (photoperiodism) — leaves begin to emerge from buds, flowers begin to bloom, and photosynthesis resumes at full capacity.

Ontario's Spring Wildflowers

Spring wildflowers are a remarkable ecological phenomenon. Before the tree canopy closes and blocks the forest floor from sunlight, spring flowers race to complete their growth, bloom, and pollination — often in just 3–4 weeks. Bloodroot, hepatica, spring beauty, trout lily, and the provincial flower, the white trillium, all bloom in this narrow spring window. They are called 'spring ephemerals.'

The Return of Animals

Spring marks the return of migratory birds — warblers, swallows, shorebirds, and waterfowl returning from Central and South America, the Gulf Coast, and the Caribbean. Hibernating animals emerge (often thin and hungry). Amphibians — frogs, toads, and salamanders — migrate to breeding ponds on the first warm rainy nights of spring. Baby animals begin to appear: fawns, baby rabbits, ducklings.

The spring peeper: The spring peeper frog is one of Ontario's most beloved harbingers of spring. These frogs, only about 2 cm long, create a sound out of all proportion to their size — a massive chorus of high-pitched whistles from every wetland and pond. They are often calling while there is still ice on some ponds. Their call is triggered by air temperatures reliably above 5°C and is one of Ontario's most reliable seasonal signals.
🧪

Week 10

☀️ Summer — A Season in Detail

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E3.5 – Describe seasonal changes in the natural environment: summer
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

On the longest day of the year in Ontario (around June 21), the Sun rises before 5:30am and sets after 9pm — more than 15 hours of daylight. The Sun is at its highest point in the sky all year. The energy arriving at Ontario's surface per square metre is more than twice what it is at the winter solstice.

The abundance of summer: Summer in a temperate place like Ontario is a period of extraordinary biological productivity. The combination of long days, warmth, and ample moisture drives an explosion of plant growth. Insects multiply explosively (a single aphid can produce thousands of offspring in a single summer season). Birds race to raise their young while food is abundant. The entire living world is maximizing growth, reproduction, and energy capture before the shortening days signal another fall. Summer is biology running at full speed.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Describe in detail the characteristics of summer in Ontario
  • Identify seasonal changes in plants during summer
  • Identify seasonal changes in animals during summer
  • Describe summer weather patterns and how they affect daily life
  • Connect summer changes to the concept of adaptation
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Best Summer Activities

Take your child outside to directly experience summer if you are studying it in the right season, or use photographs and research if the season has passed. Direct sensory experience of the season is invaluable — what it looks, sounds, smells, and feels like cannot be captured in words alone.

Living Things Interview

Imagine you can interview a tree, a robin, a bear, and a honey bee about what they are doing in summer. What would each one say? This imaginative exercise develops deep understanding of how different living things respond to the same season in very different ways.

📖

The Big Lesson

Summer in Ontario

Summer (June, July, August) is Ontario's warmest season — defined by long days, high temperatures, and biological abundance. Average temperatures in southern Ontario range from 20°C to 30°C, with occasional heat waves bringing temperatures above 35°C.

The Long Days of Summer

Summer's defining characteristic is its long days. With 15+ hours of sunlight, plants have maximum time to photosynthesize. Temperatures are high enough for rapid growth. This combination produces an explosion of plant biomass — forests reach their maximum leaf area, fields fill with crops, and gardens overflow.

Ontario's Summer Wildlife

Summer is the season of maximum wildlife activity. Birds are nesting, raising young, and feeding constantly. Insects — the foundation of most food chains — are at their most abundant and diverse: dragonflies patrol ponds, fireflies signal from meadows at dusk, butterflies move among flowers, and monarch butterflies produce summer generations that will eventually become the migrating generation in fall.

Mammals are raising young. White-tailed deer fawns, born in May and June, spend summer growing rapidly. Black bears feed almost continuously to build the fat reserves for winter. Raccoons, foxes, and skunks are teaching their young to find food.

Summer Weather Extremes

Summer also brings some of Ontario's most extreme weather. Thunderstorms are most common in summer — afternoon heating creates the instability needed for towering cumulonimbus storm clouds. These storms can produce lightning, hail, and tornadoes (especially in southwestern Ontario, which lies at the northern edge of Tornado Alley). Droughts can occur when weeks pass without significant rain.

The firefly mystery: Fireflies (also called lightning bugs) are beetles that produce light through a chemical reaction called bioluminescence — combining the chemical luciferin with oxygen, catalyzed by an enzyme called luciferase. The 'flash' is actually a mating signal: males flash in specific patterns, and females of the same species respond from the ground. Each species has its own flash code. Ontario has about 20 species of fireflies, though their numbers have been declining due to light pollution and habitat loss. Their population peaks in late June and July in Ontario.
🧪

Week 11

🌧️ Weather — What Is Happening in the Atmosphere?

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E3.6 – Identify and describe types of weather; explain how weather affects daily life
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

A hurricane is a rotating storm system that can span 1,000 kilometres and generate more energy in a single day than all the world's nuclear weapons combined. It begins as a disturbance over warm ocean water, where evaporating water rises, condensing into clouds, releasing heat, causing more air to rise, which pulls in more moist air, which creates a self-reinforcing system that spins faster and faster, fed by the warm ocean.

Now here is what is extraordinary: every drop of rain, every lightning bolt, every tornado, every winter storm — all of this weather is ultimately powered by just two things: the Sun (heating the atmosphere unevenly) and gravity (pulling water down from clouds). The incredible variety of weather we experience is just different versions of these two forces playing out across the atmosphere.

Weather prediction limits: The atmosphere is what scientists call a 'chaotic system' — small differences in starting conditions can lead to huge differences in outcomes. This is why weather forecasts become unreliable beyond about 10–14 days, no matter how powerful the computers used. The atmosphere is governed entirely by the laws of physics, yet it is fundamentally unpredictable past a certain time horizon. This is called the 'butterfly effect' — theoretically, a butterfly flapping its wings in Brazil could set off a chain of events leading to a tornado in Texas.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Identify common types of weather: sunny, cloudy, rainy, snowy, windy, foggy, stormy
  • Understand that weather is caused by the Sun heating the atmosphere unevenly
  • Describe how meteorologists observe and measure weather
  • Understand the basic instruments: thermometer, barometer, wind vane, rain gauge
  • Understand how weather affects human activities, clothing, and transportation
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Weather Station Setup

Set up a simple home weather station together. You can make or buy:

  • A thermometer (outside, in shade)
  • A rain gauge (a straight-sided cup with a ruler)
  • A simple wind sock or wind vane (a strip of cloth on a stick)
  • A barometer (a simple home version can be made with a jar and balloon)

Record readings every day at the same time. This becomes the basis for the weather diary assignment.

Cloud Identification

Spend time looking at clouds together. Teach the main cloud types: cumulus (fluffy, fair weather), stratus (flat, grey, overcast), cumulonimbus (towering, thunderstorm), cirrus (wispy, high, made of ice crystals). Ask: 'What do you think the weather will be like tomorrow, based on today's clouds?'

📖

The Big Lesson

What Is Weather?

Weather is the short-term state of the atmosphere — temperature, precipitation, wind, humidity, and cloud cover at a specific place and time. Weather changes from hour to hour and day to day.

What Causes Weather?

Weather is driven by the Sun's uneven heating of Earth's surface. Land heats up faster than water; dark surfaces heat up faster than light ones; tropical regions receive more direct sunlight than polar regions. These differences in temperature cause differences in air pressure, which drive wind — the movement of air from high-pressure regions to low-pressure regions. Wind carries moisture (evaporated water) from warmer to cooler areas, where it condenses to form clouds and precipitation.

Types of Weather

Clear/Sunny: High pressure, descending air, little moisture condensation. The Sun heats the ground directly.

Cloudy: Water vapour condenses around tiny particles in the air (dust, pollen, sea salt) to form water droplets — clouds. If enough droplets combine, they fall as rain. If temperatures are below 0°C, ice crystals form in clouds, growing into snowflakes as they fall.

Thunderstorms: Tall cumulonimbus clouds, often forming on hot summer afternoons, can produce lightning, thunder, heavy rain, and hail. Lightning is a massive electrical discharge — the thunder is the sound of air expanding explosively along the lightning bolt's path.

Wind: Air moving from high-pressure areas to low-pressure areas. Gentle breezes are pleasant; strong winds (in tornadoes and hurricanes) are among the most destructive forces in nature.

Fog: Essentially a cloud at ground level — water droplets suspended in air near the surface, reducing visibility.

Ontario weather extremes: Ontario has recorded temperatures as high as 42.2°C (Hearst, 1936) and as low as -58.3°C (Iroquois Falls, 1935). The province receives between 400 mm of precipitation per year in the northwest to 1,200 mm near the Great Lakes. Lake Erie is shallow enough to freeze most winters; Lake Superior almost never does. Twisters (tornadoes) strike southern Ontario an average of about 13 times per year.

Measuring Weather

Meteorologists use instruments to measure weather precisely: a thermometer measures temperature, a barometer measures air pressure (falling pressure usually means rain is coming), a hygrometer measures humidity (moisture in the air), an anemometer measures wind speed, and a rain gauge measures precipitation. Modern weather forecasting uses networks of thousands of these instruments, weather balloons, radar, and satellites.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Weather Prediction Practice

For 5 days, observe the sky each morning and make a prediction: What do you think the weather will be like this afternoon?

Record: morning observations (cloud type, wind, temperature), afternoon prediction, afternoon actual weather.

Calculate your prediction accuracy: how many times were you correct?

Professional meteorologists are correct about tomorrow's weather about 90% of the time, but only about 70% of the time for 3 days ahead. Why is it so hard to predict weather further in advance?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Cloud Identification Journal

Over the next 3 days, photograph or sketch the clouds you see. Identify each type. Note: What weather followed each cloud type? At the end, create a 'Cloud Guide' showing all types you observed, with descriptions and what weather they predicted.

Day 2: Weather Instruments Report

Research and write about 5 weather instruments: thermometer, barometer, anemometer, rain gauge, and weather radar. For each: draw the instrument, explain what it measures, describe how it works, and explain why that measurement is useful for weather forecasting.

Day 3: How Weather Affects Life

Interview a parent about how the weather affects their daily decisions. Then research 3 professions that especially depend on weather: farming, aviation, and outdoor construction. For each, write how weather affects their work and what happens when the weather is unexpected.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Home Weather Station Report

Record weather data every day for 5 days:

  1. Temperature (morning and afternoon)
  2. Cloud type and cover (0% to 100%)
  3. Precipitation (rain? snow? how much?)
  4. Wind direction and estimated speed (calm, light, moderate, strong, very strong)
  5. Your weather 'forecast' for the next day and whether it came true

Create a 5-day weather chart and graph your temperature readings. Write a conclusion: What patterns did you notice? What was most surprising? How accurate was your forecasting?

🏠

Homework Activities

  • El Niño and La Niña: Research these climate patterns caused by changes in Pacific Ocean temperatures. How do they affect weather in Ontario?
  • Storm chasers: Research tornado chasers — scientists and enthusiasts who drive toward tornadoes to study them. Why is this important? What do they learn?
  • Extreme weather event: Research one of the most extreme weather events in Ontario's history (e.g., the 1998 Ice Storm, Hurricane Hazel in 1954, the 2013 Toronto Flood). What caused it? How did people respond?
  • Climate vs. weather: What is the difference between weather (short-term) and climate (long-term)? How do scientists study past climates without weather records?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • The Butterfly Effect says small changes can have huge consequences in chaotic systems. Does this mean weather will never be predictable beyond 2 weeks? Or could more powerful computers eventually extend the prediction window?
  • Weather on other planets is unimaginably extreme: winds on Neptune reach 2,100 km/h, Saturn has a hexagonal polar storm the size of two Earths, and Jupiter's Great Red Spot is a hurricane that has been raging for at least 350 years. What do these extremes tell us about what drives weather?
  • Could climate engineering — deliberately modifying Earth's weather and climate — be safe and effective? Research geoengineering proposals and controversies.
  • Before modern meteorology, how did people predict the weather? Research ancient and traditional weather prediction methods. How accurate were they?
Week 12

📊 Weather Patterns — Science of the Atmosphere

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E1.1–E1.4 – Collect and analyze weather data; identify patterns
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

In 1950, the first computerized weather forecast was calculated. It took 24 hours of computing time — on the most powerful computer in existence — to produce a 24-hour weather forecast. The forecast was not very accurate.

Today, the most powerful weather supercomputers make 1 quadrillion calculations per second (that is 1,000,000,000,000,000 per second), processing data from thousands of satellites, weather stations, buoys, and weather balloons. Modern 5-day forecasts are now as accurate as 1-day forecasts were in 1980.

Why weather forecasting matters so much: Accurate weather forecasts save thousands of lives per year — by warning people of dangerous storms, enabling farmers to plant and harvest at the right time, allowing airports to route flights safely, and letting engineers schedule concrete pours (which can fail if temperatures drop unexpectedly). The economic value of accurate weather forecasting has been estimated at hundreds of billions of dollars per year worldwide.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Collect and organize weather data over time
  • Identify patterns in weather data (daily temperature cycles, seasonal trends)
  • Use graphs and charts to display weather data
  • Draw evidence-based conclusions from weather data
  • Understand the difference between weather (short-term) and climate (long-term average weather)
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Using Historical Weather Data

Visit Environment Canada's historical climate data website (climate.weather.gc.ca) with your child. Look up historical data for your nearest city. Compare: average high and low temperatures for each month, average precipitation, and record temperatures. This data becomes the basis for comparison with your own observations.

Pattern Recognition Skills

Work on recognizing patterns in data. Ask: 'What is the warmest month on average? The coldest? The wettest? Which month has the most variable temperature — the biggest difference between the warmest and coldest recorded?'

📖

The Big Lesson

Weather vs. Climate

It is important to understand the difference between weather and climate. Weather is what is happening in the atmosphere right now or over the next few days — it changes rapidly. Climate is the long-term average of weather conditions over decades — it changes very slowly.

A simple way to remember it: 'Climate is what you expect; weather is what you get.' Ontario's climate tells us to expect cold winters and warm summers. Today's weather tells us whether to wear a coat.

Daily Weather Patterns

Within a single day, temperature follows a predictable pattern: coolest just before sunrise (the atmosphere has been radiating heat all night without receiving sunlight), then warming through the morning as the Sun heats the surface, reaching its highest temperature in mid-afternoon (around 2–4pm, a few hours after the Sun's energy peaks at noon), then cooling again as the Sun sets.

Ontario's Climate Pattern

Ontario has a humid continental climate, with warm to hot summers and cold winters. There is no distinct dry season — precipitation is distributed fairly evenly throughout the year, though summer can bring intense but short thunderstorms, and winter brings persistent snow in some regions.

Lake effect snow: The Great Lakes have a dramatic effect on Ontario's winter weather. Cold air flowing across the relatively warm lake water picks up enormous amounts of moisture. When this moisture-laden air hits the colder land on the downwind shore, it dumps heavy snow in narrow bands. The 'snowbelts' downwind of Georgian Bay and Lake Huron in Ontario can receive 300–400 cm of snow per winter — some of the highest snowfall totals in eastern Canada. Barrie, Owen Sound, and Collingwood are famous for lake-effect snow.

Analyzing and Graphing Weather Data

Scientists use graphs and charts to identify patterns in data that are not obvious from raw numbers. A line graph of daily temperatures reveals the daily cycle. A bar graph of monthly precipitation reveals seasonal patterns. A scatter plot of temperature versus ice cream sales might reveal a correlation.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Weather Data Analysis Investigation

Use your 5-day weather diary (from Lesson E11) and historical data from Environment Canada for your city.

Compare: Was this week warmer or cooler than the historical average for this time of year? Was it wetter or drier? By how much?

Graph your 5 days of temperature data alongside the historical average line. What do you notice?

If you collected data like this for 30 years, you would be contributing to climate science. Long-term amateur data collection has contributed to our understanding of climate — research 'citizen science weather observation networks.'
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Long-Term Climate Data Analysis

Using historical data, create a climate chart for your city. Show average monthly temperatures (line graph) and average monthly precipitation (bar chart) on the same diagram. Label the seasons. Write 5 observations about what the data tells you about your city's climate.

Day 2: My Week vs. Historical Average

Compare your 5-day weather diary data to the historical averages. For each day: how different was the actual temperature from the average? Was it above average or below average? Create a comparison graph.

Day 3: Climate Change Data

Research: Has Ontario's average annual temperature changed over the past 100 years? Find data from Environment Canada or another reliable source. Create a graph of the change. Write a conclusion: What does the data show? How confident are scientists about this trend?

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Climate of the World Comparison

Research the climate of 5 different places around the world: one each in the tropics, the subtropics, a Mediterranean climate, a subarctic climate, and a polar region. For each place:

  1. Describe the climate in your own words
  2. Create a simple climate graph (monthly temperatures)
  3. Describe what life is like there
  4. Name 3 animals and 3 plants adapted to that climate
  5. Explain how the climate relates to the geography (distance from equator, altitude, proximity to ocean)

Present as a 'Climate Passport' booklet, one page per place.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Climate change data: Research the average global temperature over the past 150 years. How much has it changed? Research what scientists predict for the next 100 years under different scenarios.
  • Climate records: Research the hottest and coldest places on Earth, the wettest and driest places, the windiest place, and the foggiest place. What makes each place extreme?
  • Citizen weather science: Research 'CoCoRaHS' (Community Collaborative Rain, Hail and Snow Network) — a network of volunteers measuring precipitation across North America. How does citizen science like this contribute to real science?
  • Weather maps: Research how to read a weather map — isobars (pressure lines), fronts (warm and cold), and high/low pressure systems. Find a current weather map online and try to predict the weather for your city based on it.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Weather forecasting improved dramatically when satellites were launched in the 1960s. What would weather forecasting look like without satellites? How accurate could it be?
  • Some scientists are working on geoengineering — deliberately changing Earth's climate to counteract global warming. One idea is to spray tiny particles into the upper atmosphere to reflect sunlight. What are the risks of such an approach?
  • If Earth's climate warms by 2°C on average, what would change? What about 4°C? Research the 'tipping points' that could cause dramatic and rapid climate changes.
  • Weather modification (cloud seeding to cause rain) is already practiced in some countries. Should governments be allowed to modify weather? What if the rain 'taken' by one region was 'supposed' to fall on a neighboring region?
Week 13

🦋 Living Things and Seasons — Life's Response to the Annual Cycle

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E3.7 – Describe how living things respond to seasonal changes
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Every fall, a tiny bird called the ruby-throated hummingbird — weighing less than a nickel — leaves its summer territory in Ontario and flies non-stop across the Gulf of Mexico, 800 kilometres of open ocean, to wintering grounds in Central America. This tiny creature, with a heart beating 1,200 times per minute and wings beating 53 times per second, crosses an ocean it has never crossed before, guided by the position of the Sun, Earth's magnetic field, and an inherited map that exists in its genes.

Migration is one of biology's great mysteries: How does a young bird that has never made the journey know where to go? Experiments with migratory birds have shown that many navigate using the stars (they learn the night sky as nestlings and memorize the North Star's position), the Sun's position and angle, Earth's magnetic field, and even smells. This navigational capability is partly learned, partly innate — and we are still discovering how it works.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand that living things respond to seasonal changes in predictable ways
  • Describe migration, hibernation, and dormancy as responses to changing seasons
  • Explain how plants respond to seasonal triggers (photoperiodism)
  • Identify specific Ontario plants and animals and their seasonal behaviours
  • Understand the concept of phenology — the study of seasonal biological events
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

Phenology Journal

Phenology is the science of seasonal biological events. Start a simple phenology journal: record the dates of seasonal 'firsts' — the first robin sighted, the first dandelion blooming, the last snow, the first tulip, the first frog calling. These observations, made year after year, reveal how seasonal timing is changing in response to climate change.

Adaptation Research Project

Each day of the week, research one Ontario species and its seasonal adaptations. Ask: How does this animal or plant know what season it is? What triggers its seasonal behaviour? (For most organisms, the trigger is day length, not temperature — because day length is a more reliable indicator of the season than temperature, which varies year to year.)

📖

The Big Lesson

Seasonal Responses: How Living Things Know What Season It Is

Living things must respond to seasons because each season presents different challenges and opportunities. But how do they know what season it is? Three main cues:

Day length (photoperiod) is the most reliable seasonal cue — because it never varies year to year. Plants and animals have internal biological clocks (circadian rhythms) that can track day length precisely. A maple tree 'knows' when spring arrives partly because the days have reached a specific length.

Temperature is also a cue, but less reliable because of weather variability. Many spring events (bud burst, insect emergence) are triggered by cumulative heat above a threshold.

Internal biological clocks control many seasonal behaviours independently of external cues — animals in laboratories without natural light still show seasonal rhythms.

Migration

Migration is the seasonal movement of animals from one region to another, usually driven by food availability and breeding requirements. Ontario sits at the intersection of two major North American migratory flyways. In spring, billions of birds pass through Ontario heading north. In fall, they return south.

Migration is not only for birds: monarch butterflies migrate 4,500 km. Caribou migrate across the tundra. Even some insects (dragonflies) migrate. The energy cost of migration is enormous — but staying through the winter would be impossible for animals that depend on insects or nectar.

Hibernation

Hibernation is a state of deep dormancy in which an animal's metabolism drops to a minimum to conserve energy through winter. Body temperature, heart rate, and breathing all decrease dramatically. Black bears' heart rates drop from 50 beats per minute to about 8. Woodchucks (groundhogs) have body temperatures that drop to near freezing.

Not just sleeping: Hibernation is not just deep sleep — it is a completely different physiological state. During hibernation, animals periodically arouse for short periods (we don't fully understand why). During true hibernation, an animal's immune system is partially suppressed and some brain functions are suspended. Understanding hibernation has medical applications — it may lead to techniques for inducing torpor in humans for long space journeys.

Dormancy in Plants

Plants respond to shortening days and cooling temperatures by entering dormancy. Deciduous trees drop their leaves, close off their vascular system, and enter a state where their metabolism is minimal. Some perennial plants die back to their roots, which survive in the soil. Annual plants die entirely, leaving seeds to germinate the following spring.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Phenology Investigation

Research the average dates of seasonal 'firsts' in Ontario:

  • Average date of last frost (spring)
  • Average date of first robin sighting
  • Average date of first trillium bloom
  • Average date of first monarch butterfly sighting
  • Average date of fall colour peak

Compare these to current research: are these dates changing over time? By how much and in what direction?

Scientists have found that spring phenological events (when birds return, when flowers bloom, when insects emerge) are happening 1–2 weeks earlier in many parts of North America compared to 50 years ago. What is causing this? What are the ecological consequences if different species shift their timing at different rates?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Ontario Species Seasonal Calendar

Create a detailed seasonal calendar for 10 Ontario species (your choice of plants and animals). Show what each species is doing in each month, triggered by what environmental cue. Use colour coding and illustrations.

Day 2: Migration Map

Choose one migratory Ontario bird (e.g., the rose-breasted grosbeak, the blackpoll warbler, or the ruby-throated hummingbird). Research its complete migratory journey: where it winters, its migration route, when it arrives in Ontario, where it breeds, and when it leaves. Draw a detailed map and write a narrative of the bird's year.

Day 3: Hibernation Science Report

Choose one hibernating Ontario animal. Research deeply: when it enters hibernation, what physiological changes occur, what it does during hibernation, when it emerges, and what the scientific importance of hibernation research is. Write a full scientific report with diagrams.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Living Things and Seasons Research Portfolio

Create a portfolio of 5 detailed research profiles, one per day:

  1. A migratory bird: full lifecycle and migration story
  2. A hibernating mammal: hibernation physiology and ecology
  3. A dormant tree species: the year in the life of one Ontario tree
  4. A spring ephemeral plant: its brief, brilliant life strategy
  5. A winter-active animal: how it survives Ontario's coldest months

Each profile should be at least 1 page with illustrations, labels, and biological details.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • Monarch butterfly crisis: Monarch butterfly populations have declined by 90% in recent decades. Research why and what is being done to help them.
  • Wood frog revisited: The wood frog of Ontario survives winter by freezing solid. Research the biochemistry of how it does this without killing its cells. What medical applications might this have?
  • Phenology citizen science: Research 'Project BudBurst' or 'eBird' — citizen science projects that track seasonal events. How do you participate? Why is this data valuable?
  • Climate change and migration: Research how climate change is affecting the timing and routes of bird migration in North America. Which species are most threatened?
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Some scientists are concerned that climate change is causing 'phenological mismatches' — when a bird returns to breed in spring but the peak of caterpillar abundance (its food) now happens 2 weeks earlier. How serious is this problem for ecosystems?
  • Could humans develop 'hibernation drugs' that allow people to safely enter a hibernate-like state for medical purposes or long space travel? What would need to be solved?
  • Some animal populations (like lemmings and snowshoe hares) go through regular cycles of boom and bust population every few years. What drives these cycles and how are they connected to seasonal patterns?
  • If you could add one animal to Ontario's ecosystem that currently doesn't live here, what would you add and why? What seasonal challenges would it face?
Week 14

🌍 Our Impact on Earth — Stewardship of Our Planet

Ontario 2022 Curriculum — E2.1–E2.2 – Describe human impacts on Earth's systems; identify stewardship actions
🔭

Wonder Hook — Start Here!

Geologists have proposed naming a new geological epoch: the Anthropocene — the age of humans. This would be the first geological epoch named after a single species. The evidence for its beginning (variously proposed as 1950, 1945, or the Industrial Revolution) includes: a global layer of plastic microparticles in ocean sediment, radioactive isotopes from nuclear bomb tests visible in rock strata everywhere on Earth, a dramatic increase in CO₂ in the atmosphere visible in ice cores, and the extinction of species at 1,000 times the normal background rate.

For the first time in Earth's 4.5-billion-year history, one species has become a geological force. This is an extraordinary achievement and an extraordinary responsibility.

A hopeful fact: In 1987, the world agreed to stop using chemicals that were destroying the ozone layer (the CFCs in old aerosol cans and refrigerators). The ozone layer is now recovering — it is expected to return to pre-1980 levels by 2065. This is one of the greatest environmental success stories in history. Humanity can solve big environmental problems when we choose to. The question is whether we will choose to, and how quickly.
🎯

Learning Goals

  • Understand that human activities affect Earth's systems: atmosphere, water, land, and living things
  • Identify specific human impacts: climate change, pollution, deforestation, habitat loss, species extinction
  • Understand the concept of environmental stewardship and sustainable living
  • Identify actions individuals, communities, and governments can take
  • Develop a sense of personal responsibility for Earth's health
👩‍🏫

Parent Teaching Guide

The Big Picture Conversation

This lesson deals with complex and sometimes distressing information about the environment. Frame it positively: 'Humans have caused these problems, and humans are working to solve them. The most important thing is that you understand what is happening and can participate in the solutions.'

Emphasize: individuals matter, but systemic change (in government, industry, and infrastructure) matters more. It is not fair to put all the responsibility on individuals — large-scale change requires large-scale action. But individuals can both make personal choices AND advocate for systemic change.

Stewardship Action Planning

Work with your child to identify 3 stewardship actions your family will actually take — not just discuss. These should be specific, measurable, and committed to. Write them down. Put them on the fridge. Follow up in a month.

📖

The Big Lesson

Human Impacts on Earth

For most of human history, our impact on Earth's systems was local and limited. But in the past 200 years — powered by fossil fuels and driven by rapid population growth — human activity has become a global force affecting Earth's atmosphere, oceans, land, and biodiversity.

Climate Change

The burning of fossil fuels (coal, oil, and natural gas) releases carbon dioxide (CO₂) into the atmosphere. CO₂ is a greenhouse gas — it traps heat in the atmosphere. Since the Industrial Revolution, atmospheric CO₂ has risen from about 280 parts per million to over 420 parts per million — the highest level in at least 3 million years. This enhanced greenhouse effect is raising global average temperatures, causing changes in precipitation patterns, melting ice caps, raising sea levels, and making extreme weather events more frequent and intense.

Pollution

Human activities release pollutants into the air, water, and soil. Air pollution from burning fuels and industry causes respiratory diseases and affects global weather patterns. Water pollution from agricultural runoff, industrial waste, and plastic contamination affects aquatic ecosystems and human health. Soil pollution from pesticides, heavy metals, and waste affects the organisms that make soil fertile.

Habitat Loss and Biodiversity

Humanity has cleared about half of Earth's forests and converted about one-third of the world's land surface to agriculture. This habitat destruction, combined with pollution, hunting, and climate change, has driven species extinction at about 1,000 times the natural background rate. Scientists estimate that 1 million species are currently threatened with extinction — the sixth mass extinction in Earth's history, and the first caused by a single species.

The good news: Despite these challenges, there are genuine success stories. Bald eagle and whooping crane populations have recovered from near-extinction in North America. Wolves have been reintroduced to Yellowstone, recovering the ecosystem. Air quality in most Western cities is dramatically better than it was in the 1970s. Renewable energy is growing faster than any technology in history. Young people around the world are driving demand for climate action. The problems are real and urgent — but so is the progress.

Environmental Stewardship

Environmental stewardship means caring for the natural world — protecting ecosystems, using resources responsibly, reducing pollution, and leaving Earth in better condition for future generations. Stewardship happens at every level: individual (choices about consumption, diet, transportation), community (local conservation projects, urban greening, composting programs), and governmental (environmental laws, international agreements, carbon pricing).

Ontario's Conservation Efforts

Ontario has taken important steps to protect its natural heritage: the Greenbelt protects 810,000 hectares around the Greater Toronto Area from development; the Endangered Species Act protects threatened species; Ontario is home to 6 UNESCO Biosphere Reserves. Niagara Escarpment, the Oak Ridges Moraine, and the vast boreal forest of northern Ontario are protected or partially protected landscapes.

🧪

Think Like a Scientist

Personal Carbon Footprint Reflection

With a parent, estimate your family's approximate carbon footprint in 3 areas:

  • Transportation: How do you usually travel? (Car: estimate km per week; public transit; walking/cycling)
  • Home energy: How is your home heated? (Natural gas? Electric? What is your monthly energy use?)
  • Diet: How much meat does your family eat? (Beef production creates about 10 times the carbon emissions of chicken, and about 50 times that of vegetables)

Research approximate carbon footprints for each activity. Which of the three categories has the most impact for your family?

A report by researchers at Lund University found that the four actions with the highest individual climate impact are: have one fewer child, live car-free, avoid one transatlantic flight, and eat a plant-based diet. Why might these four actions have so much more impact than things like recycling or using LED bulbs?
✏️

Classwork — 2 to 3 Days

Day 1: Impact and Solution Web

Create a large 'Impact and Solution Web' diagram. In the centre: 'Human Impact on Earth.' Branch out to 4 major categories of impact: climate change, pollution, habitat loss, resource depletion. For each, add: what causes it, what it affects, and at least 2 specific solutions at different scales (individual, community, government).

Day 2: Environmental Good News Report

Research 5 environmental success stories from around the world. For each: what was the problem? What was done? What are the results? How long did it take? Write a 'Good News in the Environment' newsletter.

Day 3: My Stewardship Plan

Create a personal stewardship plan for the next year. Include: 5 specific actions you will take (with dates and measurements), one thing you will learn more about, one community action you will participate in, and a statement of why you care about the natural world. Make it personal and honest.

📋

Assignment — 1 Week Project

One Week: Environmental Documentary Project

Create a short 'environmental documentary' — either as a written report with illustrations, or as actual video if technology is available. Your documentary must cover:

  1. The environmental issue you chose (be specific — not 'climate change' but 'sea level rise in coastal Ontario communities')
  2. The science behind the issue
  3. Who is affected and how
  4. What is already being done
  5. What more needs to be done
  6. Your personal call to action

Present your documentary to your family. Be persuasive, be factual, and be hopeful.

🏠

Homework Activities

  • The Paris Agreement: Research the 2015 Paris Agreement — what is it, what did countries agree to, and are they meeting their commitments?
  • Youth climate activism: Research the global youth climate movement. Who are the key figures? What actions have they taken? What has been accomplished?
  • Indigenous environmental stewardship: Research how Indigenous land stewardship practices in Canada maintain biodiversity and ecosystem health. What can Western conservation learn from traditional ecological knowledge?
  • Rewilding: Research the concept of 'rewilding' — reintroducing species and allowing natural processes to restore ecosystems. Find one successful rewilding project and describe it.
🌟

Keep Wondering…

  • Some scientists argue that we are in the middle of the sixth mass extinction event. What caused the previous five? Are any of the causes similar to what humans are doing now?
  • Could we terraform Mars — make it habitable for humans and other Earth life? What would this take? And is it ethical to change another planet's environment?
  • Technology has caused many of our environmental problems, but technology is also one of our best hopes for solving them. What technologies are you most hopeful about? What are their risks?
  • Some philosophers argue that nature has rights — not just humans and animals, but rivers, forests, and mountains have legal rights of their own. Several countries have started to adopt this idea. What do you think of it?
Reference · All Year

📚 Vocabulary Dictionary

All key science words for Grade 1
📖

Science Word Bank

Learn these important science words. Read each word and its meaning. Can you use the word in your own sentence?

Rotation
Earth spinning on its axis once every 24 hours — the cause of day and night.
Revolution
Earth orbiting (traveling around) the Sun once every 365.25 days — the cause of the year.
Axis
The imaginary line through Earth's centre, from the North Pole to the South Pole, around which Earth rotates.
Orbit
The path that one body takes around another in space. Earth orbits the Sun; the Moon orbits Earth.
Axial Tilt
Earth's axis is tilted 23.5° from vertical relative to its orbit. This tilt is the cause of the seasons.
Solstice
The two days of the year when the Sun is at its highest or lowest noon position: the summer solstice (longest day) around June 21 and the winter solstice (shortest day) around December 21 in the Northern Hemisphere.
Equinox
The two days each year (around March 21 and September 21) when day and night are approximately equal in length everywhere on Earth.
Phase
The changing appearance of the Moon as seen from Earth, caused by our changing view of the sunlit side during the Moon's orbit.
Constellation
A named pattern of stars as seen from Earth. 88 official constellations divide the entire sky.
Season
One of four periods of the year (spring, summer, fall/autumn, winter) characterized by specific weather patterns and day lengths, caused by Earth's axial tilt during its orbit.
Weather
The short-term state of the atmosphere at a specific place and time: temperature, precipitation, wind, clouds.
Climate
The long-term average of weather conditions in a region over decades or centuries. Climate changes very slowly; weather changes daily.
Precipitation
Water falling from the atmosphere to Earth's surface in any form: rain, snow, sleet, hail.
Migration
The seasonal movement of animals from one region to another, typically driven by food availability and breeding requirements.
Hibernation
A state of deep dormancy in which an animal's metabolism slows dramatically to conserve energy through winter.
Dormancy
A state of reduced metabolic activity in plants (similar to hibernation) that allows them to survive unfavourable conditions.
Phenology
The study of seasonal biological events and their timing: when birds return, when flowers bloom, when insects emerge.
Greenhouse Effect
The natural process by which certain gases in the atmosphere (including CO₂ and water vapour) trap heat, keeping Earth warm. Human activities are intensifying this effect.
Stewardship
Responsible care for something in one's charge. Environmental stewardship means protecting and sustainably managing natural resources.
Biodiversity
The variety of all living things in an area or on Earth. High biodiversity makes ecosystems more resilient and productive.
